Beyond Parallel Pancakes: Quasi-Polynomial Time Guarantees for Non-Spherical Gaussian Mixtures Thanks: This project has received funding from the European Research Council (ERC) under the European Union’s Horizon 2020 research and innovation programme (grant agreement No 815464).
Rares-Darius Buhai, David Steurer

Abstract
We consider mixtures of k ​ \geqslant ​ 2 k\geqslant 2 Gaussian components with unknown means and unknown covariance (identical for all components) that are well-separated, i.e., distinct components have statistical overlap at most k − C k^{-C} for a large enough constant C ​ \geqslant ​ 1 C\geqslant 1 . Previous statistical-query [ DKS17 ] and lattice-based [ BRST21 , GVV22 ] lower bounds give formal evidence that, even for the special case of colinear means, distinguishing such mixtures from (pure) Gaussians may be exponentially hard (in k k ). We show that, surprisingly, this kind of hardness can only appear if mixing weights are allowed to be exponentially small. For polynomially lower bounded mixing weights, we show how to achieve non-trivial statistical guarantees in quasi-polynomial time. Concretely, we develop an algorithm based on the sum-of-squares method with running time quasi-polynomial in the minimum mixing weight. The algorithm can reliably distinguish between a mixture of k ​ \geqslant ​ 2 k\geqslant 2 well-separated Gaussian components and a (pure) Gaussian distribution. As a certificate, the algorithm computes a bipartition of the input sample that separates some pairs of mixture components, i.e., both sides of the bipartition contain most of the sample points of at least one component. For the special case of colinear means, our algorithm outputs a k k -clustering of the input sample that is approximately consistent with all components of the underlying mixture. We obtain similar clustering guarantees also for the case that the overlap between any two mixture components is lower bounded quasi-polynomially in k k (in addition to being upper bounded polynomially in k k ). A significant challenge for our results is that they appear to be inherently sensitive to small fractions of adversarial outliers unlike most previous algorithmic results for Gaussian mixtures. The reason is that such outliers can simulate exponentially small mixing weights even for mixtures with polynomially lower bounded mixing weights. A key technical ingredient of our algorithms is a characterization of separating directions for well-separated Gaussian components in terms of ratios of polynomials that correspond to moments of two carefully chosen orders logarithmic in the minimum mixing weight.



1
Introduction

Gaussian mixture models (GMMs) are among the most extensively studied statistical models in a wide range of scientific disciplines
[
Pea94
,
Das99
,
ABH+20
]
.
Over the course of the last two decades, a major body of research explored what kinds of algorithmic guarantees are feasible for GMMs
[
Das99
,
VW02
,
KMV10
,
MV10
,
HK13
]
.

Recent years have seen significant algorithmic advances along two dimensions.

The first kinds of advances concern mixtures of a large number of spherical Gaussians, i.e., Gaussians with identity
I_{d}
as covariance.
1
1

1

Many known algorithms for mixtures of Gaussians with covariance
I_{d}
also extend to somewhat more general settings, e.g., the case that the covariances are different multiples of
I_{d}
or diagonal matrices (axis-aligned case) or that case that the covariance is upper bounded in the Loewner order by
I_{d}
.
For our discussion, we focus on the simplest case (all covariances identity) because, to the best of our knowledge, these kinds of generalizations are orthogonal to the kind of generalization we aim for in this work.

Several works showed how to cluster such mixtures in time quasi-polynomial in the number
k
of components under a minimum mean-separation requirement of
O(\sqrt{\log k})
, which up to a constant factor matches the minimum separation that guarantees clusterability of the mixture
[
HL18
,
DKS18
,
KSS18
]
.
In a recent breakthrough, the running time has been improved to polynomial assuming a slightly larger minimum separation of
O(\log^{1/2+c}k)
for any
c>0

[
LL22
]
.
Even without any separation requirement, it is possible to compute quasi-polynomially sized covers of the set of means
[
DK20
]
.

The second kinds of advances concern mixtures of a small number of Gaussian components with unknown covariances.
These advances extended previous algorithmic guarantees to the robust setting, i.e., in the presence of a small constant fraction of adversarially chosen outliers.
Concretely, it is now possible to estimate the parameters of an arbitrary mixture of Gaussian components in the presence of such outliers
[
BDH+20
,
LM21
,
BDJ+20
]
.
The running time is polynomial in the ambient dimension but (at least) exponential in the number of components.

One of the most outstanding challenges remaining in this area is to clarify what kinds of algorithmic guarantees are possible when the number of components is large and their covariances are unknown. So far, mixtures of a large number of Gaussian components with unknown covariances have defied comparable algorithmic progress.
2
2

2

A notable exception is a particular smoothed model for such mixtures when the ambient dimension is large enough
[
GHK15
]
.

Indeed, there is formal evidence, in the form of statistical-query
[
DKS17
]
and lattice-based
[
BRST21
,
GVV22
]
lower bounds, to suggest that this setting is computationally inherently harder than the spherical setting.
Specifically, these results suggest that even for
k
components with tiny statistical overlaps, say at most
2^{-k}
, approximately clustering the components may require time exponential in
k
despite the sample complexity being polynomial in
d
and
k
.
Underlying this evidence is the well-known parallel pancakes construction:
Orthogonal to a randomly chosen direction
u
, all components of this mixture distribution agree with a (pure) standard Gaussian distribution, and along direction
u
, the components are well-separated but their mixture matches the first
k
moments of a (univariate) standard Gaussian distribution.
3
3

3

As a consequence of this construction, all means are colinear with
u
.
We also emphasize that these means are well-separated relative to the variance of each component in direction
u
.
However, since this variance is very small (about
k^{-O(1)}
), the standard Euclidean distance between the components is tiny.
We remark that parallel pancakes constructions have been discussed in the literature already before
[
DKS17
]
.
The influential work
[
BV08
]
provided an efficient algorithm for the case
k=2
.

In this work, we show that, surprisingly, this kind of hardness can appear only in the case that mixing weights are allowed to be exponentially small.
Indeed, we develop algorithms with substantial statistical guarantees that run in quasi-polynomial time whenever the mixing weights are bounded from below by a polynomial.
Before our work, the best known running times to achieve these kinds of guarantees were (at least) exponential in
k
.
We hope that our work opens up a new direction of research on efficient algorithms for mixtures of well-separated Gaussians with polynomially lower bounded mixing weights.

Within this new direction of research, we identify the following appealing open question:

Consider a mixture of
k\geqslant 2
Gaussian components with unknown means
\mu_{1},\ldots,\mu_{k}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and unknown covariance
\Sigma\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
(identical for all components)

4
4

4

The hard instances in all lower bounds cited above are mixtures with identical covariances. A natural motivation to consider identical covariances is affine invariance. Algorithms for the spherical case assume that the input data is presented in a favorable affine transformation. However, a natural property desired for algorithms operating on geometric data is to be invariant under affine transformations
[
BV08
]
.

and with minimum mixing weight
p_{\mathrm{min}}>0
.
Suppose the components are well-separated in the sense that any two distinct components have statistical overlap at most
p_{\mathrm{min}}^{C}
for a large enough constant
C\geqslant 1
.

Given a sample of size
n\geqslant d^{O(\log(1/p_{\mathrm{min}}))}
, can we compute in time polynomial in
n
a
k
-clustering of the sample that is consistent with the mixture components on all but at most a
p_{\mathrm{min}}^{10}
fraction of the sample?

We conjecture that such an algorithm does exist.
Indeed, we confirm the conjecture for the special case that the means are colinear (
Theorem

1.2
) and under a diameter bound (
Theorem

1.3
).
In the general case, our algorithm provides a somewhat weaker guarantee and computes only a bipartition of the sample that separates at least one pair of mixture components (
Theorem

1.1
).
5
5

5

The algorithm for the general case also computes such a bipartition under the weaker assumption that
there exists
a pair of mixture components that has small overlap (as opposed to all pairs having small overlap). Under this assumption full clustering is impossible and a partial clustering seems the appropriate guarantee to aim for.

We identify an interesting challenge in the context of establishing the above conjecture that our techniques can partially overcome:
Any hypothetical algorithm establishing the above conjecture or our (non-hypothetical) algorithms inherently cannot be robust to even a tiny fraction of outliers (assuming the hardness of the parallel-pancakes constructions in
[
DKS17
,
BRST21
,
GVV22
]
).
The reason is that a tiny
1/k^{100}
fraction of outliers are enough to simulate these hard instances by adding components with appropriately decaying mixing weights and spaced means.
At the same time, many recent algorithmic approaches in the context of GMMs are inherently tied to robustness.
For example, certain kinds of identifiability proofs used in the analysis of sum-of-squares based algorithms automatically imply robust algorithms.
Also many kinds of iteration schemes inherently require robustness for their subroutines in order to guarantee that the next iteration can successfully deal with the errors introduced by previous iterations.

1.1
Results

Separating bipartition

Suppose we are given a quasi-polynomial size sample of a mixture of
k
Gaussian components with unknown means
\mu_{1},\ldots,\mu_{k}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and unknown covariance
\Sigma\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
and with minimum mixing weight at least
1/k^{100}
such that there exists a pair of mixture components
a\neq b
with
\lVert\Sigma^{-1/2}(\mu_{a}-\mu_{b})\rVert\gg\sqrt{\log k}
.
Then, as
Theorem

1.1
shows, it is possible to compute in quasi-polynomial time a bipartition of the samples such that, for each side of the bipartition, there exists a component with
0.99
of its samples assigned to it.

Theorem 1.1
.

Given a sample of size
n\geqslant(kd)^{O(\log k)}
from a mixture of
k
Gaussian components
N(\mu_{1},\Sigma),\ldots,N(\mu_{k},\Sigma)
with minimum mixing weight at least
1/k^{100}
such that
\max_{a\neq b}\lVert\Sigma^{-1/2}(\mu_{a}-\mu_{b})\rVert\gg\sqrt{\log k}
,
there exists an algorithm that runs in time
n\cdot d^{O(\log k)}
and returns with probability
0.99
a partition of
[n]
into two sets
C_{1}
and
C_{2}
such that, if the true clustering of the samples is
S_{1},...,S_{k}
, then

\max_{i}\frac{|C_{1}\cap S_{i}|}{|S_{i}|}\geqslant 0.99\quad\text{and}\quad\max_{i}\frac{|C_{2}\cap S_{i}|}{|S_{i}|}\geqslant 0.99.

For general mixing weights, the same result holds with
k
replaced by
1/p_{\mathrm{min}}
in all guarantees. See
Theorem

4.2
for the full result.

Colinear means

Suppose, in addition, that the mixture of Guassians is well-separated, i.e., the minimum mean separation satisfies
\min_{a\neq b}\lVert\Sigma^{-1/2}(\mu_{a}-\mu_{b})\rVert\gg\sqrt{\log k}
, and that the unknown means
\mu_{1},...,\mu_{k}
are colinear.
Given a quasi-polynomial number of samples from the mixture,
Theorem

1.2
shows that it is possible to compute in quasi-polynomial time a partition of the samples into
k
clusters such that the fraction of samples assigned to incorrect clusters is polynomially small in
k
.

For simplicity, in the theorem statement below we assume that all eigenvalues of
\Sigma
and all eigenvalues of the covariance matrix of the mixture are polynomially lower and upper bounded in
k
and
d
.

Theorem 1.2
.

Given a sample of size
n\geqslant(kd)^{O(\log k)}
from a mixture of
k
Gaussian components
N(\mu_{1},\Sigma),\ldots,N(\mu_{k},\Sigma)
with minimum mixing weight at least
1/k^{100}
such that
\min_{a\neq b}\lVert\Sigma^{-1/2}(\mu_{a}-\mu_{b})\rVert\gg\sqrt{\log k}
and
\mu_{1},...,\mu_{k}
colinear,
there exists an algorithm that runs in time
n^{O(\log k)}
and returns with high probability a partition of
[n]
into
k
sets
C_{1},...,C_{k}
such that, if the true clustering of the samples is
S_{1},...,S_{k}
, then there exists a permutation
\pi
of
[k]
such that

1-\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{k}|C_{i}\cap S_{\pi(i)}|\leqslant k^{-O(1)}.

For general mixing weights, the same result holds with
k
replaced by
1/p_{\mathrm{min}}
in all guarantees. See
Theorem

6.1
for the full result.

Given such a clustering, we can also recover the means and the covariance of the components using robust Gaussian estimation algorithms
[
DKK+19
]
or robust moment estimation algorithms
[
KSS18
]
.
For example, via
[
KSS18
]
, we obtain a multiplicative approximation to the covariance
(1-k^{-O(1)})\Sigma\preceq\hat{\Sigma}\preceq(1+k^{-O(1)})\Sigma
and a "covariance-aware" approximation to the means
\|\Sigma^{-1/2}(\hat{\mu}_{i}-\mu_{i})\|\leqslant k^{-O(1)}
.

Small radius

If instead of colinear means we have bounded means
\lVert\Sigma^{-1/2}\mu_{i}\rVert\leqslant R
with
{R=\operatorname{polylog}(k)}
,
Theorem

1.3
shows that it is again possible to cluster the samples with a quasi-polynomial number of samples and quasi-polynomial time.

Theorem 1.3
.

Given a sample of size
n\geqslant(kd)^{O(R^{2}+\log k)}
from a mixture of
k
Gaussian components
N(\mu_{1},\Sigma),\ldots,N(\mu_{k},\Sigma)
with minimum mixing weight at least
1/k^{100}
such that
\min_{a\neq b}\lVert\Sigma^{-1/2}(\mu_{a}-\mu_{b})\rVert\gg\sqrt{\log k}
and
\lVert\Sigma^{-1/2}\mu_{i}\rVert\leqslant R
,
there exists an algorithm that runs in time
n^{O(R^{2}+\log k)}
and returns with high probability a partition of
[n]
into
k
sets
C_{1},...,C_{k}
such that, if the true clustering of the samples is
S_{1},...,S_{k}
, then there exists a permutation
\pi
of
[k]
such that

1-\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{k}|C_{i}\cap S_{\pi(i)}|\leqslant k^{-O(1)}.

For general mixing weights, the same result holds with
k
replaced by
1/p_{\mathrm{min}}
in all guarantees. See
Theorem

7.1
for the full result.

As in the case of colinear means, given such a a clustering, we can recover the means and the covariance of the components.

Unlike our results for separating bipartitions and colinear means, this result follows from a direct reduction to a previous algorithm for spherical components
[
HL18
]
.
Concretely, we observe that this algorithm requires only a rough multiplicative approximation (in the SOS sense) of a polynomial of the form
q(v)=\lVert\Sigma^{1/2}v\rVert^{t}
for some
t
polylogarithmic in
k
.
As we show, the empirical moment tensor of the mixture readily provides such an approximation.

1.2
Related works

Comparision to recent algorithms based on lattice basis reduction

Two independent works (also independent and concurrent with a preprint of our work) obtain polynomial-time algorithms for learning parallel-pancakes mixtures for the case that the component variance is zero along the hidden direction (infinitesimally flat pancakes)
6
6

6

These works also crucially assume a mild bound on the bit complexity of the unknown means

[
ZSWB22
,
DK22
]
.
These algorithms are based on the LLL lattice basis reduction algorithm
[
LLL82
]
and have a completely different flavor than our algorithms and previous algorithms for Gaussian mixture models.
However, these lattice basis reduction techniques are expected to be brittle and limited to the case that the variance in the hidden direction is tiny.

Comparision to previous algorithms for mixtures with few components and unknown covariances

Like our algorithms, many recent algorithms for learning GMMs make use of the sum-of-squares semidefinite programming hierarchy.
While these algorithms and analyses have not been designed for our setting, we find it still instructive to discuss the differences and similarities to our algorithms.

Many of these algorithms also have in common that they employ the proof-to-algorithm paradigm, which has become the predominant way to analyze algorithms based on sum-of-squares for statistical estimation problems.
(For expositions of this paradigm, see
[
BS14
,
RSS18
,
FKP19
]
.)
This paradigm allows us to derive efficient estimation algorithms in a black-box way from identifiability proofs formalized in the sum-of-squares proof system.

As mentioned earlier, several recent algorithms consider mixtures of few well-separated Gaussian components with unknown covariances in the presence of adversarial outliers

[
BDH+20
,
BK20
,
DHKK20
]
.
While these algorithms have running times (at least) exponential in the number of components, their separation requirements are also exponentially stronger than ours.
Even in the case that all covariances are the same (
\Sigma
) and the well-separatedness stems purely from the means
\mu_{1},\ldots,\mu_{k}
, their identifiability proof requires separation
\lVert\Sigma^{-1/2}(\mu_{a}-\mu_{b})\rVert^{2}\geqslant k^{O(1)}
(e.g.,
[
BK20
, Lemma 4.16]
).
In constrast, our separation condition is logarithmic in
k
, which is the weakest separation condition, up to constant factors, that guarantees clusterability.

In order to deal with the kind of mild separation considered in this work, one could use one of the (robust) algorithms for parameter learning or density estimation of general
k
-component GMMs
[
MV10
,
BS10
,
BDJ+20
,
LM21
]
.
While some of these works use separation between components in order to compute a rough partial clustering of far-away components as a pre-processing step, there doesn’t appear to be a way to further exploit milder kinds of separation.
For example,
[
MV10
]
learns the means of the mixture up to small error after projecting along a randomly chosen direction.
This kind of projection cannot be expected to preserve any kind of separation of the high-dimensional mixture and even the sample complexity for recovering the means of this 1-dimensional mixture may be exponential in
k
(as shown in
[
MV10
]
).
Both of the more recent works
[
BDJ+20
,
LM21
]
end up enumerating subspaces related to the unknown parameters of the mixture.
To the best of our knowledge, their approaches cannot avoid this step even for the kind of mildly-separated mixtures with lower bounded mixing weights considered in our work.

2
Techniques

We consider uniform
7
7

7

In this section we restrict ourselves for ease of explanation to uniform mixtures. Our technical sections state all results for non-uniform mixtures.
mixtures of
k\geqslant 2
well-separated Gaussian components
N(\mu_{1},\Sigma),\ldots,N(\mu_{k},\Sigma)
with unknown means
\mu_{1},\ldots,\mu_{k}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and unknown covariance
\Sigma\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
(identical for all components).
Here, we say components are
well-separated
8
8

8

The term ”clusterable mixture” is sometimes used in the literature to refer to mixtures with well-separated components.

if the maximum affinity
9
9

9

The affinity of two probability measures is defined to be
1
minus their statistical distance
[
Pol02
]
.
(also called overlap) between two distinct components is bounded by
1/k^{C}
for a large enough constant
C\geqslant 1
.
For Gaussian components, this notion of well-separatedness means

\min_{a\neq b}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lVert\Sigma^{-1/2}(\mu_{a}-\mu_{b})}}\right\rVert\gg\sqrt{\log k\,}\,.

(2.1)

Distinguishing well-separated mixtures from (pure) Gaussians

Our algorithms are informed by investigating the parallel pancakes construction underlying Statistical Query lower bounds for such mixtures.
This construction provides a mixture of
k
well-separated Gaussian components that appears to be exponentially hard to distinguish from the standard Gaussian distribution
N(0,I_{d})
.
In particular, this mixture matches the first
\Omega(k)
moments of
N(0,1)
.

The starting point of our algorithms is the following observation:
In order for a mixture with
k\geqslant 2
well-separated Gaussian components to match the first
t
moments of
N(0,I_{d})
, the minimum mixing weight is necessarily smaller than
2^{-\Omega(t)}
.
In particular, if the mixture has uniform mixing weights
\frac{1}{k}
, then always one of its first
O(\log k)
moments distinguishes it from a standard Gaussian.

Underlying this observation is the following simple fact:

A distribution uniform over
k
real values can match no more than
O(\log k)
moments of
N(0,1)
.

To verify this fact, let
\bm{A}
be a random variable uniformly distributed over
k
(not necessarily distinct) real values.
Then, for all even integers
s\leqslant t
, the ratio of the normalized order-
s
and order-
t
moments of
\bm{A}
is sandwiched in the following way,

k^{-1/s}\leqslant\frac{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\E\bm{A}^{s}}}\right)^{1/s}}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\E\bm{A}^{t}}}\right)^{1/t}}\leqslant 1\,.

(2.2)

(This ratio is maximized if
\bm{A}
is constant and minimized if
\ProbOp\{\bm{A}\neq 0\}=1/k
.)
In particular for
s=\log_{2}k
, this ratio is lower bounded by
1/2
.
On the other hand, for
\bm{B}\sim N(0,1)
, the normalized moments satisfy
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\E\bm{B}^{r}}}\right)^{1/r}=\Theta(r)^{1/2}
and thus the ratio of normalized order-
s
and order-
t
moments is
\Theta(s/t)^{1/2}
.
In particular, for some choice
t=\Theta(s)
, this ratio is smaller than
1/2
.
It follows that for this choice of
s
and
t
, the ratios of normalized moments differ for
\bm{A}
and for
\bm{B}
, which means that either their order-
s
or their order-
t
moments differ.
10
10

10

This proof shows that in order to distinguish a uniform distribution over
k
values from
N(0,1)
, it is enough to compare two moments of order logarithmic in
k
where the choice of orders depends only on
k
but not on the particular distribution.

This observation about uniform mixtures of
k\geqslant 2
well-separated Gaussian components raises two questions:
(1) do the first
O(\log k)
moments also allow us to identify parameters of the mixture that are useful for clustering (in addition to allowing us to distinguish the mixture from
N(0,1)
),
and (2) can we make make these results computationally efficient?

At a high level, we address question (1) by investigating ratios akin to
Eq.

2.2
between multivariate polynomials of degree
\Theta(\log k)
derived from moments of the underlying mixture.
To address question (2), we employ the proofs-to-algorithms paradigm (cf.
[
BS14
,
RSS18
]
)
and translate our arguments to syntactic proofs captured by the sum-of-squares proof system.
These proofs then allow us to derive efficient algorithms (with running time
(kd)^{O(\log k)}
or
(kd)^{(\log k)^{O(1)}}
) in a black-box way.

From decision to search: separating directions and ratios of moments

In order to address question (1), we consider the goal of finding a direction
v\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
that may be useful for clustering in the sense that along direction
v
, two of the components are significantly further apart than their standard deviation in this direction.
More formally, we say that
v
is a
separating direction
for a mixture of
k
Gaussian components with unknown means
\mu_{1},\ldots,\mu_{k}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and unknown covariance
\Sigma\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
if there exist two means
\mu_{a}
and
\mu_{b}
such that

\lvert\langle\mu_{a}-\mu_{b},v\rangle\rvert\gg\sqrt{\log k}\cdot\lVert\Sigma^{1/2}v\rVert\,.

(2.3)

We note that this direction
v
witnesses that the overlap of the components
N(\mu_{a},\Sigma)
and
N(\mu_{b},\Sigma)
is
k^{-\omega(1)}
.
Conversely, whenever the overlap of two components is that small, there exists a vector
v
as above.

We aim to identify separating directions as solutions to inequalities between the following kind of moment polynomials:
For
r\in\mathbb{N}
, we denote the degree-
2r

moment polynomial

p_{2r}\in\mathbb{R}_{2r}[v]
by

p_{2r}(v)\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\E\langle\bm{y}-\bm{y}^{\prime},v\rangle^{2r}\,,

(2.4)

where
\bm{y},\bm{y}^{\prime}
are two independent random vectors identically distributed according to a uniform mixture of
k
Gaussian components
N(\mu_{1},\Sigma),\ldots,N(\mu_{k},\Sigma)
.

Using the fact that
\bm{y}-\bm{y}^{\prime}
can be expressed as a sum of two independent random vectors, one distributed uniformly over
\{\mu_{a}-\mu_{b}\}_{a,b\in[k]}
and one distributed according to
N(0,2\Sigma)
, these polynomials turn out to admit the following kind of approximation,

p_{2r}(v)=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(k^{-2/r}\cdot\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lVert Mv}}\right\rVert_{2r}^{2}+\Theta(r)\cdot\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lVert Av}}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}}}\right)^{r}\,.

(2.5)

Here,
A=\sqrt{2}\cdot\Sigma^{1/2}
,
M\in\mathbb{R}^{k^{2}\times d}
consists of the differences of means
(\mu_{a}-\mu_{b})_{a,b\in[k]}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
as rows and
\Theta(r)
hides a nonnegative function upper bounded
O(r)
and lower bounded by
\Omega(r/k^{2/r})
.
(Since we will only consider
r\geqslant\log k
, we have
k^{-2/r}\geqslant\Omega(1)
.)
Note that the first term
k^{-2}\cdot\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lVert Mv}}\right\rVert_{2r}^{2r}
in (the binomial expansion of)
Eq.

2.5
corresponds to the order-
r
moment of the uniform distribution over
\{\mu_{a}-\mu_{b}\}_{a,b\in[k]}
and the last term
\Theta(r)^{r}\cdot\langle v,2\Sigma v\rangle^{r}
to the order-
r
moment of
N(0,2\Sigma)
.

We claim that for an appropriate choice
s\leqslant t
with
s=\Theta(t)=\Theta(\log k)
, a direction
v
is separating in the sense of
Eq.

2.3
if and only
p_{2s}(v)^{1/s}\gtrsim p_{2t}(v)^{1/t}
.
(Note that by convexity,
p_{2s}(v)^{1/s}\leqslant p_{2t}(v)^{1/t}
holds for all directions
v
.)
Underlying this claim is the familiar fact that for all
r\geqslant\log k
, the norm
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lVert Mv}}\right\rVert_{2r}
equals up to constant factors the maximum entry of
Mv
, i.e.,
\max_{a\neq b}\lvert\langle\mu_{a}-\mu_{b},v\rangle\rvert
.

Indeed, suppose that
v
is a separating direction.
Then,
p_{2s}(v)
satisfies the lower bound,

p_{2s}(v)^{1/s}\geqslant k^{-2/s}\cdot\max_{a\neq b}\langle\mu_{a}-\mu_{b},v\rangle^{2}\,.

(2.6)

Since
s=\Theta(\log k)
, we have
p_{2s}(v)^{1/s}\gtrsim\max_{a\neq b}\langle\mu_{a}-\mu_{b},v\rangle^{2}
.
At the same time,
p_{2t}(v)
satisfies the upper bound,

p_{2t}(v)^{1/t}\leqslant\max_{a\neq b}\langle\mu_{a}-\mu_{b},v\rangle^{2}+O(t)\cdot\lVert Av\rVert_{2}^{2}\,.

(2.7)

Since
v
is a separating direction and
t=\Theta(\log k)
, the upper bound is dominated by the first term
\max_{a\neq b}\langle\mu_{a}-\mu_{b},v\rangle^{2}
.
Taking together both bounds, it follows that
p_{2s}(v)^{1/s}\gtrsim p_{2t}(v)^{1/t}
for every separating direction
v
.

Conversely, suppose that
p_{2s}(v)^{1/s}\gtrsim p_{2t}(v)^{1/t}
and our goal is to show that
v
is a separating direction.
We lower bound
p_{2t}(v)
using the last term in the approximation
Eq.

2.5
and apply the upper bound from
Eq.

2.7
to
p_{2s}(v)
.
In this way, we obtain the inequality

\Omega(t)\cdot\lVert Av\rVert_{2}^{2}\leqslant\max_{a\neq b}\langle\mu_{a}-\mu_{b},v\rangle^{2}+O(s)\cdot\lVert Av\rVert_{2}^{2}\,.

(2.8)

By choosing
t
to be a large enough constant multiplied by
s
, we can ensure that the second term on the right-hand side is negligible.
In this case,
v
satisfies
\max_{a\neq b}\langle\mu_{a}-\mu_{b},v\rangle^{2}\geqslant\Omega(t)\cdot\lVert Av\rVert_{2}^{2}
for
t=\Theta(\log k)
, which means that
v
is a separating direction.

Challenges toward efficient algorithms for clustering

Disregarding computational efficiency, the above characterization of separating directions in terms of ratios of moment polynomials suggests the following simple strategy for clustering uniform mixtures of Gaussian components
N(\mu_{1},\Sigma),\dots,N(\mu_{k},\Sigma)
:
we find an
\varepsilon
-cover of all separating directions by brute-force searching for an
\varepsilon
-cover of all solutions to an explicit polynomial system of the form
\{p_{2s}(v)=1,\penalty\ p_{2t}(v)\leqslant O(1)^{t}\}
.
Each separating direction gives us some information about what pairs of sample points belong to different components.
For large enough mean separation
\min_{a\neq b}\lVert\Sigma^{-1/2}(\mu_{a}-\mu_{b})\rVert\gg\sqrt{\log k\,}
, we can hope that by considering all such directions, we collect enough information to be able to extract a clustering of the sample that is approximately consistent with the components of the mixture.

This naive approach would require access only to moments of order
O(\log k)
(which could be accurately estimated from a sample of size
d^{O(\log k)}
) but the running time is exponentially large (due to brute-force searching for solutions to a polynomial system).

A natural strategy to make this approach computationally efficient is the sum-of-squares hierarchy of semidefinite programming relaxations for systems of polynomial inequalities.
Indeed, we can show that the above characterization of separating directions is faithfully captured by the sum-of-squares proof system underlying the sum-of-squares hierarchy.
Unfortunately, it appears to be challenging to carry out the rounding step in full generality, i.e., extracting from the sum-of-squares hierarchy enough separating directions to separate all pairs of components and obtain a complete clustering of the sample.
11
11

11

In the context of estimation problems, rounding procedures for sum-of-squares hierarchies tend to work well if there is a unique target solution (e.g., a planted sparse vector in a random subspace) or if there is a small number of target solutions (e.g., the components of a low-rank tensor).
One could try to simplify the structure of the set of separating directions, e.g., by focusing on ”extreme” separating directions of the form
v=\Sigma^{-1}(\mu_{a}-\mu_{b})
.
Unfortunately, we do not know the same kind of characterization in terms of polynomial inequalities for such a simplified set of separating directions.

However, we can show that using the sum-of-squares hierarchy, it is possible to separate at least
some
pairs of components of the mixture by what we call a separating polynomial.
Furthermore, for the special case of well-separated components with colinear means, we provide a more careful analysis and show that in this case the sum-of-squares hierarchy does offer enough information to extract a complete clustering.

Efficiently computing a separating polynomial

As discussed above, we consider the goal of separating some pairs of components of a mixture (as opposed to the stronger goal of separating all pairs of components as would be required for a complete clustering).
One way to achieve this goal is by finding a separating direction in the sense of
Eq.

2.3
.
In light of our previous characterization of separating directions, a natural starting point is a sum-of-squares relaxation for a polynomial system
\mathcal{A}
of the form
\{p_{2s}(v)=1,\penalty\ p_{2t}(v)\leqslant O(1)^{t}\}
for appropriate
s\leqslant t
satisfying
s=\Theta(t)=\Theta(\log k)
.

Unfortunately, the structure of the set of separating directions does not appear to be amenable to the usual kind of rounding techniques for sum-of-squares relaxations, and it appears to be challenging to extract a single separating direction.
To overcome this obstacle, we allow our rounding procedure to output a more general object, called a
separating polynomial
, that still allows us to separate some pairs of mixture components.

Recall that a solution to a sum-of-squares relaxation for a polynomial system
\mathcal{A}
can be interpreted as
pseudo-distribution

D
that behaves in certain ways like a distribution supported on vectors satisfying
\mathcal{A}
.
More concretely, the pseudo-distribution
D
satsifies (in expectation) all polynomial inequalities that can be derived syntactically from
\mathcal{A}
by a low-degree sum-of-squares proof (see
Section

3
, especially
Definition

3.1
).
The previously discussed characterization of separating directions in terms of the polynomial system
\mathcal{A}
turns out to be captured by low-degree sum-of-squares proofs.
Concretely, we can derive from
\mathcal{A}
via low-degree sum-of-squares proof the polynomial inequality
12
12

12

Here, we reuse the notation introduced in the context of
Eq.

2.5
.

\lVert Mv\rVert_{2s}^{2s}\geqslant(C\log k)^{s}\cdot\lVert Av\rVert_{2}^{2s}
(corresponding to
Eq.

2.3
).
Here,
C\geqslant 1
is an absolute constant that we can choose as large as we like.
Consequently, the pseudo-distribution
D
satisfies this inequality in expectation,

\tilde{\E}_{D(v)}\lVert Mv\rVert_{2s}^{2s}\geqslant(C\log k)^{s}\cdot\tilde{\E}_{D(v)}\lVert Av\rVert^{2s}_{2}\,.

By linearity of (pseudo-)expectation, there exist distinct components
a\neq b
such that

\tilde{\E_{D(v)}}\langle\mu_{a}-\mu_{b},v\rangle^{2s}\geqslant k^{-2}\tilde{\E_{D(v)}}\lVert Mv\rVert_{2s}^{2s}\geqslant(k^{-2/s}\cdot C\log k)^{s}\cdot\tilde{\E_{D(v)}}\lVert Av\rVert^{2s}_{2}\,.

(2.9)

We extract the following polynomial from this pseudo-distribution,

q(u)\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\tilde{\E_{D(v)}}\langle u,v\rangle^{2s}\,.

(2.10)

By construction,
q(\mu_{a}-\mu_{b})
equals the left-hand side of
Eq.

2.9
.
At the same time, letting
\bm{y},\bm{y}^{\prime}\sim N(\mu_{c},\Sigma)
and
\bm{w}\sim N(0,I_{d})
, we have

\displaystyle\E q(\bm{y}-\bm{y}^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\E q(A\bm{w})

\displaystyle=\tilde{\E_{D(v)}}\E\langle v,A\bm{w}\rangle^{2s}

\displaystyle=(2s-1)!!\cdot\tilde{\E_{D(v)}}\lVert A\bm{w}\rVert^{2s}_{2}

Consequently, since
s=\Theta(\log k)
and
(2s-1)!!\leqslant O(s)^{s}
,

\frac{q(\mu_{a}-\mu_{b})}{\E q(A\bm{w})}\geqslant\frac{(k^{-2/s}\cdot C\log k)^{s}}{(2s-1)!!}\geqslant\Omega(C)^{s}\,.

(2.11)

For an appropriate choice of
C\geqslant 1
, the right-hand side above is at least
10^{s}
.
Since by convexity
\E q(\mu_{a}-\mu_{b}+A\bm{w})\geqslant q(\mu_{a}-\mu_{b})
, we obtain the following inequality,

\frac{\E q(\mu_{a}-\mu_{b}+A\bm{w})}{\E q(A\bm{w})}\geqslant 10^{s}\,.

(2.12)

This inequality shows that the polynomial
q(u)
separates the components
N(\mu_{a},\Sigma)
and
N(\mu_{b},\Sigma)
in the following sense:
The numerator of
Eq.

2.12
is the typical value of
q(\bm{y}-\bm{y}^{\prime})
for
\bm{y}\sim N(\mu_{a},\Sigma)
and
\bm{y}^{\prime}\sim N(\mu_{b},\Sigma)
.
The denominator of
Eq.

2.12
is the typical value of
q(\bm{y}-\bm{y}^{\prime})
for
\bm{y},\bm{y}^{\prime}\sim N(\mu_{c},\Sigma)
and all
c\in[k]
.

Equation

2.12
asserts that the gap between these values is at least
10^{s}
.

The polynomial
q(u)
can be used to compute a bipartition of the sample that separates at least one pair of components.
Note that
q(u)^{1/2s}=(\tilde{\E}_{D(v)}\langle u,v\rangle^{2s})^{1/2s}
satisfies the triangle inequality (see Lemma 4.5 in
[
BS14
]
).
Then we can define the distance function
d_{q}(x,y)=q(x-y)^{1/2s}
and use it in a greedy algorithm in order to obtain the bipartition.

Efficiently computing a clustering for colinear means

For the case that the means are colinear, we consider a strengthening of our previous approach.
Instead of trying to solve a polynomial system of the form
\{p_{2s}(v)=1,\penalty\ p_{2t}(v)\leqslant O(1)^{t}\}
, we aim to solve the following related optimization problem:

\text{minimize}\quad\frac{p_{2t}(v)^{1/t}}{p_{2s}(v)^{1/s}}\quad\text{subject to}\quad v\in\mathbb{R}^{d}.

(2.13)

Algorithmically, we again employ an appropriate sum-of-squares formulation.

To simplify some of our arguments, it is useful to preprocess the mixture and bring
\bm{y}-\bm{y}^{\prime}
in isotropic position so that
\tfrac{1}{k^{2}}\sum_{a,b=1}^{k}(\mu_{a}-\mu_{b})(\mu_{a}-\mu_{b}){}^{\mkern-1.5mu\mathsf{T}}+2\Sigma=I_{d}
.
(Here,
\bm{y},\bm{y}^{\prime}
are two independent random vectors distributed according to the mixture.)
For every vector
v
, we denote by
v^{\parallel}
its orthogonal projection into the span of
\{\mu_{a}-\mu_{b}\}_{a,b\in[k]}
and by
v^{\perp}=v-v^{\parallel}
its projection into the orthogonal complement.

Every optimizer
v
of
Eq.

2.13
necessarily satisfies,

\frac{p_{2t}(v)^{1/t}}{p_{2s}(v)^{1/s}}\leqslant\frac{p_{2t}(v^{\parallel})^{1/t}}{p_{2s}(v^{\parallel})^{1/s}}\leqslant O(1)\,.

(2.14)

Here, the upper bound
O(1)
hides an absolute constant whenever we have well-separated components and
\log k\leqslant s\leqslant t
.
The argument for this upper bound is similar to our discussion for the characterization of separating directions.

We can also use the decomposition
v=v^{\parallel}+v^{\perp}
for our previous approximation
Eq.

2.5
of moment polynomials,

p_{2r}(v)=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(k^{-2/r}\cdot\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lVert Mv^{\parallel}}}\right\rVert_{2r}^{2}+\Theta(r)\cdot\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lVert Av^{\parallel}}}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lVert v^{\perp}}}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}}}\right)}}\right)^{r}\,.

(2.15)

Here, we use that after bringing
\bm{y}-\bm{y}^{\prime}
in isotropic position, the covariance
\Sigma
acts as identity orthogonal to the span of
\{\mu_{a}-\mu_{b}\}_{a,b\in[k]}
.
In particular,
Av=Av^{\parallel}+v^{\perp}
and
\lVert Av\rVert^{2}_{2}=\lVert Av^{\parallel}\rVert_{2}^{2}+\lVert v^{\perp}\rVert_{2}^{2}
.

An immediate consequence of
Eq.

2.15
is the following representation of the ratio we seek to minimize,

\frac{p_{2t}(v)^{1/t}}{p_{2s}(v)^{1/s}}=\frac{p_{2t}(v^{\parallel})^{1/t}+\Theta(t)\cdot\lVert v^{\perp}\rVert_{2}^{2}\pm\Theta(t)\cdot\lVert Av^{\parallel}\rVert_{2}^{2}}{p_{2s}(v^{\parallel})^{1/s}+\Theta(s)\cdot\lVert v^{\perp}\rVert_{2}^{2}\pm\Theta(s)\cdot\lVert Av^{\parallel}\rVert_{2}^{2}}\,.

(2.16)

We claim that for an appropriate choice of
s
and
t

Eq.

2.16
and
Eq.

2.14
together imply that
\lVert v^{{\perp}}\rVert\lesssim\lVert Av^{\parallel}\rVert
.
Indeed, for the sake of a contradiction, suppose
\lVert v^{{\perp}}\rVert\gg\lVert Av^{\parallel}\rVert
so that the terms involving
\lVert Av^{\parallel}\rVert
in
Eq.

2.16
are negligible.
But then, if we choose
t
as
s
times a sufficiently larger constant factor, the remaining ratio
\frac{p_{2t}(v^{\parallel})^{1/t}+\Theta(t)\cdot\lVert v^{\perp}\rVert_{2}^{2}}{p_{2s}(v^{\parallel})^{1/s}+\Theta(s)\cdot\lVert v^{\perp}\rVert_{2}^{2}}
is strictly bigger than
\frac{p_{2t}(v^{\parallel})^{1/t}}{p_{2s}(v^{\parallel})^{1/s}}
, which contradicts our optimality condition
Eq.

2.14
.
(For this argument, we are also using the previous upper bound
\frac{p_{2t}(v^{\parallel})^{1/t}}{p_{2s}(v^{\parallel})^{1/s}}\leqslant O(1)
from
Eq.

2.14
.)

It turns out that in order to compute a clustering for colinear means, it suffices to find a vector satisfying
\lVert v^{{\perp}}\rVert\lesssim\lVert Av^{\parallel}\rVert
.

We note that the algorithm we present in
Sections

5
and
6
to find such a direction
v
follows a somewhat different strategy and minimizes ratios of the form
{\lVert v\rVert_{2}^{2}}/{p_{2s}(v)^{1/s}}
or
{p_{2t}(v)^{1/t}}/{\lVert v\rVert_{2}^{2}}
via appropriate sum-of-squares formulations.

3
Preliminaries

In this section we introduce sum-of-squares proofs and their duals, pseudo-distributions and pseudo-expectations.

Sum-of-squares proofs.

Definition 3.1

(Sum-of-squares proofs)
.

Let
p(x)
and
q_{1}(x),...,q_{m}(x)
be polynomials over
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
and let
\mathcal{A}=\{q_{1}(x)\geqslant 0,...,q_{m}(x)\geqslant 0\}
be a system of polynomial inequalities.
A
sum-of-squares proof of degree
t
that
p(x)\geqslant 0
under
\mathcal{A}
is an identity of the form

p(x)=\sum_{S\subseteq[m]}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sum_{i=1}^{m_{S}}r_{S,i}(x)^{2}}}\right)\prod_{j\in S}q_{j}(x)

(3.1)

for polynomials
r_{S,i}(x)
, such that
\max_{S,i}\operatorname{deg}(r_{S,i}(x)^{2}\prod_{j\in S}q_{j}(x))\leqslant t
.

If there exists a sum-of-squares proof of degree
t
that
p(x)\geqslant 0
under
\mathcal{A}
, we write
\mathcal{A}\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{8.53467pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-8.53467pt\raisebox{-6.26668pt}{\makebox[8.53467pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{t}$}}}\hskip-8.53467pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[8.53467pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{x}$}}}$}}}p(x)\geqslant 0
.
We also use the notation
\mathcal{A}\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{8.53467pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-8.53467pt\raisebox{-6.26668pt}{\makebox[8.53467pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{t}$}}}\hskip-8.53467pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[8.53467pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{x}$}}}$}}}p(x)\geqslant q(x)
if
\mathcal{A}\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{8.53467pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-8.53467pt\raisebox{-6.26668pt}{\makebox[8.53467pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{t}$}}}\hskip-8.53467pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[8.53467pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{x}$}}}$}}}p(x)-q(x)\geqslant 0
and
\mathcal{A}\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{8.53467pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-8.53467pt\raisebox{-6.26668pt}{\makebox[8.53467pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{t}$}}}\hskip-8.53467pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[8.53467pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{x}$}}}$}}}p(x)\leqslant q(x)
if
\mathcal{A}\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{8.53467pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-8.53467pt\raisebox{-6.26668pt}{\makebox[8.53467pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{t}$}}}\hskip-8.53467pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[8.53467pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{x}$}}}$}}}q(x)-p(x)\geqslant 0
.
If
\mathcal{A}=\emptyset
, we omit it altogether and write
\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{8.53467pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-8.53467pt\raisebox{-6.26668pt}{\makebox[8.53467pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{t}$}}}\hskip-8.53467pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[8.53467pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{x}$}}}$}}}p(x)\geqslant 0
.
We also sometimes omit
\mathcal{A}
if it is clear from context what axioms are assumed.
We note that if
\mathcal{A}\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{8.53467pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-8.53467pt\raisebox{-6.26668pt}{\makebox[8.53467pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{t}$}}}\hskip-8.53467pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[8.53467pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{x}$}}}$}}}p(x)\geqslant q(x)
and
\mathcal{A}\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{7.77426pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-7.77426pt\raisebox{-6.26668pt}{\makebox[7.77426pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{t}$}}}\hskip-7.77426pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[7.77426pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{s}$}}}$}}}q(x)\geqslant r(x)
, then
\mathcal{A}\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{8.53467pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-8.53467pt\raisebox{-6.26668pt}{\makebox[8.53467pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{t}$}}}\hskip-8.53467pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[8.53467pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{x}$}}}$}}}p(x)\geqslant r(x)
, which allows writing chains of inequalities of the form
\mathcal{A}\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{8.53467pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-8.53467pt\raisebox{-6.26668pt}{\makebox[8.53467pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{t}$}}}\hskip-8.53467pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[8.53467pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{x}$}}}$}}}p(x)\geqslant s(x)\geqslant r(x)
.

Pseudo-distributions and pseudo-expectations.

We begin by defining pseudo-distributions and pseudo-expectations.

Definition 3.2

(Pseudo-distributions)
.

A
pseudo-distribution

D
of degree
t
is a function from
\mathbb{R}^{n}
to
\mathbb{R}
with finite support such that
\sum_{x\in\operatorname{supp}(D)}D(x)=1
and
\sum_{x\in\operatorname{supp}(D)}D(x)p(x)^{2}\geqslant 0
for all polynomials
p(x)
with
\operatorname{deg}(p(x)^{2})\leqslant t
.

Definition 3.3

(Pseudo-expectations)
.

Given a pseudo-distribution
D
of degree
t
, the associated
pseudo-expectation

\tilde{\mathbb{E}}_{D(x)}
is defined by
\tilde{\mathbb{E}}_{D(x)}f(x)=\sum_{x\in\operatorname{supp}(D)}D(x)f(x)
for a function
f(x)
.

We now define the notion of a pseudo-distribution that satisfies a set of polynomial inequalities.

Definition 3.4

(Constrained pseudo-distributions)
.

A pseudo-distribution
D
of degree
t

satisfies
the set of polynomial inequalities
\mathcal{A}=\{q_{1}(x)\geqslant 0,...,q_{m}(x)\geqslant 0\}
if, for all
S\subseteq[m]
,
\tilde{\mathbb{E}}_{D(x)}r(x)^{2}\prod_{j\in S}q_{j}(x)\geqslant 0
for all polynomials
r(x)
such that
\operatorname{deg}(r(x)^{2}\prod_{j\in S}q_{j}(x))\leqslant t
.

D

approximately satisfies

\mathcal{A}
up to error
\eta
if, under the same conditions as in the previous case,
\tilde{\mathbb{E}}_{D(x)}r(x)^{2}\prod_{j\in S}q_{j}(x)\geqslant-\eta\|r(x)^{2}\|_{2}\prod_{j\in S}\|q_{j}(x)\|_{2}
, where
\|p(x)\|_{2}
denotes the
2
-norm of the vector of coefficients of the polynomial
p(x)
.

The connection between pseudo-distributions and sum-of-squares proofs is made in
3.5
, which shows that if a pseudo-distribution satisfies a set of polynomial inequalities, then it also satisfies any other polynomial inequalities derived from this set through sum-of-squares proofs.

Fact 3.5
.

If
D
is a pseudo-distribution of degree
t
that satisfies
\mathcal{A}
and if
\mathcal{A}\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{8.53467pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-8.53467pt\raisebox{-4.975pt}{\makebox[8.53467pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{s}$}}}\hskip-8.53467pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[8.53467pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{x}$}}}$}}}p(x)\geqslant 0
, then
{\tilde{\mathbb{E}}_{D(x)}r(x)^{2}p(x)\geqslant 0}
for all polynomials
r(x)
such that
\operatorname{deg}(r(x)^{2}p(x))\leqslant t
.
If
D
approximately satisfies
\mathcal{A}
up to error
\eta
, then, under the same conditions as in the previous case,
{\tilde{\mathbb{E}}_{D(x)}r(x)^{2}p(x)\geqslant-\eta\|r(x)^{2}\|_{2}\|p(x)\|_{2}}
.

Finally,
3.6
shows that there exists an algorithm with time complexity
(n+m)^{O(t)}
to compute a pseudo-distribution of degree
t
that approximately satisfies
\mathcal{A}
up to error
2^{-n^{\Theta(t)}}
.

Fact 3.6
.

For
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, if
\mathcal{A}=\{q_{1}(x)\geqslant 0,...,q_{m}(x)\geqslant 0\}
is feasible and explicitly bounded

13
13

13

Explicit boundedness means that
\mathcal{A}
contains a constraint of the form
x_{1}^{2}+...+x_{n}^{2}\leqslant B
.
In our applications it is possible to add such a constraint with
B
large enough such that the constraint is always satisfied by the intended solution.

, then there exists an algorithm that runs in time
(n+m)^{O(t)}
and computes a pseudo-distribution of degree
t
that approximately satisfies
\mathcal{A}
up to error
2^{-n^{\Theta(t)}}

14
14

14

In our applications this error is negligible.
.

4
Separating polynomial

Setting.

We consider a mixture of
k
Gaussian distributions
N(\mu_{i},\Sigma)
with mixing weights
p_{i}
for
i=1,...,k
,
where
\mu_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
\Sigma\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
is positive definite, and
p_{i}\geqslant 0
and
\sum_{i=1}^{k}p_{i}=1
.
Let
p_{\mathrm{min}}=\min_{i}p_{i}
.

The distribution satisfies mean separation for at least one pair of means:
for some
C_{sep}>0
, there exist
a,b\in[k]
such that

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|\Sigma^{-1/2}(\mu_{a}-\mu_{b})}}\right\|^{2}\geqslant C_{sep}\log p_{\mathrm{min}}^{-1}.

Theorem 4.1

(Separating polynomial algorithm)
.

Consider the Gaussian mixture model defined above, with
C_{sep}
larger than some universal constant. Let
n_{0}=(p_{\mathrm{min}}^{-1}d)^{O(\log p_{\mathrm{min}}^{-1})}
. Given a sample of size
n\geqslant n_{0}
from the mixture, there exists an algorithm that computes in time
n\cdot d^{O(\log p_{\mathrm{min}}^{-1})}
a
d
-variate degree-
O(\log p_{\mathrm{min}}^{-1})
polynomial
q
such that with high probability the following two properties hold. Let
s=\lceil\log p_{\mathrm{min}}^{-1}\rceil
. Then:

•

There exist distinct
a,b\in[k]
such that the independent random vectors
\bm{y}\sim N(\mu_{a},\Sigma)
and
\bm{y}^{\prime}\sim N(\mu_{b},\Sigma)
satisfy

\ProbOp\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{q(\bm{y}-\bm{y}^{\prime})\geqslant\frac{1}{20^{s}}}}\right\}\geqslant 0.99999.\,

•

For all
a\in[k]
, the independent random vectors
\bm{y},\bm{y}^{\prime}\sim N(\mu_{a},\Sigma)
satisfy

\ProbOp\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{q(\bm{y}-\bm{y}^{\prime})\leqslant\frac{1}{200^{s}}}}\right\}\geqslant 0.99999.\,

Theorem 4.2

(Separating bipartition algorithm)
.

Consider the Gaussian mixture model defined above, with
C_{sep}
larger than some universal constant. Let
n_{0}=(p_{\mathrm{min}}^{-1}d)^{O(\log p_{\mathrm{min}}^{-1})}
. Given a sample of size
n\geqslant n_{0}
from the mixture, there exists an algorithm that runs in time
n\cdot d^{O(\log p_{\mathrm{min}}^{-1})}
and returns with probability
0.99
a partition of
[n]
into two sets
C_{1}
and
C_{2}
such that, if true clustering of the samples is
S_{1},...,S_{k}
, then

\max_{i}\frac{|C_{1}\cap S_{i}|}{|S_{i}|}\geqslant 0.99\quad\text{and}\quad\max_{i}\frac{|C_{2}\cap S_{i}|}{|S_{i}|}\geqslant 0.99.

We introduce some further notation for this section. Let the random variable
\bm{z}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
be distributed according to the difference of two independent samples from the mixture. Then
\bm{z}
is distributed according to a mixture of Gaussians
N(\mu_{i}-\mu_{j},2\Sigma)
with mixing weights
p_{i}p_{j}
for all
i,j\in[k]
. Let
\Sigma_{z}=2\Sigma
, let
\bm{\mu}_{z}
be
\mu_{i}-\mu_{j}
with probability
p_{i}p_{j}
, and let
\bm{w}_{z}\sim N(0,\Sigma_{z})
. Then we also have that
\bm{z}=\bm{\mu}_{z}+\bm{w}_{z}
, with
\bm{\mu}_{z}
and
\bm{w}_{z}
independent of each other.

4.1
Exact moment results

The main ingredient of the algorithm is
Lemma

4.3
, stated below.
This lemma shows that, given a pseudo-expectation that satisfies the moment lower bound
\mathbb{E}\langle\bm{z},v\rangle^{2s}\geqslant c^{s}
and the moment upper bound
\mathbb{E}\langle\bm{z},v\rangle^{2t}\leqslant C^{t}
for
s\ll t
, it is possible to construct a separating polynomial.
Note that the constraints that the pseudo-expectation satisfies are expressed in terms of exact moments of the distribution, to which we do not have access.
Finite sample considerations are discussed starting with
Section

4.2
.

Lemma 4.3

(Separating polynomial from pseudo-expectation)
.

Let
c>0
and
C\geqslant 0
. Let
s\geqslant 1
and
t\geqslant 50000Cs/c
integers.
Given a pseudo-expectation
\tilde{\mathbb{E}}
of degree at least
2t
over a variable
v\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
that satisfies
\{\mathbb{E}\langle\bm{z},v\rangle^{2s}\geqslant c^{s},\mathbb{E}\langle\bm{z},v\rangle^{2t}\leqslant C^{t}\}
, let
q(u)=\langle\tilde{\mathbb{E}}v^{\otimes 2s},u^{\otimes 2s}\rangle
.
Then:

•

There exist distinct
a,b\in[k]
such that the independent random vectors
\bm{y}\sim N(\mu_{a},\Sigma)
and
\bm{y}^{\prime}\sim N(\mu_{b},\Sigma)
satisfy

\ProbOp\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{q(\bm{y}-\bm{y}^{\prime})\geqslant\frac{1}{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{c}{16}}}\right)^{s}}}\right\}\geqslant 0.99999\,.

•

For all
a\in[k]
, the independent random vectors
\bm{y},\bm{y}^{\prime}\sim N(\mu_{a},\Sigma)
satisfy

\ProbOp\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{q(\bm{y}-\bm{y}^{\prime})\leqslant 320\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{4Cs}{t}}}\right)^{s}}}\right\}\geqslant 0.99999\,.

In what follows, we prove a number of supporting lemmas, after which we prove
Lemma

4.3
.
Then, we state and prove
Lemma

4.8
, which shows that there exists a vector
v\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
which satisfies the constraints required by
Lemma

4.3
.

We proceed with the supporting lemmas.
Lemma

4.4
and
Lemma

4.5
give sum-of-squares bounds on the moments of the mixture.

Lemma 4.4

(Moment upper bound)
.

For
t\geqslant 1
integer,

\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{11.00691pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-11.00691pt\raisebox{-6.47223pt}{\makebox[11.00691pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{2t}$}}}\hskip-11.00691pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[11.00691pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{v}$}}}$}}}\mathbb{E}\langle\bm{z},v\rangle^{2t}\leqslant 2^{2t-1}\mathbb{E}\langle\bm{\mu}_{z},v\rangle^{2t}+2^{2t-1}(v^{\top}\Sigma_{z}v)^{t}t^{t}.

Proof.

\displaystyle\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{11.00691pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-11.00691pt\raisebox{-6.47223pt}{\makebox[11.00691pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{2t}$}}}\hskip-11.00691pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[11.00691pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{v}$}}}$}}}\mathbb{E}\langle\bm{z},v\rangle^{2t}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}(\langle\bm{\mu}_{z},v\rangle+\langle\bm{w}_{z},v\rangle)^{2t}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(1)}}{{\leqslant}}2^{2t-1}\mathbb{E}\langle\bm{\mu}_{z},v\rangle^{2t}+2^{2t-1}\mathbb{E}\langle\bm{w}_{z},v\rangle^{2t}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(2)}}{{\leqslant}}2^{2t-1}\mathbb{E}\langle\bm{\mu}_{z},v\rangle^{2t}+2^{2t-1}(v^{\top}\Sigma_{z}v)^{t}t^{t}

where in (1) we used
Lemma

A.5

and in (2) we used that
\mathbb{E}\langle\bm{w}_{z},v\rangle^{2t}=(v^{\top}\Sigma_{z}v)^{t}(2t-1)!!\leqslant(v^{\top}\Sigma_{z}v)^{t}t^{t}
.
∎

Lemma 4.5

(Moment lower bound)
.

For
t\geqslant 1
integer,

\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{11.00691pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-11.00691pt\raisebox{-6.47223pt}{\makebox[11.00691pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{2t}$}}}\hskip-11.00691pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[11.00691pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{v}$}}}$}}}\mathbb{E}\langle\bm{z},v\rangle^{2t}\geqslant\mathbb{E}\langle\bm{\mu}_{z},v\rangle^{2t}+(v^{\top}\Sigma_{z}v)^{t}\frac{t^{t}}{2^{t}}.

Proof.

\displaystyle\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{11.00691pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-11.00691pt\raisebox{-6.47223pt}{\makebox[11.00691pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{2t}$}}}\hskip-11.00691pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[11.00691pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{v}$}}}$}}}\mathbb{E}\langle\bm{z},v\rangle^{2t}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}(\langle\bm{\mu}_{z},v\rangle+\langle\bm{w}_{z},v\rangle)^{2t}=\sum_{j=0}^{2t}\binom{2t}{j}\mathbb{E}\langle\bm{\mu}_{z},v\rangle^{j}\langle\bm{w}_{z},v\rangle^{2t-j}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(1)}}{{=}}\sum_{j=0}^{2t}\binom{2t}{j}\mathbb{E}\langle\bm{\mu}_{z},v\rangle^{j}\mathbb{E}\langle\bm{w}_{z},v\rangle^{2t-j}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(2)}}{{=}}\sum_{s=0}^{t}\binom{2t}{2s}\mathbb{E}\langle\bm{\mu}_{z},v\rangle^{2s}\mathbb{E}\langle\bm{w}_{z},v\rangle^{2t-2s}

\displaystyle\geqslant\mathbb{E}\langle\bm{\mu}_{z},v\rangle^{2t}+\mathbb{E}\langle\bm{w}_{z},v\rangle^{2t}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(3)}}{{\geqslant}}\mathbb{E}\langle\bm{\mu}_{z},v\rangle^{2t}+(v^{\top}\Sigma_{z}v)^{t}\frac{t^{t}}{2^{t}}

where in (1) we used that
\bm{\mu}_{z}
and
\bm{w}_{z}
are independent, in (2) we used that
\mathbb{E}\langle\bm{w}_{z},v\rangle^{2t-j}=0
for
2t-j
odd, and in (3) we used that
\mathbb{E}\langle\bm{w}_{z},v\rangle^{2t}=(v^{\top}\Sigma_{z}v)^{t}(2t-1)!!\geqslant(v^{\top}\Sigma_{z}v)^{t}\frac{t^{t}}{2^{t}}
.
∎

Going forward,
Lemma

4.6
proves that, if the moments of
\bm{z}
are small in direction
v
, then the variance of the components of the mixture is also small in direction
v
.
Given in addition an upper bound on the moments of
\bm{z}
in direction
v
for a sufficiently large moment,
Lemma

4.7
proves that the contribution of the means in direction
v
is large.

Lemma 4.6
.

Let
C\geqslant 0
. For
t\geqslant 1
integer,

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\mathbb{E}\langle\bm{z},v\rangle^{2t}\leqslant C^{t}}}\right\}\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{11.00691pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-11.00691pt\raisebox{-6.47223pt}{\makebox[11.00691pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{2t}$}}}\hskip-11.00691pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[11.00691pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{v}$}}}$}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{v^{\top}\Sigma_{z}v\leqslant\frac{2C}{t}}}\right\}.

Proof.

Substitute the lower bound of
Lemma

4.5
into the axiom:

\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{11.00691pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-11.00691pt\raisebox{-6.47223pt}{\makebox[11.00691pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{2t}$}}}\hskip-11.00691pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[11.00691pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{v}$}}}$}}}\mathbb{E}\langle\bm{\mu}_{z},v\rangle^{2t}+(v^{\top}\Sigma_{z}v)^{t}t^{t}/2^{t}\leqslant C^{t}.

Use that
\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{11.00691pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-11.00691pt\raisebox{-6.47223pt}{\makebox[11.00691pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{2t}$}}}\hskip-11.00691pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[11.00691pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{v}$}}}$}}}\mathbb{E}\langle\bm{\mu}_{z},v\rangle^{2t}\geqslant 0
to drop the first term and then divide by
t^{t}/2^{t}
. This proves that
\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{11.00691pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-11.00691pt\raisebox{-6.47223pt}{\makebox[11.00691pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{2t}$}}}\hskip-11.00691pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[11.00691pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{v}$}}}$}}}(v^{\top}\Sigma_{z}v)^{t}\leqslant 2^{t}C^{t}/t^{t}.

Finally, by
Lemma

A.9
, this implies that
\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{11.00691pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-11.00691pt\raisebox{-6.47223pt}{\makebox[11.00691pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{2t}$}}}\hskip-11.00691pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[11.00691pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{v}$}}}$}}}v^{\top}\Sigma_{z}v\leqslant 2C/t
.
∎

Lemma 4.7
.

Let
c>0
and
C\geqslant 0
. For
s\geqslant 1
and
t\geqslant 16Cs/c
integers,

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\mathbb{E}\langle\bm{z},v\rangle^{2s}\geqslant c^{s},\mathbb{E}\langle\bm{z},v\rangle^{2t}\leqslant C^{t}}}\right\}\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{11.00691pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-11.00691pt\raisebox{-6.47223pt}{\makebox[11.00691pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{2t}$}}}\hskip-11.00691pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[11.00691pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{v}$}}}$}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\mathbb{E}\langle\bm{\mu}_{z},v\rangle^{2s}\geqslant\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{c}{4}}}\right)^{s}}}\right\}.

Proof.

Substitute the upper bound of
Lemma

4.4
into the first axiom:

\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{11.76039pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-11.76039pt\raisebox{-6.47223pt}{\makebox[11.76039pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{2s}$}}}\hskip-11.76039pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[11.76039pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{v}$}}}$}}}2^{2s-1}\mathbb{E}\langle\bm{\mu}_{z},v\rangle^{2s}+2^{2s-1}(v^{\top}\Sigma_{z}v)^{s}s^{s}\geqslant c^{s}.

Use, by
Lemma

4.6
and
Lemma

A.8
, that
\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{11.00691pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-11.00691pt\raisebox{-6.47223pt}{\makebox[11.00691pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{2t}$}}}\hskip-11.00691pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[11.00691pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{v}$}}}$}}}(v^{\top}\Sigma_{z}v)^{s}\leqslant\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(2C/t}}\right)^{s}
:

\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{11.76039pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-11.76039pt\raisebox{-6.47223pt}{\makebox[11.76039pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{2s}$}}}\hskip-11.76039pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[11.76039pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{v}$}}}$}}}2^{2s-1}\mathbb{E}\langle\bm{\mu}_{z},v\rangle^{2s}+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(8Cs/t}}\right)^{s}\geqslant c^{s}.

Then use that
t\geqslant 16Cs/c
to obtain that
\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{11.76039pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-11.76039pt\raisebox{-6.47223pt}{\makebox[11.76039pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{2s}$}}}\hskip-11.76039pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[11.76039pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{v}$}}}$}}}2^{2s-1}\mathbb{E}\langle\bm{\mu}_{z},v\rangle^{2s}\geqslant c^{s}/2
. Finally, divide by
2^{2s-1}
to obtain that
\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{11.76039pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-11.76039pt\raisebox{-6.47223pt}{\makebox[11.76039pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{2s}$}}}\hskip-11.76039pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[11.76039pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{v}$}}}$}}}\mathbb{E}\langle\bm{\mu}_{z},v\rangle^{2s}\geqslant(c/4)^{s}
.
∎

Now we prove
Lemma

4.3
.

Proof of
Lemma

4.3
.

Let
\bm{w}\sim N(0,I_{d})
.
Note that, for
\bm{y}\sim N(\mu_{a},\Sigma)
and
\bm{y}^{\prime}\sim N(\mu_{b},\Sigma)
we have that
\bm{y}-\bm{y}^{\prime}\sim\mu_{a}-\mu_{b}+\Sigma_{z}^{1/2}\bm{w}
, so
q(\bm{y}-\bm{y}^{\prime})=q(\mu_{a}-\mu_{b}+\Sigma_{z}^{1/2}\bm{w})
.
Similarly, for
\bm{y},\bm{y}^{\prime}\sim N(\mu_{a},\Sigma)
we have that
\bm{y}-\bm{y}^{\prime}\sim\Sigma_{z}^{1/2}\bm{w}
, so
q(\bm{y}-\bm{y}^{\prime})=q(\Sigma_{z}^{1/2}\bm{w})
.
Therefore, we want to show (1) that there exist distinct
a,b\in[k]
such that
q(\mu_{a}-\mu_{b}+\Sigma_{z}^{1/2}\bm{w})
is large and (2) that
q(\Sigma_{z}^{1/2}\bm{w})
is small.

By
Lemma

4.7
, we have
\tilde{\mathbb{E}}\mathbb{E}\langle\bm{\mu}_{z},v\rangle^{2s}\geqslant\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(c/4}}\right)^{s}
.
By linearity, we also have
\mathbb{E}\tilde{\mathbb{E}}\langle\bm{\mu}_{z},v\rangle^{2s}\geqslant\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(c/4}}\right)^{s}
, so
\mathbb{E}q(\bm{\mu}_{z})\geqslant(c/4)^{s}
.
Therefore there exists some
\mu_{z}
in the support of
\bm{\mu}_{z}
such that
q(\mu_{z})\geqslant(c/4)^{s}
.
Therefore, there exist
a,b\in[k]
such that
q(\mu_{a}-\mu_{b})\geqslant(c/4)^{s}
.
Furthermore,
a
and
b
are distinct, because otherwise
q(\mu_{a}-\mu_{b})=0
.

We attempt to lower bound
q(\mu_{a}-\mu_{b}+\Sigma_{z}^{1/2}\bm{w})
:

\displaystyle q(\mu_{a}-\mu_{b}+\Sigma_{z}^{1/2}\bm{w})

\displaystyle=\langle\tilde{\mathbb{E}}v^{\otimes 2s},(\mu_{a}-\mu_{b}+\Sigma_{z}^{1/2}\bm{w})^{\otimes 2s}\rangle

\displaystyle=\tilde{\mathbb{E}}\langle v,\mu_{a}-\mu_{b}+\Sigma_{z}^{1/2}\bm{w}\rangle^{2s}

\displaystyle\geqslant\frac{1}{2^{2s-1}}\tilde{\mathbb{E}}\langle v,\mu_{a}-\mu_{b}\rangle^{2s}-\tilde{\mathbb{E}}\langle v,\Sigma_{z}^{1/2}\bm{w}\rangle^{2s}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2^{2s-1}}q(\mu_{a}-\mu_{b})-q(\Sigma_{z}^{1/2}\bm{w})

\displaystyle\geqslant\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(c/16}}\right)^{s}-q(\Sigma_{z}^{1/2}\bm{w}).

We want to show that
q(\Sigma_{z}^{1/2}\bm{w})
is small with high probability. We start by analyzing the mean and second moment of
q(\Sigma_{z}^{1/2}\bm{w})
. For
\ell\in\{1,2\}
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}q(\Sigma_{z}^{1/2}\bm{w})^{\ell}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\langle\tilde{\mathbb{E}}v^{\otimes 2s},(\Sigma_{z}^{1/2}\bm{w})^{\otimes 2s}\rangle}}\right)^{\ell}=\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\tilde{\mathbb{E}}\langle v,\Sigma_{z}^{1/2}\bm{w}\rangle^{2s}}}\right)^{\ell}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(1)}}{{\leqslant}}\mathbb{E}\tilde{\mathbb{E}}\langle v,\Sigma_{z}^{1/2}\bm{w}\rangle^{2s\ell}=\tilde{\mathbb{E}}\mathbb{E}\langle v,\Sigma_{z}^{1/2}\bm{w}\rangle^{2s\ell}

\displaystyle=\tilde{\mathbb{E}}\mathbb{E}\langle\Sigma_{z}^{1/2}v,\bm{w}\rangle^{2s\ell}\leqslant(s\ell)^{s\ell}\tilde{\mathbb{E}}(v^{\top}\Sigma_{z}v)^{s\ell}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(2)}}{{\leqslant}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(2CS\ell/t}}\right)^{s\ell},

where in (1) for
\ell=2
we used
Lemma

A.14
and in (2) we used that, by
Lemma

4.6
and
Lemma

A.8
,
\tilde{\mathbb{E}}(v^{\top}\Sigma_{z}v)^{s\ell}\leqslant O\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(2C/t}}\right)^{s\ell}
.
Then
\mathbb{E}q(\Sigma_{z}{1/2}\bm{w})\leqslant\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(2Cs/t}}\right)^{s}
and
\mathbb{E}q(\Sigma_{z}^{1/2}\bm{w})^{2}\leqslant\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(4Cs/t}}\right)^{2s}
.
Therefore, by Chebyshev’s inequality, with probability
0.99999
,

q(\Sigma_{z}^{1/2}\bm{w})\leqslant\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(2Cs/t}}\right)^{s}+\sqrt{100000}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(4Cs/t}}\right)^{s}\leqslant 320\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(4Cs/t}}\right)^{s}.

In this case, we also have

q(\mu_{a}-\mu_{b}+\Sigma_{z}^{1/2}\bm{w})\geqslant\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(c/16}}\right)^{s}-320\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(4Cs/t}}\right)^{s}\geqslant\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(c/16}}\right)^{s}/2,

where in the last inequality we used that
t\geqslant 50000Cs/c
. This concludes the proof.
∎

Lemma 4.8

(Existence of vector that satisfies moment contraints)
.

Let
s,t\geqslant\lceil\log p_{\mathrm{min}}^{-1}\rceil
integers.
If
t\leqslant\max_{i,j}\|\Sigma_{z}^{-1/2}(\mu_{i}-\mu_{j})\|^{2}
, there exists some
v\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
that satisfies
\mathbb{E}\langle\bm{z},v\rangle^{2s}=1
and
\mathbb{E}\langle\bm{z},v\rangle^{2t}\leqslant 30^{t}
.
Furthermore,
\|\operatorname{cov}(\bm{z})^{1/2}v\|^{2}\leqslant 8
.

Proof.

Let
(a,b)=\arg\max_{(i,j)}\|\Sigma_{z}^{-1/2}(\mu_{i}-\mu_{j})\|
and let
v=\Sigma_{z}^{-1}(\mu_{a}-\mu_{b})
.
The vector for which we will guarantee the stated properties is
v^{*}=\frac{v}{(\mathbb{E}\langle\bm{z},v\rangle^{2s})^{1/2s}}
.

We begin by proving that
\max_{i,j}\langle\mu_{i}-\mu_{j},v\rangle^{2}=\langle\mu_{a}-\mu_{b},v\rangle^{2}
, which will be used later.
We have

\displaystyle\max_{i,j}\langle\mu_{i}-\mu_{j},v\rangle^{2}

\displaystyle=\max_{i,j}\langle\mu_{i}-\mu_{j},\Sigma_{z}^{-1}(\mu_{a}-\mu_{b})\rangle^{2}

\displaystyle=\max_{i,j}\langle\Sigma_{z}^{-1/2}(\mu_{i}-\mu_{j}),\Sigma_{z}^{-1/2}(\mu_{a}-\mu_{b})\rangle^{2}

\displaystyle\leqslant\|\Sigma_{z}^{-1/2}(\mu_{i}-\mu_{j})\|^{2}\cdot\|\Sigma_{z}^{-1/2}(\mu_{a}-\mu_{b})\|^{2}

\displaystyle\leqslant\|\Sigma_{z}^{-1/2}(\mu_{a}-\mu_{b})\|^{4}

\displaystyle=\langle\mu_{a}-\mu_{b},v\rangle^{2}.

We also have for the variance in direction
v
that

(v^{\top}\Sigma_{z}v)^{t}=((\mu_{a}-\mu_{b})^{\top}\Sigma_{z}^{-1}(\mu_{a}-\mu_{b}))^{t}=\|\Sigma_{z}^{-1/2}(\mu_{a}-\mu_{b})\|^{2t}.

We now derive upper bounds for the
2t
moments of
\langle\bm{z},v\rangle
in direction
v
and lower bounds for the
2s
moments in direction
v
. Recall that we assume
t\leqslant\max_{i,j}\|\Sigma_{z}^{-1/2}(\mu_{i}-\mu_{j})\|^{2}
. For the upper bound, using
Lemma

4.4
we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\langle\bm{z},v\rangle^{2t}

\displaystyle\leqslant 2^{2t-1}\mathbb{E}\langle\bm{\mu}_{z},v\rangle^{2t}+2^{2t-1}(v^{\top}\Sigma_{z}v)^{t}t^{t}

\displaystyle\leqslant 2^{2t-1}\max_{i,j}\langle\mu_{i}-\mu_{j},v\rangle^{2t}+2^{2t-1}\|\Sigma_{z}^{-1/2}(\mu_{a}-\mu_{b})\|^{2t}t^{t}

\displaystyle=2^{2t-1}\|\Sigma_{z}^{-1/2}(\mu_{a}-\mu_{b})\|^{4t}+2^{2t-1}\|\Sigma_{z}^{-1/2}(\mu_{a}-\mu_{b})\|^{2t}t^{t}

\displaystyle\leqslant 2^{2t-1}\|\Sigma_{z}^{-1/2}(\mu_{a}-\mu_{b})\|^{4t}+2^{2t-1}\|\Sigma_{z}^{-1/2}(\mu_{a}-\mu_{b})\|^{4t}

\displaystyle\leqslant 2^{2t}\|\Sigma_{z}^{-1/2}(\mu_{a}-\mu_{b})\|^{4t}.

For the lower bound, using
Lemma

4.5
we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\langle\bm{z},v\rangle^{2s}

\displaystyle\geqslant\mathbb{E}\langle\bm{\mu}_{z},v\rangle^{2s}+(v^{\top}\Sigma_{z}v)^{s}\frac{s^{s}}{2^{s}}

\displaystyle\geqslant p_{\mathrm{min}}^{2}\max_{i,j}\langle\mu_{i}-\mu_{j},v\rangle^{2s}+\|\Sigma_{z}^{-1/2}(\mu_{a}-\mu_{b})\|^{2s}\frac{s^{s}}{2^{s}}

\displaystyle=p_{\mathrm{min}}^{2}\|\Sigma_{z}^{-1/2}(\mu_{a}-\mu_{b})\|^{4s}+\|\Sigma_{z}^{-1/2}(\mu_{a}-\mu_{b})\|^{2s}\frac{s^{s}}{2^{s}}

\displaystyle\geqslant p_{\mathrm{min}}^{2}\|\Sigma_{z}^{-1/2}(\mu_{a}-\mu_{b})\|^{4s}.

Recall that
v^{*}=\frac{v}{(\mathbb{E}\langle\bm{z},v\rangle^{2s})^{1/2s}}
. Clearly,
\mathbb{E}\langle\bm{z},v^{*}\rangle^{2s}=1
. Furthermore,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\langle\bm{z},v^{*}\rangle^{2t}

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathbb{E}\langle\bm{z},v\rangle^{2t}}{(\mathbb{E}\langle\bm{z},v\rangle^{2s})^{t/s}}\leqslant\frac{2^{2t}\|\Sigma_{z}^{-1/2}(\mu_{a}-\mu_{b})\|^{4t}}{p_{\mathrm{min}}^{2t/s}\|\Sigma_{z}^{-1/2}(\mu_{a}-\mu_{b})\|^{4t}}=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{4}{p_{\mathrm{min}}^{2/s}}}}\right)^{t}\leqslant\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(4e^{2}}}\right)^{t}\leqslant 30^{t}

where in the last inequality we used that
p_{\mathrm{min}}^{1/s}\geqslant e^{-1}
. Therefore
v^{*}
satisfies the desired moment constraints.

Finally, we prove that
\|\operatorname{cov}(\bm{z})^{1/2}v^{*}\|^{2}\leqslant 8
.
Note that
\operatorname{cov}(\bm{z})=\operatorname{cov}(\bm{\mu}_{z})+\Sigma_{z}
.
We have then

\displaystyle\|\operatorname{cov}(\bm{z})^{1/2}v^{*}\|^{2}

\displaystyle=(v^{*})^{\top}\operatorname{cov}(\bm{z})v^{*}

\displaystyle=(v^{*})^{\top}\operatorname{cov}(\bm{\mu}_{z})v^{*}+(v^{*})^{\top}\Sigma_{z}v^{*}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(1)}}{{\leqslant}}\max_{i,j}\langle\mu_{i}-\mu_{j},v^{*}\rangle^{2}+\|\Sigma_{z}^{1/2}v^{*}\|^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{\|\Sigma_{z}^{-1/2}(\mu_{a}-\mu_{b})\|^{4}}{(\mathbb{E}\langle\bm{z},v\rangle^{2s})^{1/s}}+\frac{\|\Sigma_{z}^{1/2}\Sigma_{z}^{-1}(\mu_{a}-\mu_{b})\|^{2}}{(\mathbb{E}\langle\bm{z},v\rangle^{2s})^{1/s}}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(2)}}{{\leqslant}}\frac{\|\Sigma_{z}^{-1/2}(\mu_{a}-\mu_{b})\|^{4}}{p_{\mathrm{min}}^{2/s}\|\Sigma_{z}^{-1/2}(\mu_{a}-\mu_{b})\|^{4}}+\frac{\|\Sigma_{z}^{1/2}\Sigma_{z}^{-1}(\mu_{a}-\mu_{b})\|^{2}}{p_{\mathrm{min}}^{2/s}\|\Sigma_{z}^{-1/2}(\mu_{a}-\mu_{b})\|^{4}}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(3)}}{{\leqslant}}e^{2}+e^{2}\frac{1}{\|\Sigma_{z}^{-1/2}(\mu_{a}-\mu_{b})\|^{2}}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(4)}}{{=}}e^{2}+o(1)\leqslant 8,

where in (1) we used that
(v^{*})^{\top}\operatorname{cov}(\bm{\mu}_{z})v^{*}=(v^{*})^{\top}\mathbb{E}\bm{\mu}_{z}\bm{\mu}_{z}^{\top}v^{*}\leqslant\max_{\mu_{z}}(v^{*})^{\top}\mu_{z}\mu_{z}^{\top}v^{*}
for
\mu_{z}
in the support of
\bm{\mu}_{z}
,
in (2) we used the lower bound that we derived above on
\mathbb{E}\langle\bm{z},v\rangle^{2s}
,
in (3) we used that
p_{\mathrm{min}}^{1/s}\geqslant e^{-1}
,
and in (4) we used mean separation.

∎

4.2
Finite sample bounds

Recall that, to apply
Lemma

4.3
, we need to find a pseudo-expectation that satisfies the moment lower bound
\mathbb{E}\langle\bm{z},v\rangle^{2s}\geqslant c^{s}
and the moment upper bound
\mathbb{E}\langle\bm{z},v\rangle^{2t}\leqslant C^{t}
for
s\ll t
.

Lemma

4.9
shows that it suffices to find a pseudo-expectation that satisfies
\|\widehat{\operatorname{cov}}(\bm{z})^{1/2}v\|^{2}\lesssim 8
,
\hat{\mathbb{E}}\langle\bm{z},v\rangle^{2s}\succsim c^{s}
, and
\hat{\mathbb{E}}\langle\bm{z},v\rangle^{2t}\lesssim C^{t}
.
Without the bound on the norm of
\widehat{\operatorname{cov}}(\bm{z})^{1/2}v
the errors may be arbitrarily large.

The result is supported by
Lemma

4.10
, which shows that quadratics in the empirical covariance matrix are close to quadratics in the population covariance matrix of the components, and by
Lemma

4.11
, which shows that the empirical moments are close to the population moments.
The proofs of these two lemmas are deferred to the appendix.

Lemma 4.9

(Moment constraints from empirical moment constraints)
.

Let
\eta<0.001
. Let
c,C\geqslant 0
and let
t\geqslant 1
integer. For
n\geqslant(p_{\mathrm{min}}^{-1}d)^{O(t)}\eta^{-2}\varepsilon^{-1}
, with probability
1-\varepsilon
,

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\|\widehat{\operatorname{cov}}(\bm{z})^{1/2}v\|^{2}\leqslant(1+\eta)\cdot 8,\hat{\mathbb{E}}\langle\bm{z},v\rangle^{2s}\geqslant c^{s}+\eta,\hat{\mathbb{E}}\langle\bm{z},v\rangle^{2t}\leqslant C^{t}-\eta}}\right\}

\displaystyle\qquad\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{11.00691pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-11.00691pt\raisebox{-6.47223pt}{\makebox[11.00691pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{2t}$}}}\hskip-11.00691pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[11.00691pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{v}$}}}$}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\mathbb{E}\langle\bm{z},v\rangle^{2s}\geqslant c^{s},\mathbb{E}\langle\bm{z},v\rangle^{2t}\leqslant C^{t}}}\right\}.

Furthermore, with probability
1-\varepsilon
, the axiom is satisfied with
s
,
t
, and
v
as in
Lemma

4.8
and with
c=(1-\eta)^{1/s}
and
C=(30^{t}+\eta)^{1/t}
.

Proof.

By
Lemma

4.10
,
\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{8.25113pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-8.25113pt\raisebox{-6.47223pt}{\makebox[8.25113pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{2}$}}}\hskip-8.25113pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[8.25113pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{v}$}}}$}}}\|\operatorname{cov}(\bm{z})^{1/2}v\|^{2}\leqslant(1+\eta)^{2}\cdot 8\leqslant 9
.
Then, by
Lemma

4.11
,
\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{11.76039pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-11.76039pt\raisebox{-6.47223pt}{\makebox[11.76039pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{2s}$}}}\hskip-11.76039pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[11.76039pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{v}$}}}$}}}\mathbb{E}\langle\bm{z},v\rangle^{2s}\geqslant c^{s}
and
\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{11.00691pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-11.00691pt\raisebox{-6.47223pt}{\makebox[11.00691pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{2t}$}}}\hskip-11.00691pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[11.00691pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{v}$}}}$}}}\mathbb{E}\langle\bm{z},v\rangle^{2t}\leqslant C^{t}
.
With
n
as given, this holds with probability at least
1-\varepsilon
.

For the second claim of the lemma, we have by
Lemma

4.8
that there exists some
v
with
\mathbb{E}\langle\bm{z},v\rangle^{2s}=1
,
\mathbb{E}\langle\bm{z},v\rangle^{2t}\leqslant 30^{t}
, and
\|\operatorname{cov}(\bm{z})^{1/2}v\|^{2}\leqslant 8
.
By
Lemma

4.10
, we also have that
\|\widehat{\operatorname{cov}}(\bm{z})^{1/2}v\|^{2}\leqslant(1+\eta)^{2}\cdot 8\leqslant 9
, and then by
Lemma

4.11
, we also have that
\hat{\mathbb{E}}\langle\bm{z},v\rangle^{2s}\geqslant 1-\eta
and
\hat{\mathbb{E}}\langle\bm{z},v\rangle^{2t}\leqslant 30^{t}+\eta
.
Again, with
n
as given, this holds with probability at least
1-\varepsilon
.
∎

Lemma 4.10
.

Let
C\geqslant 0
. For
n\geqslant kd^{2}\log^{2}(d/\varepsilon)O(\eta^{-2})
, with probability
1-\varepsilon
,

\{\|\widehat{\operatorname{cov}}(\bm{z})^{1/2}v\|^{2}\leqslant C\}\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{8.25113pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-8.25113pt\raisebox{-6.47223pt}{\makebox[8.25113pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{2}$}}}\hskip-8.25113pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[8.25113pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{v}$}}}$}}}\{\|\operatorname{cov}(\bm{z})^{1/2}v\|^{2}\leqslant(1+\eta)C\},

\{\|\operatorname{cov}(\bm{z})^{1/2}v\|^{2}\leqslant C\}\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{8.25113pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-8.25113pt\raisebox{-6.47223pt}{\makebox[8.25113pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{2}$}}}\hskip-8.25113pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[8.25113pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{v}$}}}$}}}\{\|\widehat{\operatorname{cov}}(\bm{z})^{1/2}v\|^{2}\leqslant(1+\eta)C\}.

Proof.

See
Section

A.6
.
∎

Lemma 4.11
.

Let
C\geqslant 0
and let
t\geqslant 1
integer. For
n\geqslant(Cp_{\mathrm{min}}^{-1}d)^{O(t)}\eta^{-2}\varepsilon^{-1}
, with probability
1-\varepsilon
,

\{\|\operatorname{cov}(\bm{z})^{1/2}v\|^{2}\leqslant C\}\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{19.56857pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-19.56857pt\raisebox{-7.21112pt}{\makebox[19.56857pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{O(t)}$}}}\hskip-19.56857pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[19.56857pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{v}$}}}$}}}\{\hat{\mathbb{E}}\langle\bm{z},v\rangle^{2t}\leqslant\mathbb{E}\langle\bm{z},v\rangle^{2t}+\eta\},

\{\|\operatorname{cov}(\bm{z})^{1/2}v\|^{2}\leqslant C\}\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{19.56857pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-19.56857pt\raisebox{-7.21112pt}{\makebox[19.56857pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{O(t)}$}}}\hskip-19.56857pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[19.56857pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{v}$}}}$}}}\{\hat{\mathbb{E}}\langle\bm{z},v\rangle^{2t}\geqslant\mathbb{E}\langle\bm{z},v\rangle^{2t}-\eta\}.

Proof.

See
Section

A.6
.
∎

4.3
Proof of Theorem
4.1

Proof.

Let
s=\lceil\log p_{\mathrm{min}}^{-1}\rceil
and
t=10000000s
. The algorithm is:

1.

Compute a pseudo-expectation
\tilde{\mathbb{E}}
of degree
2t
over
v\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
such that
\|\widehat{\operatorname{cov}}(\bm{z})^{1/2}v\|^{2}\leqslant 1.01\cdot 8
,
\hat{\mathbb{E}}\langle\bm{y},v\rangle^{2s}\geqslant 1-0.005
, and
\hat{\mathbb{E}}\langle\bm{y},v\rangle^{2t}\leqslant 30^{t}+0.005\}
.

2.

Construct a separating polynomial
q
based on
\tilde{\mathbb{E}}
as in
Lemma

4.3
.

3.

Return
q
.

We now analyze the algorithm.
First, we argue that there exists a pseudo-expectation
\tilde{\mathbb{E}}
that satisfies the given constraints.
Note that
t\leqslant\max_{i,j}\|\Sigma_{z}^{-1/2}(\mu_{i}-\mu_{j})\|^{2}
if
C_{sep}
is a large enough constant.
Therefore, the conditions of
Lemma

4.8
for
s
and
t
are satisfied.
Then, by
Lemma

4.9
, for
n\geqslant n_{0}
, there exists a vector
v\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
that satisfies the given constraints.
Then, there also exists a pseudo-expectation that satisfies the constraints.

Second, we argue that
q
has the desired properties.
By
Lemma

4.9
,
\tilde{\mathbb{E}}
also sastisfies with high probability that
\hat{\mathbb{E}}\langle\bm{y},v\rangle^{2s}\geqslant 1-0.01\geqslant 0.99^{s}
and
\hat{\mathbb{E}}\langle\bm{y},v\rangle^{2t}\leqslant 30^{t}+0.01\leqslant 31^{t}
.
Then the conditions of
Lemma

4.3
are satisfied with
c=0.99
and
C=31
.
Then, we are guaranteed to return a separating polynomial
q
with the following properties:

•

For independent random vectors
\bm{y}
and
\bm{y}^{\prime}
sampled from different components, we have with probability at least
0.99999
that

q(\bm{y}-\bm{y}^{\prime})\geqslant\frac{1}{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{c}{16}}}\right)^{s}\geqslant\frac{1}{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{0.99}{16}}}\right)^{s}\geqslant\frac{1}{20^{s}}.

•

For independent random vectors
\bm{y}
and
\bm{y}^{\prime}
sampled from the same component, we have with probability at least
0.99999
that

q(\bm{y}-\bm{y}^{\prime})\leqslant 320\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{4Cs}{t}}}\right)^{s}\leqslant 320\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{4\cdot 31\cdot s}{t}}}\right)^{s}\leqslant\frac{1}{200^{s}}.

The time complexity of the algorithm is dominated by the time to compute the pseudo-expectation.
The pseudo-expectation is of degree
O(\log p_{\mathrm{min}}^{-1})
over
d
variables, and each constraint requires summing over the
n
samples.
Therefore, the time to compute the pseudo-expectation is
n\cdot d^{O(\log p_{\mathrm{min}}^{-1})}
.
∎

4.4
Proof of Theorem
4.2

We start by stating and proving
Lemma

4.12
, which shows how to obtain a bipartition of the samples given a suitable distance function. We then prove
Theorem

4.2
.

Lemma 4.12

(Bipartition from distance function)
.

Assume access to a distance function
d_{q}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
such that:

•

There exist distinct
a,b\in[k]
such that the independent random vectors
\bm{y}\sim N(\mu_{a},\Sigma)
and
\bm{y}^{\prime}\sim N(\mu_{b},\Sigma)
satisfy

\ProbOp\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{d_{q}(\bm{y},\bm{y}^{\prime})\geqslant\frac{1}{\sqrt{20}}}}\right\}\geqslant 0.99999.\,

•

For all
a\in[k]
, the independent random vectors
\bm{y},\bm{y}^{\prime}\sim N(\mu_{a},\Sigma)
satisfy

\ProbOp\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{d_{q}(\bm{y},\bm{y}^{\prime})\leqslant\frac{1}{\sqrt{200}}}}\right\}\geqslant 0.99999.\,

Given a sample of size
n=\Omega(p_{\mathrm{min}}^{-1})
from the mixture, there exists a polynomial-time algorithm that returns with probability
0.99
a partition of
[n]
into two sets
C_{1}
and
C_{2}
such that, if true clustering of the samples is
S_{1},...,S_{k}
, then

\max_{i}\frac{|C_{1}\cap S_{i}|}{|S_{i}|}\geqslant 0.99\quad\text{and}\quad\max_{i}\frac{|C_{2}\cap S_{i}|}{|S_{i}|}\geqslant 0.99.

Proof.

The algorithm is:

1.
