Choose
i\in[n]
uniformly at random.

2.

Let
S=\{j\in[n]:d_{q}(y_{i},y_{j})\leqslant\frac{1}{\sqrt{200}}\}
.

3.

Return
S
and
[n]\setminus S
.

We now analyze the algorithm.

First, we prove that, with probability at least
0.995
, a
0.99
-fraction of the samples from the same component as
i
are included in
S
.
With high probability, a
0.99998
-fraction of the pairs of samples
(y,y^{\prime})
with
y
and
y^{\prime}
from the same component as
i
satisfy
d_{q}(y,y^{\prime})\leqslant\frac{1}{\sqrt{200}}
.
Then, the fraction of samples from this component that are farther than
\frac{1}{\sqrt{200}}
from more than a
0.01
-fraction of the other samples in the component is at most
\frac{1-0.99998}{0.01}=0.002
.
Then, overall, with probability at least
0.995
,
i
is closer than
\frac{1}{\sqrt{200}}
to at least a
0.99
-fraction of the other samples in the component.
In this case,
S
includes a
0.99
-fraction of the samples from the same component as
i
.

Second, we prove that, with probability at least
0.995
, at least a
0.99
-fraction of the samples from one of the components are not included in
S
.
Let
a,b\in[k]
be the two components for which the large-distance guarantee holds.
With high probability, a
0.99998
-fraction of the pairs of samples
(y,y^{\prime})
with
y
from
a
and
y^{\prime}
from
b
satisfy
d_{q}(y,y^{\prime})\geqslant\frac{1}{\sqrt{20}}
.
We have
d_{q}(y,y^{\prime})\leqslant d_{q}(y,y_{i})+d_{q}(y^{\prime},y_{i})
, so if
d_{q}(y,y^{\prime})\geqslant\frac{1}{\sqrt{20}}
, then at least one of
d_{q}(y,y_{i})
or
d_{q}(y^{\prime},y_{i})
is at least
\frac{1}{2\sqrt{20}}>\frac{1}{\sqrt{200}}
.
Then, for such pairs, it is impossible for both
y
and
y^{\prime}
to be in
S
.
Suppose that a
p_{a}
-fraction of the samples from
a
are in
S
and that a
p_{b}
-fraction of the samples from
b
are in
S
.
We need then that
1-p_{a}p_{b}\geqslant 0.99998
, so
\min(p_{a},p_{b})\leqslant\sqrt{1-0.9998}\leqslant 0.01
.
Therefore,
[n]\setminus S
contains at least a
0.99
-fraction of the samples from one of
a
or
b
.

Therefore, with probability at least
0.99
, the conclusion of the lemma holds.
∎

Proof of
Theorem

4.2
.

The algorithm is:

1.

Run the algorithm from
Theorem

4.1
to obtain a polynomial
q
.

2.

Run the algorithm from
Lemma

4.12
with the distance function
d_{q}(x,y)=q(x-y)^{1/2s}
.

3.

Return the resulting bipartition.

We now analyze the algorithm.
Recall that
q(u)=\tilde{\E}\langle u,v\rangle^{2s}
, so
q(u)^{1/2s}=(\tilde{\E}\langle u,v\rangle^{2s})^{1/2s}
.
Then, we have by
Lemma

A.15
that
q^{1/2s}
satisfies the triangle inequality.
It follows that
d_{q}(x,y)=q(x-y)^{1/2s}
is a distance function.
Then, by the guarantees of
Theorem

4.1
,
d_{q}
satisfies the requirements of
Lemma

4.12
, so the stated guarantees follow.

The time complexity is dominated by the time complexity of the algorithm from
Theorem

4.1
.

∎

5
Parallel pancakes

The model studied in this section is a well-separated mixture of Gaussians with colinear means that is in isotropic position.
As shown in
Section

5.1
, the isotropic position property makes this model similar to the parallel pancakes construction, in the sense that the only direction in which the components of the mixture have variance different from
1
is the direction of the means.
In
Section

6
we study the same model without the isotropic position assumption.

Setting.

We consider a mixture of
k
Gaussian distributions
N(\mu_{i},\Sigma)
with mixing weights
p_{i}
for
i=1,...,k
,
where
\mu_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
\Sigma\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
is positive definite, and
p_{i}\geqslant 0
and
\sum_{i=1}^{k}p_{i}=1
.
Let
p_{\mathrm{min}}=\min_{i}p_{i}
.

The distribution is in isotropic position: for
\bm{y}
distributed according to the mixture, we have
\mathbb{E}\bm{y}=0
and
\operatorname{cov}(\bm{y})=I_{d}
.

The distribution also satisfies mean separation and mean colinearity:

•

Mean separation:
for some
C_{sep}>0
and for all
i\neq j
,

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|\Sigma^{-1/2}(\mu_{i}-\mu_{j})}}\right\|^{2}\geqslant C_{sep}\log p_{\mathrm{min}}^{-1}.

•

Mean colinearity:
for some unit vector
u\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and for all
i
,

\mu_{i}=\langle\mu_{i},u\rangle u.

Also define
\sigma^{2}=u^{\top}\Sigma u
, which is the variance of the components in the direction of the means.

Theorem 5.1

(Parallel pancakes algorithm)
.

Consider the Gaussian mixture model defined above, with
C_{sep}
larger than some universal constant. Let

n_{0}=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}}}}\right)^{O(1)}\cdot(p_{\mathrm{min}}^{-1}d)^{O(\log p_{\mathrm{min}}^{-1})}.

Given a sample of size
n\geqslant n_{0}
from the mixture, there exists an algorithm that runs in time
n^{O(\log p_{\mathrm{min}}^{-1})}
and returns with high probability a partition of
[n]
into
k
sets
C_{1},...,C_{k}
such that, if the true clustering of the samples is
S_{1},...,S_{k}
, then there exists a permutation
\pi
of
[k]
such that

1-\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{k}|C_{i}\cap S_{\pi(i)}|\leqslant\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{p_{\mathrm{min}}}{k}}}\right)^{O(1)}.

We introduce some further notation for this section.
Let
\bm{y}
be distributed according to the mixture.
We specify the model as
\bm{y}=\bm{\mu}+\bm{w}
, where
\bm{\mu}
takes value
\mu_{i}
with probability
p_{i}
and
\bm{w}\sim N(0,\Sigma)
, with
\bm{\mu}
and
\bm{w}
independent of each other.

5.1
Isotropic position properties

In this section we prove some consequences of the fact that
\bm{y}
is in isotropic position.

Lemma

5.2
shows that
\Sigma=I_{d}-(1-\sigma^{2})uu^{\top}
with
0<\sigma^{2}\leqslant 1
.
This means that
\Sigma
can have at most one eigenvalue less than
1
and that the eigenvectors corresponding to this eigenvalue are parallel to the direction of the means
u
.
Then
Lemma

5.3
uses this form of
\Sigma
to quantify the separation of the means along direction
u
in terms of
\sigma^{2}
.

Lemma 5.2

(Isotropic position component covariance matrix)
.

We have that (1)
\sigma^{2}=1-\sum_{i=1}^{k}p_{i}\langle\mu_{i},u\rangle^{2}
, (2)
\Sigma=I_{d}-(1-\sigma^{2})uu^{\top}
, and (3)
0<\sigma^{2}\leqslant 1
.

Proof.

We have that
\mathbb{E}\bm{y}=\mathbb{E}\bm{\mu}+\mathbb{E}\bm{w}=\mathbb{E}\bm{\mu}
. Because the distribution is in isotropoic position, we also have that
\mathbb{E}\bm{y}=0
, so the equation above implies that
\mathbb{E}\bm{\mu}=0
. Then
\operatorname{cov}(\bm{\mu})=\sum_{i=1}^{k}p_{i}\mu_{i}\mu_{i}^{\top}
.

Furthermore, since
\bm{\mu}
and
\bm{w}
are independent, we have that
\operatorname{cov}(\bm{y})=\operatorname{cov}(\bm{\mu})+\operatorname{cov}(\bm{w})=\sum_{i=1}^{k}p_{i}\mu_{i}\mu_{i}^{\top}+\Sigma
.
Because the distribution is in isotropic position, we also have that
\operatorname{cov}(\bm{y})=I_{d}
, so the equation above implies
\Sigma=I_{d}-\sum_{i=1}^{k}p_{i}\mu_{i}\mu_{i}^{\top}
. Plugging in
\mu_{i}=\langle\mu_{i},u\rangle u
, we have
\Sigma=I_{d}-\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sum_{i=1}^{k}p_{i}\langle\mu_{i},u\rangle^{2}}}\right)uu^{\top}
.

Then, it follows that
\sigma^{2}=u^{\top}\Sigma u=1-\sum_{i=1}^{k}p_{i}\langle\mu_{i},u\rangle^{2}
. This proves (1). The fact that
1-\sigma^{2}=\sum_{i=1}^{k}p_{i}\langle\mu_{i},u\rangle^{2}
also proves (2). For (3),
\sigma^{2}>0
follows by the definition using that
\Sigma
is positive definite and
\sigma^{2}\leqslant 1
follows by (1).
∎

Lemma 5.3

(Isotropic position mean separation)
.

For all
i\neq j
,

\langle\mu_{i}-\mu_{j},u\rangle^{2}\geqslant C_{sep}\sigma^{2}\log p_{\mathrm{min}}^{-1}.

Proof.

By
Lemma

5.2
,
\Sigma=I_{d}-(1-\sigma^{2})uu^{\top}
. This implies that
\Sigma^{-1/2}=I_{d}+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1/\sqrt{\sigma^{2}}-1}}\right)uu^{\top}
. Then, using that
\mu_{i}=\langle\mu_{i},u\rangle u
, we have that

\displaystyle\Sigma^{-1/2}(\mu_{i}-\mu_{j})

\displaystyle=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(I_{d}+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{\sigma^{2}}}-1}}\right)uu^{\top}}}\right)\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\langle\mu_{i}-\mu_{j},u\rangle u}}\right)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\sigma^{2}}}\langle\mu_{i}-\mu_{j},u\rangle u.

Therefore, the separation condition
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|\Sigma^{-1/2}(\mu_{i}-\mu_{j})}}\right\|^{2}\geqslant C_{sep}\log p_{\mathrm{min}}^{-1}
is equivalent to
\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}}\langle\mu_{i}-\mu_{j},u\rangle^{2}\geqslant C_{sep}\log p_{\mathrm{min}}^{-1}
. The conclusion follows by multiplying both sides by
\sigma^{2}
.
∎

5.2
Exact moment direction recovery

In this section we discuss how to recover a direction close to the direction of the means
u
, assuming oracle access to moments
\mathbb{E}\bm{y}^{\otimes t}
for any positive integer
t
.
Access to these moments allows us to calculate exactly directional moments of the form
\mathbb{E}\langle\bm{y},v\rangle^{t}
, which simplifies the analysis.
Finite sample considerations are discussed starting with
Section

5.3
.

Theorem

5.4
shows that there exists an algorithm that computes a unit vector
\hat{u}
with correlation
1-O(\min(\sigma^{2},\frac{1}{k}))
with the direction of the means
u
.
We remark that it is necessary for
\hat{u}
to have a correlation of at least
1-O(\sigma^{2})
with
u
in order for the components of the mixture to be separated along direction
\hat{u}
.

Theorem 5.4

(Direction recovery with exact moments)
.

Assume oracle access to
\mathbb{E}\bm{y}^{\otimes t}
for any positive integer
t
.
Then there exists an algorithm with time complexity
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\log\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}}}}\right)\cdot d^{O(\log p_{\mathrm{min}}^{-1})}
that outputs a unit vector
\hat{u}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
such that
\langle u,\hat{u}\rangle^{2}\geqslant 1-320\min(\sigma^{2},\frac{1}{k})
.

The two main ingredients for
Theorem

5.4
are
Theorem

5.5
, which gives an algorithm to compute pseudo-expectations over unit vectors correlated with
u
, and
Theorem

5.6
, which gives an algorithm to sample from such pseudo-expectations.
We note that
Theorem

5.5
can be interpreted as a collection of sum-of-squares identifiability proofs for the direction of the means
u
.

We state these two supporting theorems and then prove
Theorem

5.4
.
After that, we work toward proving the supporting theorems.

Theorem 5.5

(Direction sum-of-squares identifiability)
.

Assume oracle access to
\mathbb{E}\bm{y}^{\otimes t}
for any positive integer
t
.
Then there exists an algorithm with time complexity
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\log\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}}}}\right)\cdot d^{O(\log p_{\mathrm{min}}^{-1})}
that computes two peseudo-expectations
\tilde{\mathbb{E}}_{U}
and
\tilde{\mathbb{E}}_{L}
of degree
O(\log p_{\mathrm{min}}^{-1})
over a variable
v\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
such that the following holds.
Let
s=\lceil\log p_{\mathrm{min}}^{-1}\rceil
, let
t=5000s
, and let
\tau=\frac{800e}{C_{sep}k^{2}}
.
Then
\tilde{\mathbb{E}}_{U}\|v\|^{2}=1
,
\tilde{\mathbb{E}}_{L}\|v\|^{2}=1
, and:

•

If
\sigma^{2}\geqslant\tau
, then
\tilde{\mathbb{E}}_{U}\langle u,v\rangle^{2s}\geqslant(1-\tau)^{s}
.

•

If
\sigma^{2}<\tau
and
\mathbb{E}\langle\bm{\mu},u\rangle^{2s}\geqslant(4es)^{s}
, then

\tilde{\mathbb{E}}_{U}\langle u,v\rangle^{2s}\geqslant(1-\sigma^{2})^{s}
.

•

If
\sigma^{2}<0.001
and
\mathbb{E}\langle\bm{\mu},u\rangle^{2s}\leqslant(100s)^{s}
, then

\tilde{\mathbb{E}}_{L}\langle u,v\rangle^{2t}\geqslant(1-20\sigma^{2})^{t}
.

Theorem 5.6

(Direction sum-of-squares sampling)
.

Let
t\in\mathbb{N}
and
\varepsilon\in\mathbb{R}
such that
t\geqslant 1
and
0\leqslant\varepsilon\leqslant 1/(3t^{2})
.
Let
u\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
be a unit vector.
Given a pseudo-expectation
\tilde{\mathbb{E}}
of degree
2t
over a variable
v\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
that satisfies
\tilde{\mathbb{E}}\|v\|^{2}=1
and
\tilde{\mathbb{E}}\langle u,v\rangle^{2t}\geqslant(1-\varepsilon)^{t}
,
there exists an algorithm with time complexity
d^{O(t)}
that returns a unit vector
\hat{u}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
such that
\langle u,\hat{u}\rangle^{2}\geqslant 1-16\varepsilon
.

Proof of
Theorem

5.4
.

Let
\tau=\frac{800e}{C_{sep}k^{2}}
. The algorithm is:

1.

Run the algorithm from
Theorem

5.5
to obtain pseudo-expectations
\tilde{\mathbb{E}}_{U}
and
\tilde{\mathbb{E}}_{L}
.

2.

Run the algorithm from
Theorem

5.6
for pseudo-expectations
\tilde{\mathbb{E}}_{U}
and
\tilde{\mathbb{E}}_{L}
to obtain unit vectors
\hat{u}_{U}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
\hat{u}_{L}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, respectively.

3.

If
\sigma^{2}\geqslant\tau
, return
\hat{u}_{U}
. Else, for
s=\lceil\log p_{\mathrm{min}}^{-1}\rceil
, if
\mathbb{E}\langle\bm{y},\hat{u}_{U}\rangle^{2s}\geqslant(50s)^{s}
, return
\hat{u}_{U}
. Else, return
\hat{u}_{L}
.

We now analyze the algorithm. We consider the three possible cases in step (3) of the algorithm:

•

Suppose
\sigma^{2}\geqslant\tau
. Then
Theorem

5.5
guarantees that
\tilde{\mathbb{E}}_{U}\langle u,v\rangle^{2s}\geqslant(1-\tau)^{s}
, so by
Theorem

5.6
we have
\langle u,\hat{u}_{U}\rangle^{2}\geqslant 1-16\tau\geqslant 1-16\min(\sigma^{2},\tau)
.

•

Suppose
\sigma^{2}<\tau
and
(\mathbb{E}\langle\bm{y},\hat{u}_{U}\rangle^{2s})^{1/s}\geqslant 50s
. We have by
Lemma

5.8
that
(\mathbb{E}\langle\bm{y},\hat{u}_{U}\rangle^{2s})^{1/s}\leqslant(\mathbb{E}\langle\bm{\mu},u\rangle^{2s})^{1/s}+es,

so it must be the case that
(\mathbb{E}\langle\bm{\mu},u\rangle^{2s})^{1/s}\geqslant(50-e)s\geqslant 4es
. Then
Theorem

5.5
guarantees that
\tilde{\mathbb{E}}_{U}\langle u,v\rangle^{2s}\geqslant(1-\sigma^{2})^{s}
, so by
Theorem

5.6
we have
\langle u,\hat{u}_{U}\rangle^{2}\geqslant 1-16\sigma^{2}\geqslant 1-16\min(\sigma^{2},\tau)
.

•

Suppose
\sigma^{2}<\tau
and
\mathbb{E}\langle\bm{y},\hat{u}_{U}\rangle^{2s}<(50s)^{s}
. We have by
Lemma

5.9
that
(\mathbb{E}\langle\bm{y},\hat{u}_{U}\rangle^{2s})^{1/s}\geqslant(\mathbb{E}\langle\bm{\mu},u\rangle^{2s})^{1/s},
so it must be the case that
(\mathbb{E}\langle\bm{\mu},u\rangle^{2s})^{1/s}<50s\leqslant 100s
. Then
Theorem

5.5
guarantees that
\tilde{\mathbb{E}}_{L}\langle u,v\rangle^{2t}\geqslant(1-20\sigma^{2})^{t}
, so by
Theorem

5.6
we have
\langle u,\hat{u}_{L}\rangle^{2}\geqslant 1-16\cdot 20\sigma^{2}\geqslant 1-320\min(\sigma^{2},\tau)
.

Let
\hat{u}
be the unit vector returned by step (3) of the algorithm. Then we are guaranteed that in all cases
\langle u,\hat{u}\rangle^{2}\geqslant 1-320\min(\sigma^{2},\tau)\geqslant 1-320\min(\sigma^{2},\frac{1}{k})
, where we used the loose upper bound
\tau\leqslant\frac{1}{k}
.

The time complexity of the algorithm is dominated by the time to run the algorithm from
Theorem

5.5
.
∎

5.2.1
Sum-of-squares identifiability (proof of Theorem
5.5
)

We prove a number of supporting lemmas and then prove
Theorem

5.5
.
The most important components are
Lemma

5.11
and
Lemma

5.12
,
which give sum-of-squares proofs that, for suitably chosen
s,t=O(\log p_{\mathrm{min}}^{-1})
, either the maximizer of
\mathbb{E}\langle\bm{y},v\rangle^{2s}
or the minimizer of
\mathbb{E}\langle\bm{y},v\rangle^{2t}
over unit vectors
v
must be close to
u
.

We start with
Lemma

5.7
,
Lemma

5.8
and
Lemma

5.9
, which give sum-of-squares bounds on the moments of the mixture.
Informally, for
t=\Omega(\log p_{\mathrm{min}}^{-1})
, these bounds correspond to the following decomposition of the directional
2t
moments:

(\mathbb{E}\langle\bm{y},v\rangle^{2t})^{1/t}=\langle u,v\rangle^{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathbb{E}\langle\bm{\mu},u\rangle^{2t}}}\right)^{1/t}+\Theta(1)\cdot t(v^{\top}\Sigma v).

(5.1)

Lemma 5.7

(Moment equality)
.

For
t\geqslant 1
integer,

\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{11.00691pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-11.00691pt\raisebox{-6.47223pt}{\makebox[11.00691pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{2t}$}}}\hskip-11.00691pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[11.00691pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{v}$}}}$}}}\mathbb{E}\langle\bm{y},v\rangle^{2t}=\sum_{s=0}^{t}\binom{2t}{2s}\langle u,v\rangle^{2s}\mathbb{E}\langle\bm{\mu},u\rangle^{2s}(v^{\top}\Sigma v)^{t-s}(2t-2s-1)!!.

Proof.

\displaystyle\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{11.00691pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-11.00691pt\raisebox{-6.47223pt}{\makebox[11.00691pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{2t}$}}}\hskip-11.00691pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[11.00691pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{v}$}}}$}}}\mathbb{E}\langle\bm{y},v\rangle^{2t}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}(\langle\bm{\mu},v\rangle+\langle\bm{w},v\rangle)^{2t}=\sum_{j=0}^{2t}\binom{2t}{j}\mathbb{E}\langle\bm{\mu},v\rangle^{j}\langle\bm{w},v\rangle^{2t-j}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(1)}}{{=}}\sum_{j=0}^{2t}\binom{2t}{j}\mathbb{E}\langle\bm{\mu},v\rangle^{j}\mathbb{E}\langle\bm{w},v\rangle^{2t-j}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(2)}}{{=}}\sum_{s=0}^{t}\binom{2t}{2s}\mathbb{E}\langle\bm{\mu},v\rangle^{2s}\mathbb{E}\langle\bm{w},v\rangle^{2t-2s}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(3)}}{{=}}\sum_{s=0}^{t}\binom{2t}{2s}\langle u,v\rangle^{2s}\mathbb{E}\langle\bm{\mu},u\rangle^{2s}(v^{\top}\Sigma v)^{t-s}(2t-2s-1)!!

where in (1) we used that
\bm{\mu}
and
\bm{w}
are independent, in (2) we used that
\mathbb{E}\langle\bm{w},v\rangle^{2t-j}=0
for
2t-j
odd, and in (3) we used that
\bm{\mu}=\langle\bm{\mu},u\rangle u
and that
\mathbb{E}\langle\bm{w},v\rangle^{2t-2s}=(v^{\top}\Sigma v)^{t-s}(2t-2s-1)!!
.
∎

Lemma 5.8

(Moment upper bound)
.

For
t\geqslant 1
integer,

\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{11.00691pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-11.00691pt\raisebox{-6.47223pt}{\makebox[11.00691pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{2t}$}}}\hskip-11.00691pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[11.00691pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{v}$}}}$}}}\mathbb{E}\langle\bm{y},v\rangle^{2t}\leqslant\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\langle u,v\rangle^{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathbb{E}\langle\bm{\mu},u\rangle^{2t}}}\right)^{1/t}+et(v^{\top}\Sigma v)}}\right)^{t}.

Proof.

Starting with the result in
Lemma

5.7
,

\displaystyle\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{11.00691pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-11.00691pt\raisebox{-6.47223pt}{\makebox[11.00691pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{2t}$}}}\hskip-11.00691pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[11.00691pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{v}$}}}$}}}\mathbb{E}\langle\bm{y},v\rangle^{2t}

\displaystyle=\sum_{s=0}^{t}\binom{2t}{2s}\langle u,v\rangle^{2s}\mathbb{E}\langle\bm{\mu},u\rangle^{2s}(v^{\top}\Sigma v)^{t-s}(2t-2s-1)!!

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(1)}}{{\leqslant}}\sum_{s=0}^{t}\binom{2t}{2s}\langle u,v\rangle^{2s}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathbb{E}\langle\bm{\mu},u\rangle^{2t}}}\right)^{s/t}(v^{\top}\Sigma v)^{t-s}(2t-2s-1)!!

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(2)}}{{\leqslant}}\sum_{s=0}^{t}\binom{t}{s}\langle u,v\rangle^{2s}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathbb{E}\langle\bm{\mu},u\rangle^{2t}}}\right)^{s/t}(v^{\top}\Sigma v)^{t-s}(et)^{t-s}

\displaystyle=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\langle u,v\rangle^{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathbb{E}\langle\bm{\mu},u\rangle^{2t}}}\right)^{1/t}+et(v^{\top}\Sigma v)}}\right)^{t}.

In (1) we used that
s\leqslant t
and Jensen’s inequality as follows:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\langle\bm{\mu},u\rangle^{2s}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\langle\bm{\mu},u\rangle^{2t(s/t)}\leqslant\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathbb{E}\langle\bm{\mu},u\rangle^{2t}}}\right)^{s/t}.

In (2) we used that
\binom{2t}{2s}(2t-2s-1)!!\leqslant\binom{t}{s}(et)^{t-s}
for
0\leqslant s\leqslant t
integers, which is proved in
Lemma

A.28
.
∎

Lemma 5.9

(Moment lower bound)
.

For
t\geqslant 1
integer,

\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{11.00691pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-11.00691pt\raisebox{-6.47223pt}{\makebox[11.00691pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{2t}$}}}\hskip-11.00691pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[11.00691pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{v}$}}}$}}}\mathbb{E}\langle\bm{y},v\rangle^{2t}\geqslant\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\langle u,v\rangle^{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathbb{E}\langle\bm{\mu},u\rangle^{2t}}}\right)^{1/t}+p_{\mathrm{min}}^{1/t}t/2(v^{\top}\Sigma v)}}\right)^{t}.

Proof.

Starting with the result in
Lemma

5.7
,

\displaystyle\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{11.00691pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-11.00691pt\raisebox{-6.47223pt}{\makebox[11.00691pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{2t}$}}}\hskip-11.00691pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[11.00691pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{v}$}}}$}}}\mathbb{E}\langle\bm{y},v\rangle^{2t}

\displaystyle=\sum_{s=0}^{t}\binom{2t}{2s}\langle u,v\rangle^{2s}\mathbb{E}\langle\bm{\mu},u\rangle^{2s}(v^{\top}\Sigma v)^{t-s}(2t-2s-1)!!

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(1)}}{{\geqslant}}\sum_{s=0}^{t}\binom{2t}{2s}\langle u,v\rangle^{2s}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathbb{E}\langle\bm{\mu},u\rangle^{2t}}}\right)^{s/t}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(p_{\mathrm{min}}^{1/t}}}\right)^{t-s}(v^{\top}\Sigma v)^{t-s}(2t-2s-1)!!

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(2)}}{{\geqslant}}\sum_{s=0}^{t}\binom{t}{s}\langle u,v\rangle^{2s}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathbb{E}\langle\bm{\mu},u\rangle^{2t}}}\right)^{s/t}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(p_{\mathrm{min}}^{1/t}}}\right)^{t-s}(v^{\top}\Sigma v)^{t-s}(t/2)^{t-s}

\displaystyle=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\langle u,v\rangle^{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathbb{E}\langle\bm{\mu},u\rangle^{2t}}}\right)^{1/t}+p_{\mathrm{min}}^{1/t}t/2(v^{\top}\Sigma v)}}\right)^{t}.

In (1) we used that
s\leqslant t
and the fact that the
s
-norm is greater than or equal to the
t
-norm as follows:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\langle\bm{\mu},u\rangle^{2s}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{k}p_{i}\langle\mu_{i},u\rangle^{2s}=\sum_{i=1}^{k}(p_{i}^{1/s}\langle\mu_{i},u\rangle^{2})^{s}

\displaystyle\geqslant\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sum_{i=1}^{k}(p_{i}^{1/s}\langle\mu_{i},u\rangle^{2})^{t}}}\right)^{s/t}=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sum_{i=1}^{k}p_{i}^{t/s}\langle\mu_{i},u\rangle^{2t}}}\right)^{s/t}

\displaystyle\geqslant\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(p_{\mathrm{min}}^{t/s-1}\sum_{i=1}^{k}p_{i}\langle\mu_{i},u\rangle^{2t}}}\right)^{s/t}=p_{\mathrm{min}}^{1-s/t}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathbb{E}\langle\bm{\mu},u\rangle^{2t}}}\right)^{s/t}.

In (2) we used that
\binom{2t}{2s}(2t-2s-1)!!\geqslant\binom{t}{s}(t/2)^{t-s}
for
0\leqslant s\leqslant t
integers, which is proved in
Lemma

A.28
.

∎

Lemma

5.10
shows that the contribution of the means to the
\Omega(\log p_{\mathrm{min}}^{-1})
moments in direction
u
is lower bounded by
\Omega(k^{2}\sigma^{2}\log p_{\mathrm{min}}^{-1})
.
This result is used in some of the later proofs to argue that if the mean contribution is small, then
\sigma^{2}
is small, and conversely, that if
\sigma^{2}
is large, then the mean contribution is large.

Lemma 5.10
.

For
2s\geqslant\lceil\log p_{\mathrm{min}}^{-1}\rceil
integer,

(\mathbb{E}\langle\bm{\mu},u\rangle^{2s})^{1/s}\geqslant\frac{C_{sep}}{100}k^{2}\sigma^{2}\log p_{\mathrm{min}}^{-1}.

Proof.

By
Lemma

5.3
, for all
i\neq j
,
|\langle\mu_{i}-\mu_{j},u\rangle|\geqslant\sqrt{C_{sep}\sigma^{2}\log p_{\mathrm{min}}^{-1}}
. Then there exist
a,b\in[k]
such that

|\langle\mu_{a}-\mu_{b},u\rangle|\geqslant(k-1)\sqrt{C_{sep}\sigma^{2}\log p_{\mathrm{min}}^{-1}}
. Hence, there exists
a\in[k]
such that
|\langle\mu_{a},u\rangle|\geqslant\frac{k-1}{2}\sqrt{C_{sep}\sigma^{2}\log p_{\mathrm{min}}^{-1}}
. Then

(\mathbb{E}\langle\bm{\mu},u\rangle^{2s})^{1/s}\geqslant p_{\mathrm{min}}^{1/s}\max_{i}\langle\mu_{i},u\rangle^{2}\geqslant p_{\mathrm{min}}^{1/s}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{k-1}{2}}}\right)^{2}C_{sep}\sigma^{2}\log p_{\mathrm{min}}^{-1}\geqslant\frac{C_{sep}}{100}k^{2}\sigma^{2}\log p_{\mathrm{min}}^{-1},

where we used that
p_{\mathrm{min}}^{1/s}\geqslant e^{-2}
.
∎

We now state and prove the sum-of-squares identifiability proofs of
Lemma

5.11
and
Lemma

5.12
. Let
s,t=O(\log p_{\mathrm{min}}^{-1})
with
s\ll t
.
Lemma

5.11
proves that, in the case
(\mathbb{E}\langle\bm{\mu},u\rangle^{2s})^{1/s}\geqslant\Theta(s)
, if
\mathbb{E}\langle\bm{y},v\rangle^{2s}
is close to its maximum value over unit vectors
v
, then
\langle u,v\rangle^{2s}
is close to
1
.

Lemma

5.12
proves that, in the opposite case
(\mathbb{E}\langle\bm{\mu},u\rangle^{2s})^{1/s}\leqslant\Theta(s)
, if
\mathbb{E}\langle\bm{y},v\rangle^{2t}
is close to its minimum value over unit vectors
v
, then
\langle u,v\rangle^{2t}
is close to
1
.

Lemma 5.11

(Direction sum-of-squares identifiability from moment maximization)
.

Let
M\geqslant 2
. Let
s
be an integer such that
2s\geqslant\lceil\log p_{\mathrm{min}}^{-1}\rceil
. Suppose that
\mathbb{E}\langle\bm{\mu},u\rangle^{2s}\geqslant(Mes)^{s}
. Then, for
\varepsilon\leqslant\sigma^{2}/M
,

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\|v\|^{2}=1,\mathbb{E}\langle\bm{y},v\rangle^{2s}\geqslant(1-\varepsilon)\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathbb{E}\langle\bm{\mu},u\rangle^{2s}-\varepsilon}}\right)}}\right\}\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{11.76039pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-11.76039pt\raisebox{-6.47223pt}{\makebox[11.76039pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{2s}$}}}\hskip-11.76039pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[11.76039pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{v}$}}}$}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\langle u,v\rangle^{2s}\geqslant(1-4\sigma^{2}/M)^{s}}}\right\}.

Furthermore,
v=u
satisfies the axiom with
\varepsilon=0
.

Proof.

Substitute the upper bound of Lemma 3 into the axiom:

\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{11.76039pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-11.76039pt\raisebox{-6.47223pt}{\makebox[11.76039pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{2s}$}}}\hskip-11.76039pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[11.76039pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{v}$}}}$}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\langle u,v\rangle^{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathbb{E}\langle\bm{\mu},u\rangle^{2s}}}\right)^{1/s}+es(v^{\top}\Sigma v)}}\right)^{s}\geqslant(1-\varepsilon)\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathbb{E}\langle\bm{\mu},u\rangle^{2s}-\varepsilon}}\right).

Divide by
\mathbb{E}\langle\bm{\mu},u\rangle^{2s}
:

\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{11.76039pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-11.76039pt\raisebox{-6.47223pt}{\makebox[11.76039pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{2s}$}}}\hskip-11.76039pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[11.76039pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{v}$}}}$}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\langle u,v\rangle^{2}+(v^{\top}\Sigma v)\frac{es}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathbb{E}\langle\bm{\mu},u\rangle^{2s}}}\right)^{1/s}}}}\right)^{s}\geqslant(1-\varepsilon)\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1-\frac{\varepsilon}{\mathbb{E}\langle\bm{\mu},u\rangle^{2s}}}}\right).

Recall that
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathbb{E}\langle\bm{\mu},u\rangle^{2s}}}\right)^{1/s}\geqslant Mes
, and substitute the lower bound on both sides:

\displaystyle\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{11.76039pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-11.76039pt\raisebox{-6.47223pt}{\makebox[11.76039pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{2s}$}}}\hskip-11.76039pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[11.76039pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{v}$}}}$}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\langle u,v\rangle^{2}+\frac{1}{M}(v^{\top}\Sigma v)}}\right)^{s}

\displaystyle\geqslant(1-\varepsilon)\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1-\frac{\varepsilon}{(Mes)^{s}}}}\right)

Use that
v^{\top}\Sigma v=1-(1-\sigma^{2})\langle u,v\rangle^{2}
:

\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{11.00691pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-11.00691pt\raisebox{-6.47223pt}{\makebox[11.00691pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{2t}$}}}\hskip-11.00691pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[11.00691pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{v}$}}}$}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\langle u,v\rangle^{2}+\frac{1}{M}(1-(1-\sigma^{2})\langle u,v\rangle^{2})}}\right)^{s}\geqslant(1-\varepsilon)\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1-\frac{\varepsilon}{(Mes)^{s}}}}\right).

We simplify now the right-hand side. Use the loose bound
1-\varepsilon/(Mes)^{s}\geqslant 1-\varepsilon\geqslant(1-\varepsilon)^{s-1}
to obtain

\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{11.76039pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-11.76039pt\raisebox{-6.47223pt}{\makebox[11.76039pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{2s}$}}}\hskip-11.76039pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[11.76039pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{v}$}}}$}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\langle u,v\rangle^{2}+\frac{1}{M}(1-(1-\sigma^{2})\langle u,v\rangle^{2})}}\right)^{s}\geqslant(1-\varepsilon)^{s}.

Finally, apply
Lemma

A.18
with
x=\langle u,v\rangle
and
\gamma=\frac{1}{1-\varepsilon}
to obtain that

\displaystyle\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{11.76039pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-11.76039pt\raisebox{-6.47223pt}{\makebox[11.76039pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{2s}$}}}\hskip-11.76039pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[11.76039pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{v}$}}}$}}}\langle u,v\rangle^{2s}

\displaystyle\geqslant\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{M-\frac{1}{1-\varepsilon}}{\frac{1}{1-\varepsilon}}\frac{1}{M-1+\sigma^{2}}}}\right)^{s}=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{M-1-M\varepsilon}{M-1+\sigma^{2}}}}\right)^{s}

\displaystyle\geqslant\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{M-1-\sigma^{2}}{M-1+\sigma^{2}}}}\right)^{s}\geqslant\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1-4\sigma^{2}/M}}\right)^{s}.

To show that
v=u
satisfies the axiom, simply note that
Lemma

5.12
implies that
\mathbb{E}\langle\bm{y},u\rangle^{2s}\geqslant\mathbb{E}\langle\bm{\mu},u\rangle^{2s}
.

∎

Lemma 5.12

(Direction sum-of-squares identifiability from moment minimization)
.

Suppose
\sigma^{2}<0.001
.
Let
s
be an integer such that
2s\geqslant\lceil\log p_{\mathrm{min}}^{-1}\rceil
.
Suppose that
\mathbb{E}\langle\bm{\mu},u\rangle^{2s}\leqslant(100s)^{s}
.
Let
t
be an integer such that
t\geqslant 5000s
. Then, for
\varepsilon\leqslant\sigma^{2}/100
,

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\|v\|^{2}=1,\mathbb{E}\langle\bm{y},v\rangle^{2t}\leqslant(1+\varepsilon)\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathbb{E}\langle\bm{\mu},u\rangle^{2t}}}\right)^{1/t}+et\sigma^{2}}}\right)^{t}+\varepsilon}}\right)}}\right\}\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{11.00691pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-11.00691pt\raisebox{-6.47223pt}{\makebox[11.00691pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{2t}$}}}\hskip-11.00691pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[11.00691pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{v}$}}}$}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\langle u,v\rangle^{2t}\geqslant(1-20\sigma^{2})^{t}}}\right\}.

Furthemore,
v=u
satisfies the axiom with
\varepsilon=0
.

Proof.

We start by proving that, for
t\geqslant s
,
\mathbb{E}\langle\bm{\mu},u\rangle^{2t}\leqslant(100e^{2}s)^{t}
.
We have that

p_{\mathrm{min}}\cdot\max_{i}\langle\mu_{i},u\rangle^{2s}\leqslant\mathbb{E}\langle\bm{\mu},u\rangle^{2s}\leqslant(100s)^{s}.

Taking the
s
-th root and using that
p_{\mathrm{min}}^{-1/s}\leqslant e^{2}
, we obtain that
\max_{i}\langle\mu_{i},u\rangle^{2}\leqslant 100e^{2}s
.
Therefore,
\mathbb{E}\langle\bm{\mu},u\rangle^{2t}\leqslant\max_{i}\langle\mu_{i},u\rangle^{2t}=(100e^{2}s)^{t}
.

We now proceed with the main claim.
Substitute the lower bound of Lemma 4 into the axiom:

\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{11.00691pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-11.00691pt\raisebox{-6.47223pt}{\makebox[11.00691pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{2t}$}}}\hskip-11.00691pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[11.00691pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{v}$}}}$}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\langle u,v\rangle^{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathbb{E}\langle\bm{\mu},u\rangle^{2t}}}\right)^{1/t}+p_{\mathrm{min}}^{1/t}t/2(v^{\top}\Sigma v)}}\right)^{t}\leqslant(1+\varepsilon)\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathbb{E}\langle\bm{\mu},u\rangle^{2t}}}\right)^{1/t}+\sigma^{2}et}}\right)^{t}+\varepsilon}}\right).

Divide by
\mathbb{E}\langle\bm{\mu},u\rangle^{2t}
:

\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{11.00691pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-11.00691pt\raisebox{-6.47223pt}{\makebox[11.00691pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{2t}$}}}\hskip-11.00691pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[11.00691pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{v}$}}}$}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\langle u,v\rangle^{2}+(v^{\top}\Sigma v)\frac{p_{\mathrm{min}}^{1/t}t/2}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathbb{E}\langle\bm{\mu},u\rangle^{2t}}}\right)^{1/t}}}}\right)^{t}\leqslant(1+\varepsilon)\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1+\sigma^{2}\frac{et}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathbb{E}\langle\bm{\mu},u\rangle^{2t}}}\right)^{1/t}}}}\right)^{t}+\frac{\varepsilon}{\mathbb{E}\langle\bm{\mu},u\rangle^{2t}}}}\right).

Let
\Delta=\frac{p_{\mathrm{min}}^{1/t}t/2}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathbb{E}\langle\bm{\mu},u\rangle^{2t}}}\right)^{1/t}}
. Then

\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{11.00691pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-11.00691pt\raisebox{-6.47223pt}{\makebox[11.00691pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{2t}$}}}\hskip-11.00691pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[11.00691pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{v}$}}}$}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\langle u,v\rangle^{2}+\Delta(v^{\top}\Sigma v)}}\right)^{t}\leqslant(1+\varepsilon)\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1+\sigma^{2}\frac{2e}{p_{\mathrm{min}}^{1/t}}\Delta}}\right)^{t}+\frac{\varepsilon}{\mathbb{E}\langle\bm{\mu},u\rangle^{2t}}}}\right).

Note that
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathbb{E}\langle\bm{\mu},u\rangle^{2t}}}\right)^{1/t}\leqslant 100e^{2}s
and
p_{\mathrm{min}}^{1/t}=e^{-s/t}\geqslant e^{-1}
.
Then
\Delta\geqslant\frac{e^{-1}t/2}{100e^{2}s}
.
For
t\geqslant 5000s
we have then
\Delta\geqslant 10
and
p_{\mathrm{min}}^{-1/t}\leqslant 1.4
.
Then
\frac{2e}{p_{\mathrm{min}}^{1/t}}\Delta\leqslant 8\Delta
.
Then:

\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{11.00691pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-11.00691pt\raisebox{-6.47223pt}{\makebox[11.00691pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{2t}$}}}\hskip-11.00691pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[11.00691pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{v}$}}}$}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\langle u,v\rangle^{2}+\Delta(v^{\top}\Sigma v)}}\right)^{t}\leqslant(1+\varepsilon)\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1+8\Delta\sigma^{2}}}\right)^{t}+\frac{\varepsilon}{\mathbb{E}\langle\bm{\mu},u\rangle^{2t}}}}\right).

Divide by
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1+8\Delta\sigma^{2}}}\right)^{t}
:

\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{11.00691pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-11.00691pt\raisebox{-6.47223pt}{\makebox[11.00691pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{2t}$}}}\hskip-11.00691pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[11.00691pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{v}$}}}$}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{\langle u,v\rangle^{2}+\Delta(v^{\top}\Sigma v)}{1+8\Delta\sigma^{2}}}}\right)^{t}\leqslant(1+\varepsilon)\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1+\frac{\varepsilon}{\mathbb{E}\langle\bm{\mu},u\rangle^{2t}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1+8\Delta\sigma^{2}}}\right)^{t}}}}\right).

Use that
v^{\top}\Sigma v=1-(1-\sigma^{2})\langle u,v\rangle^{2}
:

\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{11.00691pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-11.00691pt\raisebox{-6.47223pt}{\makebox[11.00691pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{2t}$}}}\hskip-11.00691pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[11.00691pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{v}$}}}$}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{\langle u,v\rangle^{2}+\Delta(1-(1-\sigma^{2})\langle u,v\rangle^{2})}{1+8\Delta\sigma^{2}}}}\right)^{t}\leqslant(1+\varepsilon)\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1+\frac{\varepsilon}{\mathbb{E}\langle\bm{\mu},u\rangle^{2t}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1+8\Delta\sigma^{2}}}\right)^{t}}}}\right).

We simplify now the term involving
\varepsilon
.
Note that, by Jensen’s inequality,
\mathbb{E}\langle\bm{\mu},u\rangle^{2t}\geqslant(\mathbb{E}\langle\bm{\mu},u\rangle^{2})^{t}=(1-\sigma^{2})^{t}
. Also note that
(1-\sigma^{2})(1+8\Delta\sigma^{2})\geqslant 1
for
\sigma^{2}\leqslant 1/2
and
\Delta\geqslant 10
. Then use the loose bound

1+\frac{\varepsilon}{\mathbb{E}\langle\bm{\mu},u\rangle^{2t}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1+8\Delta\sigma^{2}}}\right)^{t}}\leqslant 1+\frac{\varepsilon}{((1-\sigma^{2})(1+8\Delta\sigma^{2}))^{t}}\leqslant 1+\varepsilon\leqslant(1+\varepsilon)^{t-1}

to obtain

\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{11.00691pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-11.00691pt\raisebox{-6.47223pt}{\makebox[11.00691pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{2t}$}}}\hskip-11.00691pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[11.00691pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{v}$}}}$}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{\langle u,v\rangle^{2}+\Delta(1-(1-\sigma^{2})\langle u,v\rangle^{2})}{1+8\Delta\sigma^{2}}}}\right)^{t}\leqslant(1+\varepsilon)^{t}.

Finally, apply
Lemma

A.19
with
x=\langle u,v\rangle
and
\gamma=\frac{1}{1+\varepsilon}
to obtain that

\displaystyle\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{11.00691pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-11.00691pt\raisebox{-6.47223pt}{\makebox[11.00691pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{2t}$}}}\hskip-11.00691pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[11.00691pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{v}$}}}$}}}\langle u,v\rangle^{2t}

\displaystyle\geqslant\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{\frac{1}{1+\varepsilon}\Delta-1}{\frac{1}{1+\varepsilon}(\Delta-1)}(1-10\sigma^{2})}}\right)^{t}=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1-\frac{\varepsilon}{\Delta-1}}}\right)(1-10\sigma^{2})}}\right)^{t}

\displaystyle=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left((1-\sigma^{2}/100)(1-10\sigma^{2})}}\right)^{t}\geqslant\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1-20\sigma^{2}}}\right)^{t}.

To show that
v=u
satisfies the axiom, simply note that
Lemma

5.11
implies that
{\mathbb{E}\langle\bm{y},u\rangle^{2t}\leqslant\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathbb{E}\langle\bm{\mu},u\rangle^{2t}}}\right)^{1/t}+et\sigma^{2}}}\right)^{t}}
.

∎

We now prove
Theorem

5.5
.

Proof of
Theorem

5.5
.

Let
s=\lceil\log p_{\mathrm{min}}^{-1}\rceil
,
t=5000s
, and
\tau=800e/(C_{sep}k^{2})
. The algorithm is:

1.

If
\sigma^{2}\geqslant\tau
, then let
M=C_{sep}k^{2}\sigma^{2}/(200e)
. Else, let
M=4
.

2.

Binary search up to resolution
\sigma^{2}/(100M)
the largest
T_{U}
in the interval
[0,(p_{\mathrm{min}}^{-1})^{s}]
such that there exists a degree-
2s
pseudo-expectation that satisfies
\{\|v\|^{2}=1,\mathbb{E}\langle\bm{y},v\rangle^{2s}\geqslant T_{U}\}
. Let
\tilde{\mathbb{E}}_{U}
be the resulting pseudo-expectation for this
T_{U}
.

3.

Binary search up to resolution
\sigma^{2}/10000
the smallest
T_{L}
in the interval
[0,(p_{\mathrm{min}}^{-1}+et)^{t}]
such that there exists a degree-
2t
pseudo-expectation that satisfies
\{\|v\|^{2}=1,\mathbb{E}\langle\bm{y},v\rangle^{2t}\leqslant T_{L}\}
.
Let
\tilde{\mathbb{E}}_{L}
be the resulting pseudo-expectation for this
T_{L}
.

4.

Return
\tilde{\mathbb{E}}_{U}
and
\tilde{\mathbb{E}}_{L}
.

We now analyze the algorithm.
To begin with, suppose that the
T_{U}
found is at least the maximum value of
\mathbb{E}\langle\bm{y},v\rangle^{2s}
and that the
T_{L}
found is at most the minimum value of
\mathbb{E}\langle\bm{y},v\rangle^{2t}
.
In this case
\tilde{\mathbb{E}}_{U}
and
\tilde{\mathbb{E}}_{L}
satisfy the axioms of
Lemma

5.11
and
Lemma

5.12
, respectively. Then our algorithm achieves the stated guarantees:

•

Suppose
\sigma^{2}\geqslant\tau
. Note that, in this case,
M=C_{sep}k^{2}\sigma^{2}/(200e)\geqslant 2
. By
Lemma

5.10
, we have that

(\mathbb{E}\langle\bm{\mu},u\rangle^{2s})^{1/s}\geqslant\frac{C_{sep}}{100}k^{2}\sigma^{2}\log p_{\mathrm{min}}^{-1}=2eM\log p_{\mathrm{min}}^{-1}\geqslant Mes.

Then the conditions of
Lemma

5.11
are satisfied, and
\tilde{\mathbb{E}}_{U}
satisfies
\langle u,v\rangle^{2s}\geqslant(1-4\sigma^{2}/M)^{s}=(1-\tau)^{s}
.

•

Suppose
\sigma^{2}<\tau
and
\mathbb{E}\langle\bm{\mu},u\rangle^{2s}\geqslant(4es)^{s}
. Note that, in this case,
M=4
. Then the conditions of
Lemma

5.11
are satisfied, and
\tilde{\mathbb{E}}_{U}
satisfies
\langle u,v\rangle^{2s}\geqslant(1-4\sigma^{2}/M)^{s}=(1-\sigma^{2})^{s}
.

•

Suppose
\sigma^{2}<0.001
and
\mathbb{E}\langle\bm{\mu},u\rangle^{2s}\leqslant(100s)^{s}
. Then the condition of
Lemma

5.12
are satisfied, and
\tilde{\mathbb{E}}_{L}
satisfies
\langle u,v\rangle^{2s}\geqslant(1-20\sigma^{2})^{s}
.

We argue now that
T_{U}
is large enough and that
T_{L}
is small enough in order for the pseudo-expectations to satisfy the axioms of the lemmas.
For that, we need

T_{U}\geqslant(1-\sigma^{2}/M)\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathbb{E}\langle\bm{\mu},u\rangle^{2s}-\sigma^{2}/M}}\right),

T_{L}\leqslant(1+\sigma^{2}/100)\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathbb{E}\langle\bm{\mu},u\rangle^{2t}}}\right)^{1/t}+\sigma^{2}et}}\right)^{t}+\sigma^{2}/100}}\right).

We prove that the intervals in which we binary search
T_{U}
and
T_{L}
contain
\mathbb{E}\langle\bm{\mu},u\rangle^{2s}
and
((\mathbb{E}\langle\bm{\mu},u\rangle^{2t})^{1/t}+\sigma^{2}et)^{t}
, respectively.
Then, binary search with the proposed resolutions is guaranteed to find
T_{U}
and
T_{L}
that satisfy the bounds stated above.

Using that
\mathbb{E}\langle\bm{\mu},u\rangle^{2}=1-\sigma^{2}
, we have that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\langle\bm{\mu},u\rangle^{2t}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{k}p_{i}\langle\mu_{i},u\rangle^{2t}=\sum_{i=1}^{k}(p_{i}^{1/t}\langle\mu_{i},u\rangle^{2})^{t}\leqslant\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sum_{i=1}^{k}p_{i}^{1/t}\langle\mu_{i},u\rangle^{2}}}\right)^{t}

\displaystyle\leqslant\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(p_{\mathrm{min}}^{1/t-1}\sum_{i=1}^{k}p_{i}\langle\mu_{i},u\rangle^{2}}}\right)^{t}=p_{\mathrm{min}}^{-(t-1)}\mathbb{E}\langle\bm{\mu},u\rangle^{2}=p_{\mathrm{min}}^{-(t-1)}(1-\sigma^{2})

\displaystyle\leqslant p_{\mathrm{min}}^{-t}

and

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\langle\bm{\mu},u\rangle^{2t}

\displaystyle\geqslant(\mathbb{E}\langle\bm{\mu},u\rangle^{2})^{t}=(1-\sigma^{2})^{t}.

Therefore,

\mathbb{E}\langle\bm{\mu},u\rangle^{2s}\in[(1-\sigma^{2})^{s},(p_{\mathrm{min}}^{-1})^{s}],

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathbb{E}\langle\bm{\mu},u\rangle^{2t}}}\right)^{1/t}+\sigma^{2}et}}\right)^{t}\in[(1-\sigma^{2})^{t},(p_{\mathrm{min}}^{-1}+et)^{t}].

Then the intervals in which we binary search are wide enough and binary search is guaranteed to succeed.

The time complexity of the algorithm is given by the number of steps in the binary search multiplied by the time to compute each of the pseudo-expectations. The number of steps in the binary search is

O\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\max\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\log((p_{\mathrm{min}}^{-1})^{s}k^{2}),\log\frac{(p_{\mathrm{min}}^{-1})^{s}}{\sigma^{2}},\log\frac{(p_{\mathrm{min}}^{-1}+et)^{t}}{\sigma^{2}}}}\right\}}}\right)=O\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\log\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}}+\log^{2}p_{\mathrm{min}}^{-1}}}\right).

For each step, we compute a pseudo-expectation of degree
O(\log p_{\mathrm{min}}^{-1})
over
d
variables, which requires time
d^{O(\log p_{\mathrm{min}}^{-1})}
.
Therefore the time complexity is

O\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\log\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}}+\log^{2}p_{\mathrm{min}}^{-1}}}\right)\cdot d^{O(\log p_{\mathrm{min}}^{-1})}=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\log\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}}}}\right)\cdot d^{O(\log p_{\mathrm{min}}^{-1})}.

∎

5.2.2
Sum-of-squares sampling (proof of Theorem
5.6
)

We state and prove
Lemma

5.13
, which is used in the proof of
Theorem

5.6
.
This lemma shows that, given a symmetric postivie definite matrix
M
correlated with a rank-
1
matrix
uu^{\top}
for a unit vector
u
, there exists an algorithm to recover a unit vector correlated with
u
.
After that, we proceed to prove the theorem.

Lemma 5.13

(Matrix rank-
1
approximation)
.

Let
0\leqslant\varepsilon<\frac{1}{8}
.
Let
u\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
be a unit vector.
Given a symmetric positive semi-definite matrix
M\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
with
\|M\|_{F}\leqslant 1
such that
\langle uu^{\top},M\rangle_{F}\geqslant 1-\varepsilon
,
there exists a polynomial-time algorithm that finds a unit vector
\hat{u}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
such that
\langle u,\hat{u}\rangle^{2}\geqslant 1-8\varepsilon
.

Proof.

The algorithm is to compute
vv^{\top}
as the best rank-
1
approximation of
M
and return
\frac{v}{\|v\|}
, which is uniquely defined up to a sign flip.

We now analyze the accuracy of the algorithm. We have that
\langle uu^{\top},M\rangle_{F}\geqslant 1-\varepsilon
, so
\|uu^{\top}-M\|_{F}^{2}\leqslant 2-2\langle uu^{\top},M\rangle_{F}\leqslant 2\varepsilon
.
For
vv^{\top}
the best rank-
1
approximation of
M
, we have then that

\|uu^{\top}-vv^{\top}\|_{F}\leqslant\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|uu^{\top}-M}}\right\|_{F}+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|M-vv^{\top}}}\right\|_{F}\leqslant 2\sqrt{2\varepsilon},

so
\|uu^{\top}-vv^{\top}\|_{F}^{2}\leqslant 8\varepsilon
. Let
\hat{u}=\frac{v}{\|v\|}
. To analyze the error of
\hat{u}
, note that

\displaystyle\|uu^{\top}-vv^{\top}\|_{F}^{2}

\displaystyle=1+\|v\|^{4}-2\|v\|^{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle uu^{\top},\hat{u}\hat{u}^{\top}}}\right\rangle_{F}

\displaystyle\geqslant 1-\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle uu^{\top},\hat{u}\hat{u}^{\top}}}\right\rangle_{F}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|uu^{\top}-\hat{u}\hat{u}^{\top}}}\right\|_{F}^{2},

where in the inequality we used that
1+x^{4}-2x^{2}y\geqslant 1-y
for
x\in\mathbb{R}
and
0\leqslant y\leqslant 1
, with
x=\|v\|
and
y=\langle uu^{\top},\hat{u}\hat{u}^{\top}\rangle
. Then
{\|uu^{\top}-\hat{u}\hat{u}^{\top}\|_{F}^{2}\leqslant 16\varepsilon}
.
Therefore,

\langle u,\hat{u}\rangle^{2}=\langle uu^{\top},\hat{u}\hat{u}^{\top}\rangle_{F}=1-\frac{1}{2}\|uu^{\top}-\hat{u}\hat{u}^{\top}\|_{F}^{2}\geqslant 1-8\varepsilon.

∎

Proof of
Theorem

5.6
.

The algorithm is to compute
M=\tilde{\mathbb{E}}vv^{\top}
, apply the algorithm from
Lemma

5.13
to
M
in order to obtain a unit vector
\hat{u}
, and return
\hat{u}
.

We now analyze the algorithm. We start by analyzing the properties of
\tilde{\mathbb{E}}
in more detail. Our first goal is to obtain the lower bound
\tilde{\mathbb{E}}\langle u,v\rangle^{2}\geqslant 1-2\varepsilon
.
We start by proving the much weaker lower bound
\tilde{\mathbb{E}}\langle u,v\rangle^{2}\geqslant 1-t\varepsilon
. Then, we use this lower bound to prove an upper bound
\tilde{\mathbb{E}}\langle u,v\rangle^{2t}\leqslant 1-t(1-\tilde{\mathbb{E}}\langle u,v\rangle^{2})/2
.
Comparing this result with the given lower bound
\tilde{\mathbb{E}}\langle u,v\rangle^{2t}\geqslant(1-\varepsilon)^{t}\geqslant 1-t\varepsilon
leads to the conclusion that
\tilde{\mathbb{E}}\langle u,v\rangle^{2}\geqslant 1-2\varepsilon
.

We proceed with the detailed proof of this fact. Recall that
\tilde{\mathbb{E}}
satisfies
\|v\|^{2}=1
and
\langle u,v\rangle^{2t}\geqslant(1-\varepsilon)^{t}
. We have that
\{\|v\|^{2}=1\}\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{8.25113pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-8.25113pt\raisebox{-6.47223pt}{\makebox[8.25113pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{2}$}}}\hskip-8.25113pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[8.25113pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{v}$}}}$}}}\{0\leqslant\langle u,v\rangle^{2}\leqslant 1\}
, where the lower bound is trivial and the upper bound is by
Lemma

A.10
. Therefore,
\tilde{\mathbb{E}}
also satisfies
0\leqslant\langle u,v\rangle^{2}\leqslant 1
.

By
Lemma

A.11
and using that
(1-\varepsilon)^{t}\geqslant 1-t\varepsilon
, we have that

\{0\leqslant\langle u,v\rangle^{2}\leqslant 1,\langle u,v\rangle^{2t}\geqslant(1-\varepsilon)^{t}\}\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{11.00691pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-11.00691pt\raisebox{-6.47223pt}{\makebox[11.00691pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{2t}$}}}\hskip-11.00691pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[11.00691pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{v}$}}}$}}}\{\langle u,v\rangle^{2}\geqslant 1-t\varepsilon\}.

By
Lemma

A.12
applied to
1-\langle u,v\rangle^{2}
with
C=\frac{1}{t^{2}\varepsilon}
, we also have that

\{1-t\varepsilon\leqslant\langle u,v\rangle^{2}\leqslant 1\}\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{11.00691pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-11.00691pt\raisebox{-6.47223pt}{\makebox[11.00691pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{2t}$}}}\hskip-11.00691pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[11.00691pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{v}$}}}$}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\langle u,v\rangle^{2t}\leqslant 1-t(1-\langle u,v\rangle^{2})/2}}\right\}.

Then

1-t\varepsilon\leqslant\tilde{\mathbb{E}}\langle u,v\rangle^{2t}\leqslant 1-t(1-\tilde{\mathbb{E}}\langle u,v\rangle^{2})/2,

so by rearranging,
\tilde{\mathbb{E}}\langle u,v\rangle^{2}\geqslant 1-2\varepsilon
.

Then
M=\tilde{\mathbb{E}}vv^{\top}
satisfies

\langle uu^{\top},M\rangle_{F}=u^{\top}Mu=\tilde{\mathbb{E}}\langle u,v\rangle^{2}\geqslant 1-2\varepsilon.

In addition,
M
is symmetric positive-definite and

\|M\|_{F}\leqslant\operatorname{Tr}(M)=\tilde{\mathbb{E}}\operatorname{Tr}(vv^{\top})=\tilde{\mathbb{E}}\|v\|^{2}=1.

Therefore,
M
satisfies the conditions of
Lemma

5.13
, and we are guaranteed that
\hat{u}
satisfies
\langle u,\hat{u}\rangle^{2}\geqslant 1-16\varepsilon
.

The given pseudo-expectation is of degree
O(t)
over
d
variables, so representing it requires
d^{O(t)}
space. Then we simply bound the time complexity by
d^{O(t)}
, which dominates the other steps of the algorithm.
∎

5.3
Finite sample bounds

In
Section

5.2
we assumed oracle access to
\mathbb{E}\bm{y}^{\otimes t}
. However, our algorithm only has access to empirical moments.
Lemma

5.14
gives a sum-of-squares proof that that the empirical moments are in fact close to the population moments. We defer the proof of the lemma to the appendix.

Lemma 5.14

(Closeness of empirical moments and population moments)
.

For
n\geqslant(p_{\mathrm{min}}^{-1}d)^{O(t)}\eta^{-2}\varepsilon^{-1}
, with probability
1-\varepsilon
,

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\|v\|^{2}=1}}\right\}\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{19.56857pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-19.56857pt\raisebox{-7.21112pt}{\makebox[19.56857pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{O(t)}$}}}\hskip-19.56857pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[19.56857pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{v}$}}}$}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\hat{\mathbb{E}}\langle\bm{y},v\rangle^{2t}\leqslant\mathbb{E}\langle\bm{y},v\rangle^{2t}+\eta}}\right\},

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\|v\|^{2}=1}}\right\}\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{19.56857pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-19.56857pt\raisebox{-7.21112pt}{\makebox[19.56857pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{O(t)}$}}}\hskip-19.56857pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[19.56857pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{v}$}}}$}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\hat{\mathbb{E}}\langle\bm{y},v\rangle^{2t}\geqslant\mathbb{E}\langle\bm{y},v\rangle^{2t}-\eta}}\right\}.

Proof.

See
Section

A.7
.
∎

5.4
Proof of Theorem
5.1

In the setting of
Theorem

5.1
we only have access to empirical moments.

Theorem

5.15
and
Theorem

5.16
adapt
Theorem

5.5
and
Theorem

5.4
to this setting, respectively.
Also recall that the goal of
Theorem

5.1
is to return a clustering, not only a unit vector close to
u
.
Toward that goal,
Theorem

5.17
shows that there exists an algorithm that, given a unit vector close to
u
, computes such a clustering.
We state and prove all of these theorems and then combine them to prove
Theorem

5.1
.

Theorem 5.15

(Finite sample equivalent of
Theorem

5.5
)
.

Let

n_{0}=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}}}}\right)^{O(1)}\cdot(p_{\mathrm{min}}^{-1}d)^{O(\log p_{\mathrm{min}}^{-1})}.

Given a sample of size
n\geqslant n_{0}
from the mixture, there exists an algorithm that runs in time
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\log\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}}}}\right)\cdot n\cdot d^{O(\log p_{\mathrm{min}}^{-1})}
that computes with high probability two peseudo-expectations
\tilde{\mathbb{E}}_{U}
and
\tilde{\mathbb{E}}_{L}
of degree
O(\log p_{\mathrm{min}}^{-1})
over a variable
v\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
such that the following holds.
Let
s=\lceil\log p_{\mathrm{min}}^{-1}\rceil
, let
t=5000s
, and let
\tau=\frac{800e}{C_{sep}k^{2}}
.
Then
\tilde{\mathbb{E}}_{U}\|v\|^{2}=1
,
\tilde{\mathbb{E}}_{L}\|v\|^{2}=1
, and:

•

If
\sigma^{2}\geqslant\tau
, then
\tilde{\mathbb{E}}_{U}\langle u,v\rangle^{2s}\geqslant(1-\tau)^{s}
.

•

If
\sigma^{2}<\tau
and
\mathbb{E}\langle\bm{\mu},u\rangle^{2s}\geqslant(4es)^{s}
, then

\tilde{\mathbb{E}}_{U}\langle u,v\rangle^{2s}\geqslant(1-\sigma^{2})^{s}
.

•

If
\sigma^{2}<0.001
and
\mathbb{E}\langle\bm{\mu},u\rangle^{2s}\leqslant(100s)^{s}
, then

\tilde{\mathbb{E}}_{L}\langle u,v\rangle^{2t}\geqslant(1-20\sigma^{2})^{t}
.

Proof.

The algorithm is the same as that in the proof of
Theorem

5.5
, except that in step (2) and step (3) of the algorithm the constraints that the pseudo-expectations are required to satisfy are
\{\|v\|^{2}=1,\hat{\mathbb{E}}\langle\bm{y},v\rangle^{2s}\geqslant T_{U}\}
and
\{\|v\|^{2}=1,\hat{\mathbb{E}}\langle\bm{y},v\rangle^{2t}\leqslant T_{L}\}
, respectively.

By
Lemma

5.14
, for
n\geqslant n_{0}
, we have that with high probability

\{\|v\|^{2}=1,\hat{\mathbb{E}}\langle\bm{y},v\rangle^{2s}\geqslant T_{U}\}\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{11.76039pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-11.76039pt\raisebox{-6.47223pt}{\makebox[11.76039pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{2s}$}}}\hskip-11.76039pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[11.76039pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{v}$}}}$}}}\{\hat{\mathbb{E}}\langle\bm{y},v\rangle^{2s}\geqslant T_{U}-\sigma^{2}/(100M)\},

\{\|v\|^{2}=1,\hat{\mathbb{E}}\langle\bm{y},v\rangle^{2t}\leqslant T_{L}\}\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{11.76039pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-11.76039pt\raisebox{-6.47223pt}{\makebox[11.76039pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{2s}$}}}\hskip-11.76039pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[11.76039pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{v}$}}}$}}}\{\hat{\mathbb{E}}\langle\bm{y},v\rangle^{2t}\leqslant T_{L}+\sigma^{2}/10000\}.

These errors, combined with the errors from the binary search resolution, are still within the amount tolerated by
Lemma

5.11
and
Lemma

5.12
, respectively, so the same guarantees hold.

The number of steps required by the binary search is the same as in
Theorem

5.5
.
For each step of the binary search, we compute a pseudo-expectation of degree
O(\log p_{\mathrm{min}}^{-1})
over
d
variables, and each constraint requires summing over the
n
samples, so the time required is
n\cdot d^{O(\log p_{\mathrm{min}}^{-1})}
.
Therefore the time complexity is

O\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\log\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}}+\log^{2}p_{\mathrm{min}}^{-1}}}\right)\cdot n\cdot d^{O(\log p_{\mathrm{min}}^{-1})}=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\log\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}}}}\right)\cdot n\cdot d^{O(\log p_{\mathrm{min}}^{-1})}.

∎

Theorem 5.16

(Finite sample equivalent of
Theorem

5.4
)
.

Let

n_{0}=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}}}}\right)^{O(1)}\cdot(p_{\mathrm{min}}^{-1}d)^{O(\log p_{\mathrm{min}}^{-1})}.

Given a sample of size
n\geqslant n_{0}
from the mixture, there exists an algorithm with time complexity
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\log\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}}}}\right)\cdot n\cdot d^{O(\log p_{\mathrm{min}}^{-1})}
that outputs with high probability a unit vector
\hat{u}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
such that
\langle u,\hat{u}\rangle^{2}=1-320\min(\sigma^{2},\frac{1}{k})
.

Proof.

The algorithm is the same as that in the proof of
Theorem

5.4
, with two exceptions:

•

In step (1) of the algorithm, we run the algorithm from
Theorem

5.15
instead of the algorithm from
Theorem

5.5
.
The pseudo-expectations
\tilde{\mathbb{E}}_{L}
and
\tilde{\mathbb{E}}_{U}
satisfy the same guarantees.

•

In step (3) of the algorithm, we check if
\hat{\mathbb{E}}\langle\bm{y},\hat{u}_{U}\rangle^{2s}\geqslant(50s)^{s}
instead of
\mathbb{E}\langle\bm{y},\hat{u}_{U}\rangle^{2s}\geqslant(50s)^{s}
.
By
Lemma

5.14
, for
n\geqslant n_{0}
, with high probability the difference between the two moments is less than
1
.
Then, if
\hat{\mathbb{E}}\langle\bm{y},\hat{u}_{U}\rangle^{2s}\geqslant(50s)^{s}
, we also have
\mathbb{E}\langle\bm{y},\hat{u}_{U}\rangle^{2s}\geqslant(50s)^{s}-1
, and if
\hat{\mathbb{E}}\langle\bm{y},\hat{u}_{U}\rangle^{2s}<(50s)^{s}
, we also have
\mathbb{E}\langle\bm{y},\hat{u}_{U}\rangle^{2s}<(50s)^{s}+1
.
It is easy to verify that the analysis in
Theorem

5.4
is still correct with these slightly weaker bounds.

Therefore, the same guarantees hold as in
Theorem

5.4
.
The time complexity of the algorithm is dominated by the time to run the algorithm from
Theorem

5.15
.

∎

Theorem 5.17

(Clustering algorithm)
.

For some
C>0
, suppose that a unit vector
\hat{u}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
is known such that
\langle u,\hat{u}\rangle^{2}\geqslant 1-C\min(\sigma^{2},\frac{1}{k})
.
Suppose that
C_{sep}/C
is larger than some universal constant.
Then, given a sample of size
n\geqslant(p_{\mathrm{min}}^{-1})^{O(1)}
from the mixture, there exists an algorithm that runs in time
n^{O(\log p_{\mathrm{min}}^{-1})}
and returns with high probability a partition of
[n]
into
k
sets
C_{1},...,C_{k}
such that, if the true clustering of the samples is
S_{1},...,S_{k}
, then there exists a permutation
\pi
of
[k]
such that

1-\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{k}|C_{i}\cap S_{\pi(i)}|\leqslant\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{p_{\mathrm{min}}}{k}}}\right)^{O(1)}.

Proof.

Our algorithm runs the algorithm from Theorem 5.1 of
[
HL18
]
with some
t=O(\log p_{\mathrm{min}}^{-1})
large enough on input samples
\langle y_{1},\hat{u}\rangle/(\sqrt{2(C+1)\sigma^{2}})
, …,
\langle y_{n},\hat{u}\rangle/(\sqrt{2(C+1)\sigma^{2}})
, and returns the clustering that this algorithm computes as an intermediate step.

We now analyze the algorithm. Note that
\langle\bm{y},\hat{u}\rangle/\sqrt{2(C+1)\sigma^{2}}
is distributed according to a one-dimensional mixture of Gaussians in which all the components have the same variance
\hat{u}^{\top}\Sigma\hat{u}/(2(C+1)\sigma^{2})
.
We have that

\hat{u}^{\top}\Sigma\hat{u}=1-(1-\sigma^{2})\langle u,\hat{u}\rangle^{2}=1-\langle u,\hat{u}\rangle^{2}+\sigma^{2}\langle u,\hat{u}\rangle^{2}\leqslant C\sigma^{2}+\sigma^{2}=(C+1)\sigma^{2}.

Therefore, the variance
\hat{u}^{\top}\Sigma\hat{u}/(2(C+1)\sigma^{2})
is upper bounded by
1/2
.
For the guarantees of the algorithm from
[
HL18
]
to hold, we further need to show that the mixture has large separation between the means of the components.
Note that the mean corresponding to
\mu_{i}
in the original mixture becomes
\langle\mu_{i},\hat{u}\rangle/\sqrt{2(C+1)\sigma^{2}}
in the new mixture.
For
i\neq j
, we have that

\displaystyle(\langle\mu_{i}-\mu_{j},\hat{u}\rangle)^{2}

\displaystyle=\langle\langle\mu_{i},u\rangle u-\langle\mu_{j},u\rangle u,\hat{u}\rangle^{2}=\langle u,\hat{u}\rangle^{2}\langle\mu_{i}-\mu_{j},u\rangle^{2}

\displaystyle\geqslant\langle u,\hat{u}\rangle^{2}C_{sep}(u^{\top}\Sigma u)\log p_{\mathrm{min}}^{-1}=\langle u,\hat{u}\rangle^{2}C_{sep}\sigma^{2}\log p_{\mathrm{min}}^{-1}

\displaystyle\geqslant\frac{C_{sep}}{2}\sigma^{2}\log p_{\mathrm{min}}^{-1}

where in the last inequality we used that
\langle u,\hat{u}\rangle^{2}\geqslant 1-C/k\geqslant 1/2
. Then

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{\langle\mu_{i}-\mu_{j},\hat{u}\rangle}{\sqrt{2(C+1)\sigma^{2}}}}}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle\geqslant\frac{C_{sep}}{4(C+1)}\log p_{\mathrm{min}}^{-1}.

For
C_{sep}/C
larger than some universal constant, the separation coefficient
C_{sep}/(4(C+1))
is large enough for the guarantees of Theorem 5.1 of
[
HL18
]
to hold meaningfully with
t=O(\log p_{\mathrm{min}}^{-1})
.
Then this algorithm computes a clustering with the stated guarantees.
The algorithm requries
n\geqslant(p_{\mathrm{min}}^{-1})^{O(1)}
and runs in time
n^{O(\log p_{\mathrm{min}}^{-1})}
.
∎

Proof of
Theorem

5.1
.

Run the algorithm from
Theorem

5.16
to obtain a unit vector
\hat{u}
that satisfies
\langle u,\hat{u}\rangle^{2}\geqslant 1-320\min(\sigma^{2},\frac{1}{k})
.
Then run the clustering algorithm from
Theorem

5.17
using this unit vector
\hat{u}
.
For
C_{sep}/320
larger than some universal constant, this algorithm is guaranteed to return a clustering with the stated guarantees.

The time complexity from
Theorem

5.16
is
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\log\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}}}}\right)\cdot n\cdot d^{O(\log p_{\mathrm{min}}^{-1})}
and the time complexity from
Theorem

6.1
is
n^{O(\log p_{\mathrm{min}}^{-1})}
.
We assume
n\geqslant\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}}}}\right)^{O(1)}\cdot(p_{\mathrm{min}}^{-1}d)^{O(\log p_{\mathrm{min}}^{-1})}
.
Therefore, the time complexity is dominated by the time to run the clustering algorithm from
Theorem

6.1
.
∎

6
Colinear means

In this section we remove the isotropic position assumption from the model in
Section

5
. Our strategy is straightfoward: we first put the mixture in isotropic position and then run the algorithm from
Theorem

5.1
.
The technical challenge is that we can only put the mixture in approximate isotropic position.
Then, we show that the guarantees of
Theorem

5.1
continue to hold with approxiamte isotropic position, albeit with a sample complexity that depends on the condition number of the covariance matrix of the mixture.

Setting.

We consider a mixture of
k
Gaussian distributions
N(\mu_{i}^{0},\Sigma^{0})
with mixing weights
p_{i}
for
i=1,...,k
,
where
\mu_{i}^{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
\Sigma^{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
is positive definite, and
p_{i}\geqslant 0
and
\sum_{i=1}^{k}p_{i}=1
.
Let
p_{\mathrm{min}}=\min_{i}p_{i}
.

The distribution also satisfies mean separation and mean colinearity:

•

Mean separation:
for some
C_{sep}>0
and for all
i\neq j
,

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\Sigma^{0}}}\right)^{-1/2}(\mu_{i}^{0}-\mu_{j}^{0})}}\right\|^{2}\geqslant C_{sep}\log p_{\mathrm{min}}^{-1}.

•

Mean colinearity:
for some vector
\mu_{base}^{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and some unit vector
u^{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and for all
i
,

\mu_{i}^{0}=\mu_{base}^{0}+\langle\mu_{i}^{0}-\mu_{base}^{0},u^{0}\rangle u^{0}.

Also define, for
\bm{y}^{0}
distributed according to the mixture,

\sigma^{2}=\frac{(u^{0})^{\top}\operatorname{cov}(\bm{y}^{0})^{-1}u^{0}}{(u^{0})^{\top}(\Sigma^{0})^{-1}u^{0}}.

As shown in
Lemma

6.3
,
\sigma^{2}
has the same interpretation as in
Section

5
: it is equal to the variance of the components in the direction of the means after an isotropic position transformation.

Theorem 6.1

(Colinear means algorithm)
.

Consider the Gaussian mixture model defined above, with
C_{sep}
larger than some universal constant. For
\bm{y}^{0}
distributed according to the mixture, let

n_{0}=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}}\cdot\|\operatorname{cov}(\bm{y}^{0})\|\cdot\|\operatorname{cov}(\bm{y}^{0})^{-1}\|}}\right)^{O(1)}\cdot(p_{\mathrm{min}}^{-1}d)^{O(\log p_{\mathrm{min}}^{-1})}.

Given a sample of size
n\geqslant n_{0}
from the mixture, there exists an algorithm that runs in time
n^{O(\log p_{\mathrm{min}}^{-1})}
and returns with high probability a partition of
[n]
into
k
sets
C_{1},...,C_{k}
such that, if the true clustering of the samples is
S_{1},...,S_{k}
, then there exists a permutation
\pi
of
[k]
such that

1-\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{k}|C_{i}\cap S_{\pi(i)}|\leqslant\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{p_{\mathrm{min}}}{k}}}\right)^{O(1)}.

We introduce some further notation for this section.
Let
\bm{y}^{0}
be distributed according to the mixture.
We specify the model as
\bm{y}^{0}=\bm{\mu}^{0}+\bm{w}^{0}
,
where
\bm{\mu}^{0}
takes value
\mu_{i}
with probability
p_{i}
and
\bm{w}^{0}\sim N(0,\Sigma^{0})
, with
\bm{\mu}^{0}
and
\bm{w}^{0}
independent of each other.

6.1
Isotropic position transformation

In this section we argue that, if we put the mixture in exact isotropic position, the conditions of
Theorem

5.1
are satisfied and we can simply run that algorithm.

Assume acces to
\mathbb{E}\bm{y}^{0}
and to an invertible matrix
W\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
such that
W\operatorname{cov}(\bm{y}^{0})W^{\top}=I_{d}
.
Then, define the random variable
\bm{y}
by the affine transformation
\bm{y}=W(\bm{y}^{0}-\mathbb{E}\bm{y}^{0})
.
The distribution of
\bm{y}
is in isotropic position: it has mean
0
and covariance matrix
I_{d}
.
Furthermore,
Lemma

6.2
shows that
\bm{y}
is a mixture of Gaussians in which the components are affine transformed versions of the original components, and that the mixture continues to satisfy mean separation and mean colinearity.
Then, the conditions of
Theorem

5.1
are satisfied. Therefore, if the original input samples are
y^{0}_{1},...,y^{0}_{n}
, we can simply run that algorithm on input samples
W(y^{0}_{1}-\mathbb{E}y^{0})
, …,
W(y^{0}_{n}-\mathbb{E}y^{0})
15
15

15

It is straightforward that if sample
y^{0}_{i}
comes from the
i
-th component in the original mixture then
W(y^{0}_{i}-\mathbb{E}y^{0})
continues to come from the
i
-th component in the affine transformed mixture.
.

We define now some variables used to state
Lemma

6.2
. Recall that
\bm{y}=W(\bm{y}^{0}-\mathbb{E}\bm{y}^{0})
. Define
\mu_{i}=W(\mu_{i}^{0}-\mathbb{E}\bm{y}^{0})
,
\Sigma=W\Sigma^{0}W^{\top}
, and
u=\frac{Wu^{0}}{\|Wu^{0}\|v}
. Also define random variables
\bm{\mu}=W(\bm{\mu}^{0}-\mathbb{E}\bm{y}^{0})
and
\bm{w}=W\bm{w}^{0}
.

Lemma 6.2

(Model after isotropic position transformation)
.

The random variable
\bm{y}
is distributed according to a mixture of
k
Gaussian distributions
N(\mu_{i},\Sigma)
with mixing weights
p_{i}
for
i=1,...,k
, where
\Sigma
is positive definite.
Alternatively, we specify the model as
\bm{y}=\bm{\mu}+\bm{w}
, with
\bm{\mu}
and
\bm{w}
independent of each other.
Furthermore, for all
i\neq j
we have mean separation

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|\Sigma^{-1/2}(\mu_{i}-\mu_{j})}}\right\|^{2}\geqslant C_{sep}\log p_{\mathrm{min}}^{-1}

and for all
i
we have mean colinearity

\mu_{i}=\langle\mu_{i},u\rangle u.

Proof.

Recall that
\bm{y}^{0}=\bm{\mu}^{0}+\bm{w}^{0}
. Then
W(\bm{y}^{0}-\mathbb{E}\bm{y}^{0})=W(\bm{\mu}^{0}-\mathbb{E}\bm{y}^{0})+W\bm{w}^{0}
, so
\bm{y}=\bm{\mu}+\bm{w}
.
Also note that
\bm{\mu}
takes value
W(\mu_{i}^{0}-\mathbb{E}\bm{y}^{0})=\mu_{i}
with probability
p_{i}
and
\bm{w}\sim N(0,W\Sigma^{0}W^{\top})=N(0,\Sigma)
.
Therefore,
\bm{y}
is distributed according to a mixture of
k
Gaussian distributions
N(\mu_{i},\Sigma)
with mixing weights
p_{i}
.

To show that
\Sigma
is positive definite, we note that for any vector
v\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
with
v\neq 0
we have that

v^{\top}\Sigma v=v^{\top}W\Sigma^{0}W^{\top}v=(W^{\top}v)^{\top}\Sigma^{0}W^{\top}v>0,

where we used that
W^{\top}v\neq 0
because
W
is invertible, after which we used that
\Sigma^{0}
is positive definite.

We prove now mean colinearity and mean separation. We start with mean colinearity. Recall that
\mu_{i}^{0}=\mu_{base}^{0}+\langle\mu_{i}^{0}-\mu_{base}^{0},u^{0}\rangle u^{0}
. Then, using that
\mathbb{E}\bm{y}^{0}=\mathbb{E}\bm{\mu}^{0}
,

\mathbb{E}\bm{y}^{0}=\mu_{base}^{0}+\langle\mathbb{E}\bm{y}^{0}-\mu_{base}^{0},u^{0}\rangle u^{0},

so

\mu_{i}=W(\mu_{i}^{0}-\mathbb{E}\bm{y}^{0})=W(\langle\mu_{i}^{0}-\mathbb{E}\bm{y}^{0},u^{0}\rangle u^{0})=\langle\mu_{i}^{0}-\mathbb{E}\bm{y}^{0},u^{0}\rangle Wu^{0}.

Then, using that
u=\frac{Wu^{0}}{\|Wu^{0}\|}
,

\displaystyle\langle\mu_{i},u\rangle u

\displaystyle=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle\langle\mu_{i}^{0}-\mathbb{E}\bm{y}^{0},u^{0}\rangle Wu^{0},\frac{Wu^{0}}{\|Wu^{0}\|}}}\right\rangle\frac{Wu^{0}}{\|Wu^{0}\|}

\displaystyle=\langle\mu_{i}^{0}-\mathbb{E}\bm{y}^{0},u^{0}\rangle\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle\frac{Wu^{0}}{\|Wu^{0}\|},\frac{Wu^{0}}{\|Wu^{0}\|}}}\right\rangle Wu^{0}

\displaystyle=\langle\mu_{i}^{0}-\mathbb{E}\bm{y}^{0},u^{0}\rangle Wu^{0}

\displaystyle=\mu_{i},

which proves mean colinearity. For mean separation, we have that

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|\Sigma^{-1/2}(\mu_{i}-\mu_{j})}}\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|(W\Sigma^{0}W^{\top})^{-1/2}W(\mu_{i}^{0}-\mu_{j}^{0})}}\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle=(\mu_{i}^{0}-\mu_{j}^{0})^{\top}W^{\top}(W\Sigma^{0}W^{\top})^{-1}W(\mu_{i}^{0}-\mu_{j}^{0})

\displaystyle=(\mu_{i}^{0}-\mu_{j}^{0})^{\top}W^{\top}(W^{\top})^{-1}(\Sigma^{0})^{-1}W^{-1}W(\mu_{i}^{0}-\mu_{j}^{0}))

\displaystyle=(\mu_{i}^{0}-\mu_{j}^{0})^{\top}(\Sigma^{0})^{-1}(\mu_{i}^{0}-\mu_{j}^{0})

\displaystyle=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|(\Sigma^{0})^{-1/2}(\mu_{i}^{0}-\mu_{j}^{0})}}\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle\geqslant C_{sep}\log p_{\mathrm{min}}^{-1}.

∎

Lemma 6.3
.

\sigma^{2}=\frac{(u^{0})^{\top}\operatorname{cov}(\bm{y}^{0})^{-1}u^{0}}{(u^{0})^{\top}(\Sigma^{0})^{-1}u^{0}}=u^{\top}\Sigma u.

Proof.

For the purposes of this proof, we define
\sigma^{2}=u^{\top}\Sigma u
as in
Section

5.1
and prove that it also matches the definition in this section.

We have, as in the proof of
Lemma

6.2
, that

\|(\Sigma^{0})^{-1/2}(\mu_{i}^{0}-\mu_{j}^{0})\|^{2}=\|\Sigma^{-1/2}(\mu_{i}-\mu_{j})\|^{2}.

For the left-hand side, we have that

\displaystyle\|(\Sigma^{0})^{-1/2}(\mu_{i}^{0}-\mu_{j}^{0})\|^{2}

\displaystyle=\|(\Sigma^{0})^{-1/2}\langle\mu_{i}^{0}-\mu_{j}^{0},u^{0}\rangle u^{0}\|^{2}

\displaystyle=\|(\Sigma^{0})^{-1/2}u^{0}\|^{2}\cdot\langle\mu_{i}^{0}-\mu_{j}^{0},u^{0}\rangle^{2}.

For the right-hand side, using from
Lemma

5.2
that
\Sigma=I_{d}-(1-\sigma^{2})uu^{\top}
, we have that

\displaystyle\|\Sigma^{-1/2}(\mu_{i}-\mu_{j})\|^{2}

\displaystyle=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(I_{d}-(1-\sigma^{2})uu^{\top}}}\right)^{-1/2}(\mu_{i}-\mu_{j}))}}\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(I_{d}-(1-\sigma^{2})\frac{(Wu^{0})(Wu^{0})^{\top}}{\|Wu^{0}\|^{2}}}}\right)^{-1/2}\langle\mu_{i}^{0}-\mu_{j}^{0},u^{0}\rangle Wu^{0}}}\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(I_{d}+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1}{\sigma}-1}}\right)\frac{(Wu^{0})(Wu^{0})^{\top}}{\|Wu^{0}\|^{2}}}}\right)Wu^{0}}}\right\|^{2}\cdot\langle\mu_{i}^{0}-\mu_{j}^{0},u^{0}\rangle^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}}\cdot\|Wu^{0}\|^{2}\cdot\langle\mu_{i}^{0}-\mu_{j}^{0},u^{0}\rangle^{2}.

Therefore

\|(\Sigma^{0})^{-1/2}u^{0}\|^{2}\cdot\langle\mu_{i}^{0}-\mu_{j}^{0},u^{0}\rangle^{2}=\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}}\cdot\|Wu^{0}\|^{2}\cdot\langle\mu_{i}^{0}-\mu_{j}^{0},u^{0}\rangle^{2},

so

\sigma^{2}=\frac{\|Wu^{0}\|^{2}}{\|(\Sigma^{0})^{-1/2}u^{0}\|^{2}}=\frac{(u^{0})^{\top}\operatorname{cov}(\bm{y}^{0})^{-1}u^{0}}{(u^{0})^{\top}(\Sigma^{0})^{-1}u^{0}},

where we used that, by
Lemma

A.27
,
W=Q\operatorname{cov}(\bm{y}^{0})^{-1/2}
for an orthogonal matrix
Q
, so
\|Wu^{0}\|=\|\operatorname{cov}(\bm{y}^{0})^{-1/2}u^{0}\|
.
∎

6.2
Finite sample isotropic position transformation

Without access to
\mathbb{E}\bm{y}^{0}
and to
W
, we apply the isotropic position transformation with
\hat{\mathbb{E}}\bm{y}^{0}
and some matrix
\hat{W}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
defined as follows.
Let the singular value decomposition of
\widehat{\operatorname{cov}}(\bm{y}^{0})
be
\hat{U}\hat{\Lambda}\hat{U}^{\top}
.
Then define
\hat{W}
and
W
as

\hat{W}=(\hat{U}^{\top}\widehat{\operatorname{cov}}(\bm{y}^{0})\hat{U})^{-1/2}\hat{U}^{\top},\quad W=(\hat{W}\operatorname{cov}(\bm{y}^{0})\hat{W}^{\top})^{-1/2}\hat{W}.

(6.1)

This choice is analogous to that in Appendix C in
[
HK13
]
.
By
Lemma

A.25
, we have that
\hat{W}\widehat{\operatorname{cov}}(\bm{y}^{0})\hat{W}^{\top}=I_{d}
and
W\operatorname{cov}(\bm{y}^{0})W^{\top}=I_{d}
.
Hence,
\hat{W}
corresponds to an isotropic position transformation for the empirical covariance matrix, and
W
to one for the population covariance matrix.
In our algorithm, we will apply the approximate isotropic position transformation to input samples
y_{1}^{0},...,y_{n}^{0}
as
\hat{W}(y_{1}^{0}-\hat{\mathbb{E}}y^{0}),...,\hat{W}(y_{n}^{0}-\hat{\mathbb{E}}y^{0})
.

6.3
Finite sample bounds

Lemma

6.4
gives a sum-of-squares proof that the empirical moments of the mixture with approximate isotropic position transformation are close to the population moments of the mixture with exact isotropic position transformation.
This lemma is supported by
Lemma

6.5
and
Lemma

6.6
, which prove that the moments do not change much due to the use of
\hat{\mathbb{E}}\bm{y}^{0}
and
\hat{W}
, respectively.

Additionally, for arbitrary unit vectors
v
,
Lemma

6.7
proves that
\langle\hat{W}(\mu_{i}^{0}-\mu_{j}^{0}),v\rangle
is close to
\langle W(\mu_{i}^{0}-\mu_{j}^{0}),v\rangle
and
Lemma

6.8
proves that
v^{\top}\hat{W}(\Sigma^{0})^{1/2}
is close to
v^{\top}W(\Sigma^{0})^{1/2}
. These facts are used in the proof of
Theorem

6.1
to argue that the clustering algorithm is correct.

Lemma 6.4

(Closeness of empirical approximate isotropic position moments and population exact isotropic position moments)
.

Let
\eta<0.001
. For

n\geqslant\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\|\operatorname{cov}(\bm{y}^{0})\|\cdot\|\operatorname{cov}(\bm{y}^{0})^{-1}\|}}\right)^{O(1)}\cdot(p_{\mathrm{min}}^{-1}d)^{O(t)}\eta^{-2}\varepsilon^{-1},

with probability
1-\varepsilon
,

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\|v\|^{2}=1}}\right\}\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{11.00691pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-11.00691pt\raisebox{-6.47223pt}{\makebox[11.00691pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{2t}$}}}\hskip-11.00691pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[11.00691pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{v}$}}}$}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\hat{\mathbb{E}}\langle\hat{W}(\bm{y}^{0}-\hat{\mathbb{E}}\bm{y}^{0}),v\rangle^{2t}\leqslant(1+\eta)\cdot\mathbb{E}\langle W(\bm{y}^{0}-\mathbb{E}\bm{y}^{0}),v\rangle^{2t}+\eta}}\right\},

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\|v\|^{2}=1}}\right\}\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{11.00691pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-11.00691pt\raisebox{-6.47223pt}{\makebox[11.00691pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{2t}$}}}\hskip-11.00691pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[11.00691pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{v}$}}}$}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\hat{\mathbb{E}}\langle\hat{W}(\bm{y}^{0}-\hat{\mathbb{E}}\bm{y}^{0}),v\rangle^{2t}\geqslant(1-\eta)\cdot\mathbb{E}\langle W(\bm{y}^{0}-\mathbb{E}\bm{y}^{0}),v\rangle^{2t}-\eta}}\right\}.

Proof.

Select
n
such that the results of
Lemma

6.5
,
Lemma

6.6
, and
Lemma

5.14
hold each with probability
1-\varepsilon/3
.
Then, overall, all three results hold with probability
1-\varepsilon
.
Then we have with probability
1-\varepsilon
that

\displaystyle\hat{\mathbb{E}}\langle\hat{W}(\bm{y}^{0}-\hat{\mathbb{E}}\bm{y}^{0}),v\rangle^{2t}

\displaystyle\quad\leqslant(1+\eta/10)\hat{\mathbb{E}}\langle\hat{W}(\bm{y}^{0}-\mathbb{E}\bm{y}^{0}),v\rangle^{2t}+\eta/10

\displaystyle\quad\leqslant(1+\eta/10)\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left((1+\eta/10)\hat{\mathbb{E}}\langle W(\bm{y}^{0}-\mathbb{E}\bm{y}^{0}),v\rangle^{2t}+\eta/10}}\right)+\eta/10

\displaystyle\quad\leqslant(1+\eta/10)\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left((1+\eta/10)\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left((1+\eta/10)\mathbb{E}\langle W(\bm{y}^{0}-\mathbb{E}\bm{y}^{0}),v\rangle^{2t}+\eta/10}}\right)+\eta/10}}\right)+\eta/10

\displaystyle\quad\leqslant(1+\eta)\mathbb{E}\langle W(\bm{y}^{0}-\mathbb{E}\bm{y}^{0}),v\rangle^{2t}+\eta.

and

\displaystyle\hat{\mathbb{E}}\langle\hat{W}(\bm{y}^{0}-\hat{\mathbb{E}}\bm{y}^{0}),v\rangle^{2t}

\displaystyle\quad\geqslant(1-\eta/10)\hat{\mathbb{E}}\langle\hat{W}(\bm{y}^{0}-\mathbb{E}\bm{y}^{0}),v\rangle^{2t}-\eta/10

\displaystyle\quad\geqslant(1-\eta/10)\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left((1-\eta/10)\hat{\mathbb{E}}\langle W(\bm{y}^{0}-\mathbb{E}\bm{y}^{0}),v\rangle^{2t}-\eta/10}}\right)-\eta/10

\displaystyle\quad\geqslant(1-\eta/10)\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left((1-\eta/10)\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left((1-\eta/10)\mathbb{E}\langle W(\bm{y}^{0}-\mathbb{E}\bm{y}^{0}),v\rangle^{2t}-\eta/10}}\right)-\eta/10}}\right)-\eta/10

\displaystyle\quad\geqslant(1-\eta)\mathbb{E}\langle W(\bm{y}^{0}-\mathbb{E}\bm{y}^{0}),v\rangle^{2t}-\eta.

∎

Lemma 6.5
.

Let
\eta<0.001
. For

n\geqslant kd\log^{2}(d/\varepsilon)\cdot\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{t\cdot\|\operatorname{cov}(\bm{y}^{0})\|\cdot\|\operatorname{cov}(\bm{y}^{0})^{-1}\|}{\eta}}}\right)^{O(1)},

with probability
1-\varepsilon
,

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\|v\|^{2}=1}}\right\}\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{11.00691pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-11.00691pt\raisebox{-6.47223pt}{\makebox[11.00691pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{2t}$}}}\hskip-11.00691pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[11.00691pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{v}$}}}$}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\hat{\mathbb{E}}\langle\hat{W}(\bm{y}^{0}-\hat{\mathbb{E}}\bm{y}^{0}),v\rangle^{2t}\leqslant(1+\eta)\cdot\hat{\mathbb{E}}\langle\hat{W}(\bm{y}^{0}-\mathbb{E}\bm{y}^{0}),v\rangle^{2t}+\eta}}\right\},

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\|v\|^{2}=1}}\right\}\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{11.00691pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-11.00691pt\raisebox{-6.47223pt}{\makebox[11.00691pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{2t}$}}}\hskip-11.00691pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[11.00691pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{v}$}}}$}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\hat{\mathbb{E}}\langle\hat{W}(\bm{y}^{0}-\hat{\mathbb{E}}\bm{y}^{0}),v\rangle^{2t}\geqslant(1-\eta)\cdot\hat{\mathbb{E}}\langle\hat{W}(\bm{y}^{0}-\mathbb{E}\bm{y}^{0}),v\rangle^{2t}-\eta}}\right\}.

Proof.

See
Section

A.8
.
∎

Lemma 6.6
.

Let
\eta<0.001
. For

n\geqslant kd\log^{2}(d)\varepsilon^{-1}\cdot\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{tp_{\mathrm{min}}^{-1}d\cdot\|\operatorname{cov}(\bm{y}^{0})\|\cdot\|\operatorname{cov}(\bm{y}^{0})^{-1}\|}{\eta}}}\right)^{O(1)},

with probability
1-\varepsilon
,

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\|v\|^{2}=1}}\right\}\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{11.00691pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-11.00691pt\raisebox{-6.47223pt}{\makebox[11.00691pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{2t}$}}}\hskip-11.00691pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[11.00691pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{v}$}}}$}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\hat{\mathbb{E}}\langle\hat{W}(\bm{y}^{0}-\mathbb{E}\bm{y}^{0}),v\rangle^{2t}\leqslant(1+\eta)\cdot\hat{\mathbb{E}}\langle W(\bm{y}^{0}-\mathbb{E}\bm{y}^{0}),v\rangle^{2t}+\eta}}\right\},

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\|v\|^{2}=1}}\right\}\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{11.00691pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-11.00691pt\raisebox{-6.47223pt}{\makebox[11.00691pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{2t}$}}}\hskip-11.00691pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[11.00691pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{v}$}}}$}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\hat{\mathbb{E}}\langle\hat{W}(\bm{y}^{0}-\mathbb{E}\bm{y}^{0}),v\rangle^{2t}\geqslant(1-\eta)\cdot\hat{\mathbb{E}}\langle W(\bm{y}^{0}-\mathbb{E}\bm{y}^{0}),v\rangle^{2t}-\eta}}\right\}.

Proof.

See
Section

A.8
.
∎

.

. Let

with probability
1-\varepsilon
, for all
i,j\in[k]
,

|\langle W(\mu_{i}^{0}-\mu_{j}^{0}),v\rangle-\langle\hat{W}(\mu_{i}^{0}-\mu_{j}^{0}),v\rangle|\leqslant\eta.

.

Lemma 6.8
.

. Let

,

.

6.4
Proof of Theorem
6.1

Proof of
Theorem

6.1
.

The first step of the algorithm is to apply the approximate istropic position transformation described in
Section

6.2
to input samples
y_{1}^{0},...,y_{n}^{0}
.
Then, the new samples are
\hat{W}(y_{1}^{0}-\hat{\mathbb{E}}y^{0}),...,\hat{W}(y_{n}^{0}-\hat{\mathbb{E}}y^{0})
.
After that, the algorithm is the same as that of
Theorem

5.1
.

We now argue that, for
n\geqslant n_{0}
, the same guarantees as in
Theorem

5.1
hold.
Recall that
Theorem

5.1
is composed of two parts:
the algorithm of
Theorem

5.16
which computes a unit vector
\hat{u}
with correlation
1-320\min(\sigma^{2},\frac{1}{k})
with
u
,
and the clustering algorithm of
Theorem

5.17
, which uses a unit vector
\hat{u}
with such correlation in order to cluster the samples.

For the algorithm of
Theorem

5.16
, we note that by
Lemma

6.4
, for
n\geqslant n_{0}
, we have sum-of-squares proofs that, for
t=O(\log p_{\mathrm{min}}^{-1})
,

\hat{\mathbb{E}}\langle\hat{W}(\bm{y}^{0}-\hat{\mathbb{E}}\bm{y}^{0}),v\rangle^{2t}\leqslant\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1+\frac{\sigma^{2}}{10000M}}}\right)\mathbb{E}\langle W(\bm{y}^{0}-\mathbb{E}\bm{y}^{0}),v\rangle^{2t}+\frac{\sigma^{2}}{10000M}

and
