\hat{\mathbb{E}}\langle\hat{W}(\bm{y}^{0}-\hat{\mathbb{E}}\bm{y}^{0}),v\rangle^{2t}\geqslant\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1-\frac{\sigma^{2}}{10000M}}}\right)\mathbb{E}\langle W(\bm{y}^{0}-\mathbb{E}\bm{y}^{0}),v\rangle^{2t}-\frac{\sigma^{2}}{10000M},

where
\hat{W}(\bm{y}^{0}-\hat{\mathbb{E}}\bm{y}^{0})
corresponds to the mixture in approximate isotropic position and
W(\bm{y}^{0}-\mathbb{E}\bm{y}^{0})
corresponds to the mixture in exact isotropic position. It is easy to verify, similarly to the analysis of the errors in the proof of
Theorem

5.16
, that these errors are tolerated by the algorithm and that it behaves as if the distribution was in exact isotropic position.

For the clustering algorithm of
Theorem

5.17
, the main issue is that the samples are colinear in direction
\frac{\hat{W}u^{0}}{\|\hat{W}u^{0}\|}
, but
\hat{u}
is guaranteed to have large correlation with
\frac{Wu^{0}}{\|Wu^{0}\|}
. We prove, nevertheless, that the algorithm has the same guarantees. First, we show that the variance of the one-dimensional components
\hat{u}^{\top}\hat{W}^{\top}\Sigma^{0}\hat{W}\hat{u}/(2(C+1)\sigma^{2})
is upper bounded by
1
, as required by the algorithm of
[
HL18
]
. We have by
Lemma

6.8
that, for
n\geqslant n_{0}
, with high probability

|\|\hat{u}^{\top}W(\Sigma^{0})^{1/2}\|-\|\hat{u}^{\top}\hat{W}(\Sigma^{0})^{1/2}\||\leqslant\|\hat{u}^{\top}W(\Sigma^{0})^{1/2}-\hat{u}^{\top}\hat{W}(\Sigma^{0})^{1/2}\|\leqslant\sigma/100,

so using that
\hat{u}^{\top}W\Sigma^{0}W^{T}\hat{u}\geqslant\sigma^{2}
,

\hat{u}^{\top}W\Sigma^{0}W^{T}\hat{u}\geqslant\frac{1}{2}\hat{u}^{\top}\hat{W}\Sigma^{0}\hat{W}^{T}\hat{u}.

Therefore, using from the proof of
Theorem

5.17
that
(\hat{u}^{\top}W^{\top}\Sigma^{0}W\hat{u})/(2(C+1)\sigma^{2})\leqslant 0.5
,

\frac{\hat{u}^{\top}\hat{W}^{\top}\Sigma^{0}\hat{W}\hat{u}}{2(C+1)\sigma^{2}}\leqslant 2\frac{\hat{u}^{\top}W^{\top}\Sigma^{0}W\hat{u}}{2(C+1)\sigma^{2}}\leqslant 1.

Second, we show that the one-dimensional means
\langle\hat{W}(\mu_{i}^{0}-\hat{\mathbb{E}}\bm{y}^{0}),\hat{u}\rangle
have large separation. We have by the proof of
Theorem

5.17
that, for
n\geqslant n_{0}
, with high probability

\langle W(\mu_{i}^{0}-\mu_{j}^{0}),\hat{u}\rangle^{2}\geqslant\frac{C_{sep}}{2}\sigma^{2}\log p_{\mathrm{min}}^{-1}.

We are interested in a similar bound with
W
changed into
\hat{W}
. By
Lemma

6.7
, for
n\geqslant n_{0}
, we have with high probability

|\langle W(\mu_{i}^{0}-\mu_{j}^{0}),\hat{u}\rangle-\langle\hat{W}(\mu_{i}^{0}-\mu_{j}^{0}),\hat{u}\rangle|\leqslant\sigma/100,

so using that
\langle W(\mu_{i}^{0}-\mu_{j}^{0}),\hat{u}\rangle^{2}\geqslant\sigma^{2}
,

\langle\hat{W}(\mu_{i}^{0}-\mu_{j}^{0}),\hat{u}\rangle^{2}\geqslant\frac{1}{2}\langle W(\mu_{i}^{0}-\mu_{j}^{0}),\hat{u}\rangle^{2}.

Therefore,

\langle\hat{W}(\mu_{i}^{0}-\mu_{j}^{0}),\hat{u}\rangle^{2}\geqslant\frac{C_{sep}}{4}\sigma^{2}\log p_{\mathrm{min}}^{-1},

so

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{\langle\hat{W}(\mu_{i}^{0}-\mu_{j}^{0}),\hat{u}\rangle}{\sqrt{2(C+1)\sigma^{2}}}}}\right)^{2}\geqslant\frac{C_{sep}}{8(C+1)}\log p_{\mathrm{min}}^{-1}.

For
C_{sep}/C
larger than some universal constant, the separation coefficient
C_{sep}/(8(C+1))
is large enough for the guarantees of Theorem 5.1 of
[
HL18
]
to hold as before with
t=O(\log p_{\mathrm{min}}^{-1})
.

Then, overall, the same guarantees as in
Theorem

5.1
hold.
∎

7
Small radius

Setting.

We consider a mixture of
k
Gaussian distributions
N(\mu_{i},\Sigma)
with mixing weights
p_{i}
for
i=1,...,k
,
where
\mu_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
\Sigma\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
is positive definite, and
p_{i}\geqslant 0
and
\sum_{i=1}^{k}p_{i}=1
.
Let
p_{\mathrm{min}}=\min_{i}p_{i}
.

The distribution also satisfies mean separation and a small radius condition:

•

Mean separation:
for some
C_{sep}>0
and for all
i\neq j
,

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|\Sigma^{-1/2}(\mu_{i}-\mu_{j})}}\right\|^{2}\geqslant C_{sep}\log p_{\mathrm{min}}^{-1}.

•

Small radius:
for some
R>0
and for all
i
,

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|\Sigma^{-1/2}\mu_{i}}}\right\|\leqslant R.

Theorem 7.1

(Small radius algorithm)
.

Consider the Gaussian mixture model defined above, with
C_{sep}
larger than some universal constant.
Let
n_{0}=(p_{\mathrm{min}}^{-1}d)^{O(R^{2}+\log p_{\mathrm{min}}^{-1})}
.
Given a sample of size
n\geqslant n_{0}
from the mixture, there exists an algorithm that runs in time
n^{O(R^{2}+\log p_{\mathrm{min}}^{-1})}
and returns with high probability a partition of
[n]
into
k
sets
C_{1},...,C_{k}
such that, if the true clustering of the samples is
S_{1},...,S_{k}
, then there exists a permutation
\pi
of
[k]
such that

1-\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{k}|C_{i}\cap S_{\pi(i)}|\leqslant\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{p_{\mathrm{min}}}{k}}}\right)^{O(1)}.

We introduce some further notation for this section.
Let
\bm{y}
be distributed according to the mixture.
We specify the model as
\bm{y}=\bm{\mu}+\bm{w}
, where
\bm{\mu}
takes value
\mu_{i}
with probability
p_{i}
and
\bm{w}\sim N(0,\Sigma)
, with
\bm{\mu}
and
\bm{w}
independent of each other.

7.1
Component covariance estimation

Lemma

7.3
gives a sum-of-squares proof that, for
t=\Omega(R^{4})
, the directional moment
\mathbb{E}\langle\bm{y},v\rangle^{2t}
approximates the
t
-th power of the variance of the components in direction
v
. This is the main ingredient of the algorithm, and it shows that the
t
-th moment of the distribution identifies within constant factors the covariance matrix of the components.
Lemma

7.2
is a simple upper bound on the means of the mixture, used in the proof of
Lemma

7.3
.

Lemma 7.2

(Bounded mean term)
.

For
t\geqslant 1
integer,

\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{11.00691pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-11.00691pt\raisebox{-6.47223pt}{\makebox[11.00691pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{2t}$}}}\hskip-11.00691pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[11.00691pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{v}$}}}$}}}\mathbb{E}\langle\bm{\mu},v\rangle^{2t}\leqslant R^{2t}(v^{\top}\Sigma v)^{t}.

Proof.

\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{11.00691pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-11.00691pt\raisebox{-6.47223pt}{\makebox[11.00691pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{2t}$}}}\hskip-11.00691pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[11.00691pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{v}$}}}$}}}\mathbb{E}\langle\bm{\mu},v\rangle^{2t}=\mathbb{E}\langle\Sigma^{1/2}\Sigma^{-1/2}\bm{\mu},v\rangle^{2t}=\mathbb{E}\langle\Sigma^{-1/2}\bm{\mu},\Sigma^{1/2}v\rangle^{2t}\leqslant\mathbb{E}\|\Sigma^{-1/2}\bm{\mu}\|^{2t}\|\Sigma^{1/2}v\|^{2t},

where in the inequality we used
Lemma

A.10
. The conclusion follows by noting that
\|\Sigma^{-1/2}\bm{\mu}\|\leqslant R
and
\|\Sigma^{1/2}v\|^{2}=v^{\top}\Sigma v
.
∎

Lemma 7.3

(Small radius component covariance estimation)
.

For
t\geqslant 4R^{2}
integer,

\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{11.00691pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-11.00691pt\raisebox{-6.47223pt}{\makebox[11.00691pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{2t}$}}}\hskip-11.00691pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[11.00691pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{v}$}}}$}}}\frac{1}{4^{t}}(v^{\top}\Sigma v)^{t}\leqslant\frac{1}{t^{t}}\mathbb{E}\langle\bm{y},v\rangle^{2t}\leqslant 4^{t}(v^{\top}\Sigma v)^{t}.

Proof.

For the upper bound, by
Lemma

4.4
and
Lemma

7.2
,

\displaystyle\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{11.00691pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-11.00691pt\raisebox{-6.47223pt}{\makebox[11.00691pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{2t}$}}}\hskip-11.00691pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[11.00691pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{v}$}}}$}}}\mathbb{E}\langle\bm{y},v\rangle^{2t}

\displaystyle\leqslant 2^{2t-1}\mathbb{E}\langle\bm{\mu},v\rangle^{2t}+2^{2t-1}(v^{\top}\Sigma v)^{t}t^{t}

\displaystyle\leqslant 2^{2t-1}(R^{2t}+t^{t})(v^{\top}\Sigma v)^{t}

\displaystyle\leqslant 4^{t}t^{t}(v^{\top}\Sigma v)^{t}.

For the lower bound, by
Lemma

4.5
and
Lemma

7.2
,

\displaystyle\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{11.00691pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-11.00691pt\raisebox{-6.47223pt}{\makebox[11.00691pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{2t}$}}}\hskip-11.00691pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[11.00691pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{v}$}}}$}}}\mathbb{E}\langle\bm{y},v\rangle^{2t}

\displaystyle\geqslant\mathbb{E}\langle\bm{\mu},v\rangle^{2t}+(v^{\top}\Sigma v)^{t}\frac{t^{t}}{2^{t}}

\displaystyle\geqslant\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(-R^{2t}+\frac{1}{2^{t}}t^{t}}}\right)(v^{\top}\Sigma v)^{t}

\displaystyle\geqslant\frac{1}{4^{t}}t^{t}(v^{\top}\Sigma v)^{t}.

∎

We note that the term
\frac{1}{t^{t}}\mathbb{E}\langle\bm{y},v\rangle^{2t}
can be rewritten as

\frac{1}{t^{t}}\mathbb{E}\langle\bm{y},v\rangle^{2t}=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle v^{\otimes t},\frac{1}{t^{t}}\mathbb{E}(\bm{y}\bm{y}^{\top})^{\otimes t}v^{\otimes t}}}\right\rangle=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle\frac{1}{t^{t}}\mathbb{E}(\bm{y}\bm{y}^{\top})^{\otimes t},(vv^{\top})^{\otimes t}}}\right\rangle.

7.2
Finite sample bounds

Lemma

7.4
gives a sum-of-squares proof that the empirical moments of the distribution are close to the population moments. We defer the proof to the appendix.

Lemma 7.4

(Closeness of empirical moments and population moments)
.

For
n\geqslant(p_{\mathrm{min}}^{-1}d)^{O(t)}\eta^{-2}\varepsilon^{-1}
, with probability
1-\varepsilon
,

\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{11.00691pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-11.00691pt\raisebox{-6.47223pt}{\makebox[11.00691pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{2t}$}}}\hskip-11.00691pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[11.00691pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{v}$}}}$}}}(1-\eta)\cdot\mathbb{E}\langle\bm{y},v\rangle^{2t}\leqslant\hat{\mathbb{E}}\langle\bm{y},v\rangle^{2t}\leqslant(1+\eta)\cdot\mathbb{E}\langle\bm{y},v\rangle^{2t}.

Proof.

See
Section

A.9
.
∎

7.3
Proof of Theorem
7.1

If the covariance matrix of the components were known, we could apply an affine transformation to the samples and change the distribution into a mixture of spherical Gaussians.
After that we could simply apply an algorithm for clustering mixtures of spherical Gaussians.

It might look like the covariance matrix approximation of
Lemma

7.3
could be used to design a sum-of-squares program that identifies this covariance matrix.
However, because
Lemma

7.3
only gives an approximation in each direction for the
t
-th power of the variance of the components, and because it is non-trivial to take
t
-th roots in sum-of-squares proofs, we found it challenging to obtain a low-degree sum-of-squares proof of identifiability for the covariance matrix.

Instead, we observe that the sum-of-squares algorithm of
[
HL18
]
for clustering mixtures of spherical Gaussians only uses as axioms upper bounds on the
t
-th moments of the distribution of the components.
It is not difficult to adapt this algorithm to work with the
t
-th power approximations that we obtain from
Lemma

7.3
.

Proof of
Theorem

7.1
.

The algorithm is:

1.

Set
t=O(R^{2}+\log p_{\mathrm{min}}^{-1})
large enough.

2.

Estimate
D=\frac{1}{t^{t}}\hat{\mathbb{E}}(\bm{y}\bm{y}^{\top})^{\otimes t}
.

3.

Apply the algorithm from Theorem 5.1 of
[
HL18
]
, but with the following moment constraint in the set of axioms instead of the original moment constraint:

\forall v\in\mathbb{R}^{d},\frac{1}{\alpha n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}w_{i}\langle y_{i}-\mu,v\rangle^{2t}\leqslant 4(8t)^{t}\langle v^{\otimes t},Dv^{\otimes t}\rangle,

where
\alpha
is a parameter and
w_{i}
and
\mu
are system variables, as in the original axioms.

4.

Return the clustering that this algorithm computes as an intermediate step.

We now analyze the algorithm.
We first discuss the new constraint.
The universal quantifier over
v\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
can be modeled by requiring that there exists a sum-of-squares proof in
v
of the constraint (see
[
FKP19
]
).
For the random variable
\Sigma^{-1/2}\bm{y}
, which is distributed according to a mixture of spherical Gaussians with covariance matrix
I_{d}
, it follows by standard arguments (see
[
HL18
]
) that, for our choice of
n
, with high probability

\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{11.00691pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-11.00691pt\raisebox{-6.47223pt}{\makebox[11.00691pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{2t}$}}}\hskip-11.00691pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[11.00691pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{v}$}}}$}}}\frac{1}{\alpha n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}w_{i}\langle\Sigma^{-1/2}(y_{i}-\mu),v\rangle^{2t}\leqslant 2(2t)^{t}\|v\|^{2t}

when
\alpha
is the fraction of samples coming from one of the components,
w_{i}
is
1
for all samples from that component and
0
for all other samples, and
\mu
is the mean of that component.
Then, by a change of variables
v\to\Sigma^{1/2}v
,

\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{11.00691pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-11.00691pt\raisebox{-6.47223pt}{\makebox[11.00691pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{2t}$}}}\hskip-11.00691pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[11.00691pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{v}$}}}$}}}\frac{1}{\alpha n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}w_{i}\langle y_{i}-\mu,v\rangle^{2t}\leqslant 2(2t)^{t}(v^{\top}\Sigma v)^{t}.

We connect now this to
D
. By
Lemma

7.4
, for our choice of
n
, with high probability

\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{11.00691pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-11.00691pt\raisebox{-6.47223pt}{\makebox[11.00691pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{2t}$}}}\hskip-11.00691pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[11.00691pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{v}$}}}$}}}\frac{1}{2}\cdot\mathbb{E}\langle\bm{y},v\rangle^{2t}\leqslant t^{t}\langle v^{\otimes t},Dv^{\otimes t}\rangle\leqslant 2\cdot\mathbb{E}\langle\bm{y},v\rangle^{2t}.

By combining this result with
Lemma

7.3
,

\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{11.00691pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-11.00691pt\raisebox{-6.47223pt}{\makebox[11.00691pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{2t}$}}}\hskip-11.00691pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[11.00691pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{v}$}}}$}}}\frac{1}{2\cdot 4^{t}}(v^{\top}\Sigma v)^{t}\leqslant\langle v^{\otimes t},Dv^{\otimes t}\rangle\leqslant 2\cdot 4^{t}\cdot(v^{\top}\Sigma v)^{t}.

Therefore,

\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{11.00691pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-11.00691pt\raisebox{-6.47223pt}{\makebox[11.00691pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{2t}$}}}\hskip-11.00691pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[11.00691pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{v}$}}}$}}}\frac{1}{\alpha n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}w_{i}\langle y_{i}-\mu,v\rangle^{2t}\leqslant 4(8t)^{t}\langle v^{\otimes t},Dv^{\otimes t}\rangle,

so the constraint is valid.

To study the guarantees of the algorithm, we show that the new constraint implies a constraint of the form required by the original algorithm, which only includes a term
\|v\|^{2t}
on the right-hand side.
We use that
\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{11.00691pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-11.00691pt\raisebox{-6.47223pt}{\makebox[11.00691pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{2t}$}}}\hskip-11.00691pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[11.00691pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{v}$}}}$}}}\langle v^{\otimes t},Dv^{\otimes t}\rangle\leqslant 2\cdot 4^{t}\cdot(v^{\top}\Sigma v)^{t}
in

\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{11.00691pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-11.00691pt\raisebox{-6.47223pt}{\makebox[11.00691pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{2t}$}}}\hskip-11.00691pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[11.00691pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{v}$}}}$}}}\frac{1}{\alpha n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}w_{i}\langle y_{i}-\mu,v\rangle^{2t}\leqslant 4(8t)^{t}\langle v^{\otimes t},Dv^{\otimes t}\rangle

to obtain

\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{11.00691pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-11.00691pt\raisebox{-6.47223pt}{\makebox[11.00691pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{2t}$}}}\hskip-11.00691pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[11.00691pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{v}$}}}$}}}\frac{1}{\alpha n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}w_{i}\langle y_{i}-\mu,v\rangle^{2t}\leqslant 8(32t)^{t}(v^{\top}\Sigma v)^{t}.

By a change of variables
v\to\Sigma^{-1/2}v
,

\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{11.00691pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-11.00691pt\raisebox{-6.47223pt}{\makebox[11.00691pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{2t}$}}}\hskip-11.00691pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[11.00691pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{v}$}}}$}}}\frac{1}{\alpha n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}w_{i}\langle\Sigma^{-1/2}(y_{i}-\mu),v\rangle^{2t}\leqslant 8(32t)^{t}\|v\|^{2t}.

Finally, by dividing both sides by
4\cdot 16^{t}
we obtain

\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{11.00691pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-11.00691pt\raisebox{-6.47223pt}{\makebox[11.00691pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{2t}$}}}\hskip-11.00691pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[11.00691pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{v}$}}}$}}}\frac{1}{\alpha n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}w_{i}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle\frac{1}{2^{1/t}\cdot 4}\Sigma^{-1/2}(y_{i}-\mu),v}}\right\rangle^{2t}\leqslant 2(2t)^{t}\|v\|^{2t}.

Then the algorithm from Theorem 5.1 of
[
HL18
]
behaves as if we had samples from
\frac{1}{2^{1/t}\cdot 4}\Sigma^{-1/2}\bm{y}
, which is distributed according to a mixture of well-separated spherical Gaussians with covariance matrix
\frac{1}{4^{1/t}\cdot 16}I_{d}
.
It is easy then to verify that we inherit the guarantees of the algorithm of
[
HL18
]
and, for
t=O(R^{2}+\log p_{\mathrm{min}}^{-1})
large enough, we return a clustering that satisfies the statement of our theorem.

The algorithm of
[
HL18
]
requires
n\geqslant(p_{\mathrm{min}}^{-1})^{O(1)}\cdot d^{O(t)}=(p_{\mathrm{min}}^{-1})^{O(1)}\cdot d^{O(R^{2}+\log p_{\mathrm{min}}^{-1})}
, so our choice of
n
is large enough to satisfy this.
The time complexity is dominated by the algorithm of
[
HL18
]
, which has a time complexity of
n^{O(t)}=n^{O(R^{2}+\log p_{\mathrm{min}}^{-1})}
.

∎

Appendix A
Appendix

A.1
Moment matching

Lemma

A.1
shows that there exists an equally-weighted discrete distribution on
k
points whose first
k
moments are equal to those of an equally-weighted mixture of
k
Gaussian distributions with variance
1
.

We give the statement of
Lemma

A.1
below.
Then we state an auxiliarly definition and lemma, and finally we prove
Lemma

A.1
.

Lemma A.1

(Moment matching)
.

For
k\in\mathbb{N}
, there exist
x_{1},...,x_{k}\in\mathbb{R}
and
y_{1},...,y_{k}\in\mathbb{R}
such that the first
k
moments of the equally-weighted discrete distribution on
\{x_{1},...,x_{k}\}
are equal to the first
k
moments of the equally-weighted mixture of Gaussian distributions
N(y_{1},1)
, …,
N(y_{k},1)
. Furthermore,
|x_{1}|,...,|x_{k}|
and
|y_{1}|,...,|y_{k}|
are upper bounded by
2^{\operatorname{poly}(k)}
.

Definition A.2

(Newton’s identities moment matrix)
.

Given
m_{1},...,m_{k}\in\mathbb{R}
, define the moment matrix
\mathcal{M}(m_{1},...,m_{k})\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times k}
as follows.
Let
p_{0}=k
and
p_{t}=k\cdot m_{t}
for all
t\in[k]
.
Also let
e_{0}=1
and

e_{t}=\frac{1}{k}\sum_{i=1}^{t}(-1)^{i-1}e_{t-i}p_{i},\quad\forall t\in[k],

p_{t}=\sum_{i=t-k}^{i-1}(-1)^{t+i-1}e_{t-i}p_{i},\quad\forall t>k.

Then
\mathcal{M}(m_{1},...,m_{k})
has entries
\mathcal{M}(m_{1},...,m_{k})_{i,j}=\frac{1}{k}p_{i+j-2}
.

Lemma A.3

(Newton’s identities moment matrix condition)
.

Given
m_{1},...,m_{k}\in\mathbb{R}
, there exist
x_{1},...,x_{k}\in\mathbb{R}
such that for all
t=1,...,k

\frac{1}{k}\sum_{i=1}^{k}x_{i}^{t}=m_{t}

if and only if
\mathcal{M}(m_{1},...,m_{k})
is positive semi-definite.

Proof.

The following technique has been used before, for example, in
[
DL67
]
.
Let
e_{i}
and
p_{i}
be as in
Definition

A.2
.
Let the degree-
k
monic polynomial
\sum_{i=0}^{k}(-1)^{i}e_{i}x^{k-i}
have roots
{x_{1},...,x_{k}\in\mathbb{C}}
.
By expanding
\prod_{i=1}^{k}(x-x_{i})
and matching coefficients, these roots satisfy for all
t=1,...,k

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}S\subseteq[k]\\
|S|=t\end{subarray}}\prod_{i\in S}x_{i}=e_{t}.

Then, by Newton’s identities
[
Mea92
]
, for all
t\in\mathbb{N}
,
\sum_{i=1}^{k}x_{i}^{t}=p_{t}
.
Note that
\frac{1}{k}p_{1},...,\frac{1}{k}p_{k}
coincide with
m_{1},...,m_{k}
.

It remains to consider whether
x_{1},...,x_{k}
are real.
It is known
[
LR12
]
that the roots
x_{1},...,x_{k}
are real if and only if their
k\times k
moment matrix is positive semi-definite.
This moment matrix is defined as the matrix
M\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times k}
with entries
M_{i,j}=\frac{1}{k}\sum_{\ell=1}^{k}x_{\ell}^{i+j-2}
, and by the fact that
\sum_{i=1}^{k}x_{i}^{t}=p_{t}
and by
Definition

A.2
it follows that this matrix coincides with
\mathcal{M}(m_{1},...,m_{k})
.
This completes the proof.
∎

Proof of
Lemma

A.1
.

Let
y_{i}=i
for all
i\in[k]
.
Denote by
m_{t,\sigma^{2}}
the
t
-th moment of the equally-weighted mixture of Guassian distributions
N(y_{1},\sigma^{2})
, …,
N(y_{k},\sigma^{2})
.
Denote
\mathcal{M}_{\sigma^{2}}=\mathcal{M}(m_{1,\sigma^{2}},...,m_{k,\sigma^{2}})
.

We start by proving that the minimum eigenvalue of
\mathcal{M}_{0}
is lower bounded by
2^{-\operatorname{poly}(k)}
. By the proof of
Lemma

A.3
,
\mathcal{M}_{0}
is the moment matrix of the equally-weighted discrete distribution on
\{y_{1},...,y_{k}\}
, so

\mathcal{M}_{0}=\frac{1}{k}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\begin{array}[]{ccccc}1&y_{1}&y_{1}^{2}&\cdots&y_{1}^{k-1}\\
1&y_{2}&y_{2}^{2}&\cdots&y_{2}^{k-1}\\
\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
1&y_{k}&y_{k}^{2}&\cdots&y_{k}^{k-1}\end{array}}}\right]^{\top}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\begin{array}[]{ccccc}1&y_{1}&y_{1}^{2}&\cdots&y_{1}^{k-1}\\
1&y_{2}&y_{2}^{2}&\cdots&y_{2}^{k-1}\\
\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
1&y_{k}&y_{k}^{2}&\cdots&y_{k}^{k-1}\end{array}}}\right].

The expression above is a Cholesky decomposition, so
\mathcal{M}_{0}
is positive semi-definite. Furthermore, the matrices in the decomposition are Vandermonde matrices with determinant
\prod_{1\leqslant i<j\leqslant k}(y_{j}-y_{i})
, for which a very weak lower bound is
1
. Then the determinant of
\mathcal{M}_{0}
is at least
\frac{1}{k^{k}}
. We also have that the trace of
\mathcal{M}_{0}
is
\frac{1}{k}\sum_{t=0}^{k-1}\sum_{i=1}^{k}y_{i}^{2t}\leqslant k^{O(k)}
, which is also an upper bound on the largest eigenvalue of
\mathcal{M}_{0}
. Then the minimum eigenvalue of
\mathcal{M}_{0}
is at least
\frac{1}{k^{k}}\cdot\frac{1}{(k^{O(k)})^{k}}=2^{-\operatorname{poly}(k)}
.

We show now that, for some
\sigma^{2}=2^{-\operatorname{poly}(k)}
small enough,
\|\mathcal{M}_{\sigma^{2}}-\mathcal{M}_{0}\|
is smaller than the minimum eigenvalue of
\mathcal{M}_{0}
. This implies that
\mathcal{M}_{\sigma^{2}}
is also positive semi-definite. An inspection of
\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}}(\mathcal{M}_{\sigma^{2}}-\mathcal{M}_{0})
shows that its entries are polynomials in
y_{1},...,y_{k}
and
\sigma^{2}
of degree
\operatorname{poly(k)}
with coefficients bounded in absolute value by
2^{\operatorname{poly}(k)}
, so the entries themselves are bounded in absolute value by
2^{\operatorname{poly}(k)}
. We have that
\|\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}}(\mathcal{M}_{\sigma^{2}}-\mathcal{M}_{0})\|
is bounded by
k
times the maximum absolute value of an entry, so this spectral norm is also bounded by
2^{\operatorname{poly}(k)}
. Then, by choosing
\sigma^{2}=2^{-\operatorname{poly}(k)}
small enough, we can ensure that
\|\mathcal{M}_{\sigma^{2}}-\mathcal{M}_{0}\|
is smaller than the minimum eigenvalue of
\mathcal{M}_{0}
.

Therefore
\mathcal{M}_{\sigma^{2}}
is positive semi-definite. Then, by
Lemma

A.3
, there exist
x_{1},...,x_{k}\in\mathbb{R}
such that the first
k
moments of the equally-weighted discrete distribution on
\{x_{1},...,x_{k}\}
are equal to the first
k
moments of the equally-weighted mixture of Gaussian distributions
N(y_{1},\sigma^{2})
, …,
N(y_{k},\sigma^{2})
. We note that
|x_{1}|,...,|x_{k}|
must be bounded by
\operatorname{poly}(k)
, otherwise the second moments of the discrete distribution would be larger than those of the mixture of Gaussian distributions. Finally, by scaling, the desired moment matching holds for
\frac{1}{\sigma}x_{1},...,\frac{1}{\sigma}x_{k}
and
\frac{1}{\sigma}y_{1},...,\frac{1}{\sigma}y_{k}
. These values clearly satisfy the stated upper bound.
∎

A.2
Sum-of-squares lemmas

We first prove a number of useful SOS facts.

Lemma A.4

(Restatement of Lemma A.1 in
[
KS17
]
)
.

For variables
X_{1},...,X_{t}\in\mathbb{R}
,

\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{11.10205pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-11.10205pt\raisebox{-6.26668pt}{\makebox[11.10205pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{t}$}}}\hskip-11.10205pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[11.10205pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{X}$}}}$}}}X_{1}\cdot...\cdot X_{t}\leqslant\frac{1}{t}(X_{1}^{t}+...+X_{t}^{t}).

Lemma A.5

(Restatement of Lemma A.2 in
[
KS17
]
)
.

For variables
A,B\in\mathbb{R}
and
t\geqslant 2
even,

\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{18.66075pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-18.66075pt\raisebox{-6.26668pt}{\makebox[18.66075pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{t}$}}}\hskip-18.66075pt\raisebox{3.32222pt}{\makebox[18.66075pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{A,B}$}}}$}}}(A+B)^{t}\leqslant 2^{t-1}A^{t}+2^{t-1}B^{t}.

Lemma A.6
.

For variables
A,B\in\mathbb{R}
and
\delta>0
and
t\geqslant 2
even,

\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{18.66075pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-18.66075pt\raisebox{-6.26668pt}{\makebox[18.66075pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{t}$}}}\hskip-18.66075pt\raisebox{3.32222pt}{\makebox[18.66075pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{A,B}$}}}$}}}(A+B)^{t}\leqslant(1+\delta)^{t-1}A^{t}+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1+\frac{1}{\delta}}}\right)^{t-1}B^{t}.

Proof.

\displaystyle\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{18.66075pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-18.66075pt\raisebox{-6.26668pt}{\makebox[18.66075pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{t}$}}}\hskip-18.66075pt\raisebox{3.32222pt}{\makebox[18.66075pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{A,B}$}}}$}}}(A+B)^{t}

\displaystyle=\sum_{s=0}^{t}\binom{t}{s}A^{s}B^{t-s}

\displaystyle=\sum_{s=0}^{t}\binom{t}{s}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\delta^{1-s/t}A}}\right)^{s}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1}{\delta^{s/t}}B}}\right)^{t-s}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(1)}}{{\leqslant}}\sum_{s=0}^{t}\binom{t}{s}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{s}{t}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\delta^{1-s/t}A}}\right)^{t}+\frac{t-s}{s}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1}{\delta^{s/t}}B}}\right)^{t}}}\right)

\displaystyle=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sum_{s=0}^{t}\binom{t}{s}\frac{s}{t}\delta^{t-s}}}\right)A^{t}+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sum_{s=0}^{t}\binom{t}{s}\frac{t-s}{t}\frac{1}{\delta^{s}}}}\right)B^{t}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(2)}}{{=}}(1+\delta)^{t-1}A^{t}+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1+\frac{1}{\delta}}}\right)^{t-1}B^{t}

where in (1) we used
Lemma

A.4
and in (2) we used the identities

\sum_{s=0}^{t}\binom{t}{s}\frac{s}{t}x^{t-s}=\sum_{s=0}^{t-1}\binom{t-1}{s}x^{t-1-s}=(1+x)^{t-1}

and

\sum_{s=0}^{t}\binom{t}{s}\frac{t-s}{t}x^{s}=\sum_{s=0}^{t-1}\binom{t-1}{s}x^{s}=(1+x)^{t-1}.

∎

Lemma A.7
.

For variable
X\in\mathbb{R}
and
t\geqslant 0
integer,

\{X\geqslant 0\}\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{11.10205pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-11.10205pt\raisebox{-6.26668pt}{\makebox[11.10205pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{t}$}}}\hskip-11.10205pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[11.10205pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{X}$}}}$}}}\{X^{t}\geqslant 0\}.

Proof.

For
t
even,
\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{11.10205pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-11.10205pt\raisebox{-6.26668pt}{\makebox[11.10205pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{t}$}}}\hskip-11.10205pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[11.10205pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{X}$}}}$}}}X^{t}\geqslant 0
is trivial. For
t
odd, we have that

\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{11.10205pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-11.10205pt\raisebox{-6.26668pt}{\makebox[11.10205pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{t}$}}}\hskip-11.10205pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[11.10205pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{X}$}}}$}}}X^{t}=X^{t-1}X\geqslant 0
, where we used that
\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{13.70137pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-13.70137pt\raisebox{-6.47223pt}{\makebox[13.70137pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{t-1}$}}}\hskip-13.70137pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[13.70137pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{X}$}}}$}}}X^{t-1}\geqslant 0
because
t-1
is even.
∎

Lemma A.8
.

For variable
X\in\mathbb{R}
and
t\geqslant 1
integer,

\{0\leqslant X\leqslant 1\}\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{11.10205pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-11.10205pt\raisebox{-6.26668pt}{\makebox[11.10205pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{t}$}}}\hskip-11.10205pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[11.10205pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{X}$}}}$}}}\{X^{t}\leqslant 1\}.

Proof.

We have
\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{11.10205pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-11.10205pt\raisebox{-6.74446pt}{\makebox[11.10205pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{X}$}}}\hskip-11.10205pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[11.10205pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{t}$}}}$}}}1-X^{t}=(1-X)(1+X+...+X^{t-1})\geqslant 0
, where we used that, by
Lemma

A.7
,
\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{11.10205pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-11.10205pt\raisebox{-6.59305pt}{\makebox[11.10205pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{i}$}}}\hskip-11.10205pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[11.10205pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{X}$}}}$}}}X^{i}\geqslant 0
for
i\in\{0,...,t-1\}
.
∎

Lemma A.9

(Restatement of Lemma A.3 in
[
KS17
]
)
.

For variable
X\in\mathbb{R}
and
t\geqslant 2
even,

\{X^{t}\leqslant 1\}\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{11.10205pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-11.10205pt\raisebox{-6.26668pt}{\makebox[11.10205pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{t}$}}}\hskip-11.10205pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[11.10205pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{X}$}}}$}}}\{X\leqslant 1\}.

Lemma A.10
.

For variables
u,v\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,

\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{15.24309pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-15.24309pt\raisebox{-6.47223pt}{\makebox[15.24309pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{2}$}}}\hskip-15.24309pt\raisebox{3.32222pt}{\makebox[15.24309pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{u,v}$}}}$}}}\langle u,v\rangle^{2}\leqslant\|u\|^{2}\cdot\|v\|^{2}.

Proof.

By Lagrange’s identity,

\langle u,v\rangle^{2}=\|u\|^{2}\cdot\|v\|^{2}+\sum_{i=1}^{d-1}\sum_{j=i+1}^{d}(u_{i}v_{j}-u_{j}v_{j})^{2},

so

\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{15.24309pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-15.24309pt\raisebox{-6.47223pt}{\makebox[15.24309pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{2}$}}}\hskip-15.24309pt\raisebox{3.32222pt}{\makebox[15.24309pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{u,v}$}}}$}}}\langle u,v\rangle^{2}\leqslant\|u\|^{2}\cdot\|v\|^{2}.

∎

Lemma A.11
.

For variable
X\in\mathbb{R}
and
\delta\in\mathbb{R}
and
t\geqslant 1
integer,

\{0\leqslant X\leqslant 1,X^{t}\geqslant\delta\}\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{11.10205pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-11.10205pt\raisebox{-6.26668pt}{\makebox[11.10205pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{t}$}}}\hskip-11.10205pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[11.10205pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{X}$}}}$}}}\{X\geqslant\delta\}.

Proof.

We have
\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{11.10205pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-11.10205pt\raisebox{-6.26668pt}{\makebox[11.10205pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{t}$}}}\hskip-11.10205pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[11.10205pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{X}$}}}$}}}X=(X^{t}-\delta)+(1-X)(1+X+...+X^{t-1})+\delta\geqslant\delta
, where we used that, by
Lemma

A.7
,
\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{11.10205pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-11.10205pt\raisebox{-6.59305pt}{\makebox[11.10205pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{i}$}}}\hskip-11.10205pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[11.10205pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{X}$}}}$}}}X^{i}\geqslant 0
for
i\in\{0,...,t-1\}
.
∎

Lemma A.12
.

For variable
X\in\mathbb{R}
and
C\geqslant 2
and
t\geqslant 1
integer,

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{0\leqslant X\leqslant\frac{1}{Ct}}}\right\}\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{11.10205pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-11.10205pt\raisebox{-6.26668pt}{\makebox[11.10205pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{t}$}}}\hskip-11.10205pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[11.10205pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{X}$}}}$}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{(1-X)^{t}\leqslant 1-\frac{C-2}{C-1}tX}}\right\}.

Proof.

We have that

\displaystyle\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{11.10205pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-11.10205pt\raisebox{-6.26668pt}{\makebox[11.10205pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{t}$}}}\hskip-11.10205pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[11.10205pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{X}$}}}$}}}(1-X)^{t}

\displaystyle=1-tX+\sum_{i=2}^{t}\binom{t}{i}(-1)^{i}X^{i}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(1)}}{{\leqslant}}1-tX+\sum_{i=2}^{t}\binom{t}{i}X^{i}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(2)}}{{\leqslant}}1-tX+\sum_{i=2}^{t}t^{i}X^{i}=1-tX+tX\sum_{i=1}^{t-1}t^{i}X^{i}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(3)}}{{\leqslant}}1-tX+tX\sum_{i=1}^{t-1}\frac{1}{C^{i}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(4)}}{{\leqslant}}1-tX+\frac{1}{C-1}tX

\displaystyle=1-\frac{C-2}{C-1}tX.

We use throughout that, by
Lemma

A.7
,
\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{11.10205pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-11.10205pt\raisebox{-6.59305pt}{\makebox[11.10205pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{i}$}}}\hskip-11.10205pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[11.10205pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{X}$}}}$}}}X^{i}\geqslant 0
for
i\in\{0,...,t\}
. In (1) we used that
\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{11.10205pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-11.10205pt\raisebox{-6.59305pt}{\makebox[11.10205pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{i}$}}}\hskip-11.10205pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[11.10205pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{X}$}}}$}}}-X^{i}\leqslant X^{i}
. In (2) we used that
\binom{t}{i}\leqslant t^{i}
. In (3) we used that
\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{11.10205pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-11.10205pt\raisebox{-6.47223pt}{\makebox[11.10205pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{1}$}}}\hskip-11.10205pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[11.10205pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{X}$}}}$}}}0\leqslant X\leqslant\frac{1}{Ct}
implies that
\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{16.95427pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-16.95427pt\raisebox{-6.59305pt}{\makebox[16.95427pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{i+1}$}}}\hskip-16.95427pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[16.95427pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{X}$}}}$}}}0\leqslant X^{i+1}\leqslant\frac{1}{(Ct)^{i}}X
. The upper bound is true because

\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{16.95427pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-16.95427pt\raisebox{-6.59305pt}{\makebox[16.95427pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{i+1}$}}}\hskip-16.95427pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[16.95427pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{X}$}}}$}}}\frac{1}{(Ct)^{i}}X-X^{i+1}=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1}{Ct}-X}}\right)\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sum_{j=0}^{i-1}\frac{1}{(Ct)^{j}}X^{i-1-j}}}\right)X\geqslant 0.

In (4) we used that
\sum_{i=1}^{t-1}\frac{1}{C^{i}}\leqslant\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{C^{i}}=\frac{1}{1-\frac{1}{C}}-1=\frac{1}{C-1}
.
∎

Lemma A.13

(Restatement of Claim 1.5 in
[
RSS18
]
)
.

If
\tilde{\mathbb{E}}
is a degree-
d
pseudo-expectation and if
p,q
are polynomials of degree at most
\frac{d}{2}
, then
\tilde{\mathbb{E}}[q(x)\cdot p(x)]\leqslant\frac{1}{2}\tilde{\mathbb{E}}[q(x)^{2}]+\frac{1}{2}\tilde{\mathbb{E}}[p(x)^{2}]
.

Lemma A.14
.

If
\tilde{\mathbb{E}}
is a degree-
d
pseudo-expectation and if
p
is a polynomial of degree at most
\frac{d}{2}
, then
(\tilde{\mathbb{E}}[p(x)])^{2}\leqslant\tilde{\mathbb{E}}[p(x)^{2}]
.

Proof.

Let
\tilde{\mathbb{E}}_{x}
be the given pseudo-expectation over
x
, and let
\tilde{\mathbb{E}}_{x^{\prime}}
be a copy of the given pseudo-expectaiton but over
x^{\prime}
instead of
x
. Then we have

(\tilde{\mathbb{E}}_{x}[p(x)])^{2}=(\tilde{\mathbb{E}}_{x}[p(x)])(\tilde{\mathbb{E}}_{x^{\prime}}[p(x^{\prime})])=\tilde{\mathbb{E}}_{x,x^{\prime}}[p(x)p(x^{\prime})].

Then, by
Lemma

A.13
,

(\tilde{\mathbb{E}}_{x}[p(x)])^{2}\leqslant\frac{1}{2}\tilde{\mathbb{E}}_{x,x^{\prime}}[p(x)^{2}]+\frac{1}{2}\tilde{\mathbb{E}}_{x,x^{\prime}}[p(x^{\prime})^{2}]=\tilde{\mathbb{E}}_{x}[p(x)^{2}].

∎

Lemma A.15

(Restatement of Lemma 4.5 in
[
BS14
]
)
.

If
\tilde{\mathbb{E}}
is a degree-
d
pseudo-expectation over vectors
u
,
v
, then

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\tilde{\mathbb{E}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lVert u+v}}\right\rVert_{d}^{d}}}\right)^{1/d}\leqslant\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\tilde{\mathbb{E}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lVert u}}\right\rVert_{d}^{d}}}\right)^{1/d}+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\tilde{\mathbb{E}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lVert v}}\right\rVert_{d}^{d}}}\right)^{1/d}.

We give now some sum-of-squares proofs that are more specific to our setting.
The purpose of
Lemma

A.16
and
Lemma

A.17
is to aid in transforming some sum-of-squares proofs about polynomials
p(x)
and
q(x)
into sum-of-squares proofs about polynomials
p(x)^{t}
and
q(x)^{t}
.

Lemma

A.16
shows that, under some conditions, if
\{p(x)\geqslant 1\}\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{8.53467pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-8.53467pt\raisebox{-1.96112pt}{\makebox[8.53467pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\hskip-8.53467pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[8.53467pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{x}$}}}$}}}\{q(x)\geqslant 1\}
, then also
\{p(x)^{t}\geqslant 1\}\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{8.53467pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-8.53467pt\raisebox{-1.96112pt}{\makebox[8.53467pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\hskip-8.53467pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[8.53467pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{x}$}}}$}}}\{q(x)^{t}\geqslant 1\}
,
while
Lemma

A.17
shows that, again under some conditions, if
\{p(x)\leqslant 1\}\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{8.53467pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-8.53467pt\raisebox{-1.96112pt}{\makebox[8.53467pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\hskip-8.53467pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[8.53467pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{x}$}}}$}}}\{q(x)\geqslant 1\}
, then also
\{p(x)^{t}\leqslant 1\}\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{8.53467pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-8.53467pt\raisebox{-1.96112pt}{\makebox[8.53467pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\hskip-8.53467pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[8.53467pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{x}$}}}$}}}\{q(x)^{t}\geqslant 1\}
.
These are used in
Lemma

A.18
and
Lemma

A.19
, which implement sum-of-squares proofs with some polynomials raised to the
t
-th power.

Lemma A.16
.

Let
p,q:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
with
p(x)\geqslant 0
for all
x\in\mathbb{R}
.
Let
\gamma>1
be a real number and
t\geqslant 2
be an even integer.
Suppose that, for all
x\in\mathbb{R}
,
q(x)-1-\gamma(p(x)-1)\geqslant 0
.
Then, for all
x\in\mathbb{R}
,

q(x)^{t}-1-\gamma(p(x)^{t}-1)\geqslant 0.

Proof.

We consider two cases.
First, suppose that
1+\gamma(p(x)-1)<0
.
This implies that
p(x)<1-\frac{1}{\gamma}
, which implies that
1+\gamma(p(x)^{t}-1)<1+\gamma(p(x)-1)<0
.
Therefore
q(x)^{t}\geqslant 1+\gamma(p(x)^{t}-1)
is satisfied trivially for
t
even.

Second, suppose that
1+\gamma(p(x)-1)\geqslant 0
.
Then the given assumption implies that
{q(x)^{t}\geqslant\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1+\gamma(p(x)-1)}}\right)^{t}}
.
Then

q(x)^{t}-1-\gamma(p(x)^{t}-1)\geqslant\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1+\gamma(p(x)-1)}}\right)^{t}-1-\gamma(p(x)^{t}-1).

To show that the expression on the right-hand side is non-negative, it suffices to show that

f(x)=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1+\gamma(x-1)}}\right)^{t}-1-\gamma(x^{t}-1)

is non-negative everywhere.
For
\gamma>1
, we have that
\lim_{x\to-\infty}f(x)=\infty
and
\lim_{x\to\infty}f(x)=\infty
.
Then, it suffices to show that
f(x)
is non-negative at all its critical points.
We have

\frac{d}{dx}f(x)=\gamma t(\gamma(x-1)+1)^{t-1}-\gamma tx^{t-1},

so

\frac{d}{dx}f(x)=0\Longleftrightarrow\gamma(x-1)+1=x\Longleftrightarrow x=1.

We have
f(1)=0\geqslant 0
.
Therefore,
f(x)\geqslant 0
for all
x\in\mathbb{R}
.

∎

Lemma A.17
.

Let
p,q:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
for all
x\in\mathbb{R}
.
Let
\gamma>0
be a real number and
t\geqslant 2
be an even integer.
Suppose that, for all
x\in\mathbb{R}
,
q(x)-1-\gamma(1-p(x))\geqslant 0
.
Then, for all
x\in\mathbb{R}
,

q(x)^{t}-1-\gamma(1-p(x)^{t})\geqslant 0.

Proof.

We consider two cases.
First, suppose that
1+\gamma(1-p(x))<0
.
This implies that
p(x)>1+\frac{1}{\gamma}
, which implies that
1+\gamma(1-p(x)^{t})<1+\gamma(1-p(x))<0
.
Therefore
q(x)^{t}\geqslant 1+\gamma(1-p(x)^{t})
is satisfied trivially for
t
even.

Second, suppose that
1+\gamma(1-p(x))\geqslant 0
.
Then the given assumption implies that
{q(x)^{t}\geqslant\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1+\gamma(1-p(x))}}\right)^{t}}
.
Then

q(x)^{t}-1-\gamma(1-p(x)^{t})\geqslant\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1+\gamma(1-p(x))}}\right)^{t}-1-\gamma(1-p(x)^{t}).

To show that the expression on the right-hand side, it suffices to show that

f(x)=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1+\gamma(1-x)}}\right)^{t}-1-\gamma(1-x^{t})

is non-negative everywhere.
For
\gamma>0
, we have that
\lim_{x\to-\infty}f(x)=\infty
and
\lim_{x\to\infty}f(x)=\infty
.
Then, it suffices to show that
f(x)
is non-negative at all its critical points.
We have

\frac{d}{dx}f(x)=\gamma tx^{t-1}-\gamma t(\gamma(1-x)+1)^{t-1},

so

\frac{d}{dx}f(x)=0\Longleftrightarrow x=\gamma(1-x)+1\Longleftrightarrow x=1.

We have
f(1)=0\geqslant 0
.
Therefore,
f(x)\geqslant 0
for all
x\in\mathbb{R}
.

∎

Lemma

A.18
, which is used in
Lemma

5.11
, provides a sum-of-squares proof that if
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(x^{2}+\frac{1}{M}(1-(1-\sigma^{2})x^{2})}}\right)^{t}\geqslant\frac{1}{\gamma^{t}}
, then
x^{2t}\geqslant\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{M-\gamma}{\gamma}\frac{1}{M-1+\sigma^{2}}}}\right)^{t}
.

Lemma A.18
.

For a variable
x\in\mathbb{R}
and for
0\leqslant\sigma^{2}<1
and
0<\gamma<M
and
M\geqslant 2
, we have that

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\gamma\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(x^{2}+\frac{1}{M}(1-(1-\sigma^{2})x^{2})}}\right)}}\right)^{t}\geqslant 1}}\right\}\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{11.00691pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-11.00691pt\raisebox{-6.47223pt}{\makebox[11.00691pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{2t}$}}}\hskip-11.00691pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[11.00691pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{x}$}}}$}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{\gamma}{M-\gamma}(M-1+\sigma^{2})x^{2}}}\right)^{t}\geqslant 1}}\right\}.

Proof.

Let

p(x)=\gamma\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(x^{2}+\frac{1}{M}(1-(1-\sigma^{2})x^{2})}}\right)=\gamma\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{M-1+\sigma^{2}}{M}x^{2}+\frac{1}{M}}}\right)

and

q(x)=\delta(M-1+\sigma^{2})x^{2},

for some
\delta>0
to be determined later.
Note that
p(x)\geqslant 0
for all
x\in\mathbb{R}
.

We check now that, for all
x\in\mathbb{R}
,

q(x)-1-\frac{M\delta}{\gamma}(p(x)-1)\geqslant 0,

which corresponds to a sum-of-squares proof that
\{p(x)\geqslant 1\}\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{8.53467pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-8.53467pt\raisebox{-1.96112pt}{\makebox[8.53467pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\hskip-8.53467pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[8.53467pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{x}$}}}$}}}\{q(x)\geqslant 1\}
.
We note that the coefficient
\frac{M\delta}{\gamma}
was chosen such that
x^{2}
cancels.
We have then

\displaystyle q(x)-1-\frac{M\delta}{\gamma}(p(x)-1)=-1-\frac{M\delta}{\gamma}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{\gamma}{M}-1}}\right)=\frac{M\delta}{\gamma}-1-\delta.

Set
\delta=\frac{\gamma}{M-\gamma}
, which makes the term equal to
0
. Therefore, for all
x\in\mathbb{R}
,

q(x)-1-\frac{2\frac{\gamma}{M-\gamma}}{\gamma}(p(x)-1)\geqslant 0.

Therefore, by
Lemma

A.16
, for all
x\in\mathbb{R}
,

f(x)=q(x)^{t}-1-\frac{2\frac{\gamma}{M-\gamma}}{\gamma}(p(x)^{t}-1)\geqslant 0.

Because
f(x)
is a univariate polynomial of degree
2t
, there also exists a sum-of-squares proof of degree at most
2t
that
f(x)\geqslant 0
.
Note that this constitutes a degree-
2t
sum-of-squares proof that
\{p(x)^{t}\geqslant 1\}\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{8.53467pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-8.53467pt\raisebox{-1.96112pt}{\makebox[8.53467pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\hskip-8.53467pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[8.53467pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{x}$}}}$}}}\{q(x)^{t}\geqslant 1\}
.
This concludes the proof.

∎

Lemma

A.19
, which is used in
Lemma

5.12
, provides a sum-of-squares proof that if
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{x^{2}+\Delta(1-(1-\sigma^{2})x^{2})}{1+8\Delta\sigma^{2}}}}\right)^{t}\leqslant\frac{1}{\gamma^{t}}
, then
x^{2t}\geqslant\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{\gamma\Delta-1}{\gamma(\Delta-1)}(1-10\sigma^{2})}}\right)^{t}
.

Lemma A.19
.

For a variable
x\in\mathbb{R}
and for
0\leqslant\sigma^{2}<0.1
and
\Delta\geqslant 10
and
t
even and
\gamma\geqslant 0.9
, we have that

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\gamma\frac{x^{2}+\Delta(1-(1-\sigma^{2})x^{2})}{1+8\Delta\sigma^{2}}}}\right)^{t}\leqslant 1}}\right\}\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{11.00691pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-11.00691pt\raisebox{-6.47223pt}{\makebox[11.00691pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{2t}$}}}\hskip-11.00691pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[11.00691pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{x}$}}}$}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{\gamma(\Delta-1)}{\gamma\Delta-1}\frac{x^{2}}{1-10\sigma^{2}}}}\right)^{t}\geqslant 1}}\right\}.

Proof.

Note that we need
\sigma^{2}<0.1
in order to have
1-10\sigma^{2}>0
.

Let

p(x)=\gamma\frac{x^{2}+\Delta(1-(1-\sigma^{2})x^{2})}{1+8\Delta\sigma^{2}}=\gamma\frac{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1-\Delta(1-\sigma^{2})}}\right)x^{2}+\Delta}{1+8\Delta\sigma^{2}}

and

q(x)=\delta\frac{x^{2}}{1-10\sigma^{2}},

for some
\delta>0
to be determined later.

We check now that, for all
x\in\mathbb{R}
,

q(x)-1-\frac{\delta(1+8\Delta\sigma^{2})}{\gamma(\Delta(1-\sigma^{2})-1)(1-10\sigma^{2})}(1-p(x))\geqslant 0,

which corresponds to a sum-of-squares proof that
\{p(x)\leqslant 1\}\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{8.53467pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-8.53467pt\raisebox{-1.96112pt}{\makebox[8.53467pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\hskip-8.53467pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[8.53467pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{x}$}}}$}}}\{q(x)\geqslant 1\}
.
We note that the coefficient
\frac{\delta(1+8\Delta\sigma^{2})}{\gamma(\Delta(1-\sigma^{2})-1)(1-10\sigma^{2})}
was chosen such that
x^{2}
cancels.
We have then

\displaystyle q(x)-1-\frac{\delta(1+8\Delta\sigma^{2})}{\gamma(\Delta(1-\sigma^{2})-1)(1-10\sigma^{2})}(1-p(x))

\displaystyle\quad=-1-\frac{\delta(1+8\Delta\sigma^{2})}{\gamma(\Delta(1-\sigma^{2})-1)(1-10\sigma^{2})}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1-\frac{\gamma\Delta}{1+8\Delta\sigma^{2}}}}\right)

\displaystyle\quad=-1-\frac{\delta(1+8\Delta\sigma^{2})}{\gamma(\Delta(1-\sigma^{2})-1)(1-10\sigma^{2})}\frac{1+8\Delta\sigma^{2}-\gamma\Delta}{1+8\Delta\sigma^{2}}

\displaystyle\quad=-1-\frac{\delta(1+8\Delta\sigma^{2}-\gamma\Delta)}{\gamma(\Delta(1-\sigma^{2})-1)(1-10\sigma^{2})}

\displaystyle\quad=\frac{-\gamma(\Delta(1-\sigma^{2})-1)(1-10\sigma^{2})-\delta(1+8\Delta\sigma^{2}-\gamma\Delta)}{\gamma(\Delta(1-\sigma^{2})-1)(1-10\sigma^{2})}

\displaystyle\quad=\frac{(-10\gamma\Delta)\sigma^{4}+(11\gamma\Delta-10\gamma-8\delta\Delta)\sigma^{2}+(\gamma\delta\Delta-\gamma\Delta+\gamma-\delta)}{\gamma(\Delta(1-\sigma^{2})-1)(1-10\sigma^{2})}.

Note that the denominator is positive.
Set
\delta=\frac{\gamma(\Delta-1)}{\gamma\Delta-1}
.
Then the numerator, viewed as a quadratic in
\sigma^{2}
, has roots at
0
and at
\frac{11\gamma\Delta^{2}-10\gamma\Delta-8\Delta^{2}-3\Delta+10}{10\Delta(\gamma\Delta-1)}
.
Furthermore, when the second root is positive, the quadratic is also positive for all
\sigma^{2}
between the two roots.
Hence, in order to prove that the expression is positive for all
0\leqslant\sigma^{2}<0.1
, it suffices to show that the second root is at least
0.1
in our setting.
Indeed, for all
\gamma\geqslant 0.9
and all
\Delta\geqslant 10
, we have that
\frac{11\gamma\Delta^{2}-10\gamma\Delta-8\Delta^{2}-3\Delta+10}{10\Delta(\gamma\Delta-1)}\geqslant 0.1
.

Therefore, for all
x\in\mathbb{R}
,

f(x)=q(x)-1-\frac{\frac{\gamma(\Delta-1)}{\gamma\Delta-1}(1+8\Delta\sigma^{2})}{\gamma(\Delta(1-\sigma^{2})-1)(1-10\sigma^{2})}(1-p(x))\geqslant 0.

Therefore, by
Lemma

A.17
, for all
x\in\mathbb{R}
,

f(x)=q(x)^{t}-1-\frac{\frac{\gamma(\Delta-1)}{\gamma\Delta-1}(1+8\Delta\sigma^{2})}{\gamma(\Delta(1-\sigma^{2})-1)(1-10\sigma^{2})}(1-p(x)^{t})\geqslant 0.

Because
f(x)
is a univariate polynomial of degree
2t
, there also exists a sum-of-squares proof of degree at most
2t
that
f(x)\geqslant 0
.
Note that this constitutes a degree-
2t
sum-of-squares proof that
\{p(x)^{t}\leqslant 1\}\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{8.53467pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-8.53467pt\raisebox{-1.96112pt}{\makebox[8.53467pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\hskip-8.53467pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[8.53467pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{x}$}}}$}}}\{q(x)^{t}\geqslant 1\}
.
This concludes the proof.

∎

A.3
Finite sample lemmas

Lemma A.20

(Restatement of Theorem 4 in
[
BV08
]
)
.

For
n\geqslant C\frac{kd\log^{2}(d/\delta)}{\varepsilon^{2}}
, with probability
1-\delta
,

\|\operatorname{cov}(\bm{y}^{0})^{-1/2}(\hat{\mathbb{E}}\bm{y}^{0}-\mathbb{E}\bm{y}^{0})\|\leqslant\varepsilon

and

\|I_{d}-\operatorname{cov}(\bm{y}^{0})^{-1/2}\widehat{\operatorname{cov}}(\bm{y}^{0})\operatorname{cov}(\bm{y}^{0})^{-1/2}\|\leqslant\varepsilon.

Lemma A.21
.

For
n\geqslant C\frac{kd\log^{2}(d/\delta)}{\varepsilon^{2}}
, with probability
1-\delta
,

\|I_{d}-\widehat{\operatorname{cov}}(\bm{y}^{0})^{-1/2}\operatorname{cov}(\bm{y}^{0})\widehat{\operatorname{cov}}(\bm{y}^{0})^{-1/2}\|\leqslant\varepsilon.

Proof.

By
Lemma

A.20
,

\|I_{d}-\operatorname{cov}(\bm{y}^{0})^{-1/2}\widehat{\operatorname{cov}}(\bm{y}^{0})\operatorname{cov}(\bm{y}^{0})^{-1/2}\|\leqslant 2\varepsilon.

Then

(1-\varepsilon)I_{d}\preceq\operatorname{cov}(\bm{y}^{0})^{-1/2}\widehat{\operatorname{cov}}(\bm{y}^{0})\operatorname{cov}(\bm{y}^{0})^{-1/2}\preceq(1+\varepsilon)I_{d},

(1-\varepsilon)\operatorname{cov}(\bm{y}^{0})\preceq\widehat{\operatorname{cov}}(\bm{y}^{0})\preceq(1+\varepsilon)\operatorname{cov}(\bm{y}^{0}),

\frac{1}{1+\varepsilon}\widehat{\operatorname{cov}}(\bm{y}^{0})\preceq\operatorname{cov}(\bm{y}^{0})\preceq\frac{1}{1-\varepsilon}\widehat{\operatorname{cov}}(\bm{y}^{0}).

Using that
\frac{1}{1+\varepsilon}\geqslant 1-2\varepsilon
and
\frac{1}{1-\varepsilon}\leqslant 1+2\varepsilon
for
\varepsilon\leqslant 1/2
,

(1-2\varepsilon)\widehat{\operatorname{cov}}(\bm{y}^{0})\preceq\operatorname{cov}(\bm{y}^{0})\preceq(1+2\varepsilon)\widehat{\operatorname{cov}}(\bm{y}^{0}),

(1-2\varepsilon)I_{d}\preceq\widehat{\operatorname{cov}}(\bm{y}^{0})^{-1/2}\operatorname{cov}(\bm{y}^{0})\widehat{\operatorname{cov}}(\bm{y}^{0})^{-1/2}\preceq(1+2\varepsilon)I_{d},

\|I_{d}-\widehat{\operatorname{cov}}(\bm{y}^{0})^{-1/2}\operatorname{cov}(\bm{y}^{0})\widehat{\operatorname{cov}}(\bm{y}^{0})^{-1/2}\|\leqslant 2\varepsilon.

∎

Lemma A.22

(Restatement of Lemma 22 in
[
MV10
]
)
.

Let the random variable
\overline{\bm{y}}\in\mathbb{R}
be distributed according to an istotropic mixture of
k
one-dimensional Gaussian distributions with minimum mixing weight
p_{\mathrm{min}}
.
Let
\overline{y}_{1},...,\overline{y}_{n}\in\mathbb{R}
be generated i.i.d. according to the distribution of
\overline{\bm{y}}
.
Then, with probability
1-\delta
,

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\overline{y}_{i}^{t}-\mathbb{E}\overline{\bm{y}}^{t}}}\right)^{2}\leqslant\frac{1}{n\delta}p_{\mathrm{min}}^{-O(t)}.

Lemma A.23
.

Let the random variable
\bm{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
be distributed according to an istotropic mixture of
k

d
-dimensional Gaussian distributions with minimum mixing weight
p_{\mathrm{min}}
. Let
y_{1},...,y_{n}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
be generated i.i.d. according to the distribution of
\bm{y}
. Then, with probability
1-d^{t}\delta
,

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}y_{i}^{\otimes t}-\mathbb{E}\bm{y}^{\otimes t}}}\right\|^{2}\leqslant\frac{1}{n\delta}(p_{\mathrm{min}}^{-1}d)^{O(t)}.

Proof.

The proof is similar to the proof of Lemma 22 in
[
MV10
]
.

We denote by
\bm{y}^{(j)}
the
j
-th coordinate of
\bm{y}
. Let
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{d}
satisfy
\sum_{j=1}^{d}\alpha_{j}=t
. Let
\bm{z}^{\alpha}=\prod_{j=1}^{d}(\bm{y}^{(j)})^{\alpha_{j}}
. By Chebyshev’s inequality, with probability at least
1-\delta
,

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}z_{i}^{\alpha}-\mathbb{E}\bm{z}^{\alpha}}}\right)^{2}\leqslant\frac{1}{\delta}\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}z_{i}^{\alpha}-\mathbb{E}\bm{z}^{\alpha}}}\right)^{2}}}\right].

We now bound the right-hand side. Note that
\mathbb{E}[\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}z_{i}^{\alpha}-\mathbb{E}\bm{z}^{\alpha}]=0
. Using that for independent random variables the variance of the sum is equal to the sum of the variances,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}z_{i}^{\alpha}-\mathbb{E}\bm{z}^{\alpha}}}\right)^{2}}}\right]=\frac{1}{n}\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{z}^{\alpha}-\mathbb{E}\bm{z}^{\alpha}}}\right)^{2}}}\right]\leqslant\frac{1}{n}\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{z}^{\alpha}}}\right)^{2}}}\right]\leqslant\frac{1}{n}p_{\mathrm{min}}^{-O(t)}.

The last inequality follows by using that
\mathbb{E}(\bm{y}^{(j)})^{t}\leqslant p_{\mathrm{min}}^{-O(t)}
for all
j
and that, for random varaibles
\bm{x}_{1},...,\bm{x}_{t}\in\mathbb{R}
,

|\mathbb{E}[\bm{x}_{1}\cdot...\cdot\bm{x}_{t}]|\leqslant(\mathbb{E}\bm{x}_{1}^{t}\cdot...\cdot\mathbb{E}\bm{x}_{t}^{t})^{1/t}
. Then, by a union bound, with probability at least
1-d^{t}\delta
,

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}y_{i}^{\otimes t}-\mathbb{E}\bm{y}^{\otimes t}}}\right\|^{2}\leqslant\frac{1}{n\delta}d^{t}p_{\mathrm{min}}^{-O(t)}.

∎

Lemma A.24
.

Let the random variable
\bm{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
be distributed according to an istotropic mixture of
k

d
-dimensional Gaussian distributions with minimum mixing weight
p_{\mathrm{min}}
. Let
y_{1},...,y_{n}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
be generated i.i.d. according to the distribution of
\bm{y}
. Then, for
n\geqslant\frac{1}{\delta}
, with probability
1-d\delta
,

\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|y_{i}}}\right\|^{2t}\leqslant(p_{\mathrm{min}}^{-1}d)^{O(t)}.

Proof.

Denote by
\bm{y}^{(j)}
the
j
-th coordinate of
\bm{y}
. We have

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\|y_{i}\|^{2t}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sum_{j=1}^{d}(y_{i}^{(j)})^{2}}}\right)^{t}\leqslant\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}d^{t-1}\sum_{j=1}^{d}(y_{i}^{(j)})^{2t}=d^{t-1}\sum_{j=1}^{d}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(y_{i}^{(j)})^{2t}.

Note that, for each
j
,
\bm{y}^{(j)}
is distributed according to an isotropic mixture of
k
one-dimensional Gaussian distributions. By a union bound, with probability
1-d\delta
the result in
Lemma

A.22
holds for each coordinate
\bm{y}^{(j)}
. Then

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\|y_{i}\|^{2t}

\displaystyle\leqslant d^{t-1}\sum_{j=1}^{d}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathbb{E}(\bm{y}^{(j)})^{2t}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{n\delta}}p_{\mathrm{min}}^{-O(t)}}}\right).

We have that
\mathbb{E}(\bm{y}^{(j)})^{2t}\leqslant p_{\mathrm{min}}^{-O(t)}
. Using that
n\delta\geqslant 1
, we get then

\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\|y_{i}\|^{2t}\leqslant d^{t}\cdot p_{\mathrm{min}}^{-O(t)}\leqslant(p_{\mathrm{min}}^{-1}d)^{O(t)}.

∎

A.4
Isotropic position transformation lemmas

The setting for the following two lemmas is that of
Section

6.3
.

Lemma A.25

(See Lemma 10 in
[
HK13
]
)
.

We have

•

\hat{W}\widehat{\operatorname{cov}(\bm{y}^{0})}\hat{W}^{\top}=I_{d}
,

•

\hat{W}\operatorname{cov}(\bm{y}^{0})\hat{W}^{\top}\succ 0
,

•

W\operatorname{cov}(\bm{y}^{0})W^{\top}=I_{d}
.

Proof.

The results are immediate by substitution.
∎

Lemma A.26

(See Lemma 10 in
[
HK13
]
)
.

Suppose that

\|I_{d}-\operatorname{cov}(\bm{y}^{0})^{-1/2}\widehat{\operatorname{cov}}(\bm{y}^{0})\operatorname{cov}(\bm{y}^{0})^{-1/2}\|\leqslant\varepsilon.

Then

\|I_{d}-(\hat{W}\operatorname{cov}(\bm{y}^{0})\hat{W}^{\top})^{1/2}\|\leqslant O(\varepsilon)\cdot\|\operatorname{cov}(\bm{y}^{0})\|\cdot\|\operatorname{cov}(\bm{y}^{0})^{-1}\|.

Proof.

The given assumption implies that all eigenvalues of
\operatorname{cov}(\bm{y}^{0})^{-1/2}\widehat{\operatorname{cov}}(\bm{y}^{0})\operatorname{cov}(\bm{y}^{0})^{-1/2}
lie between
1-\varepsilon
and
1+\varepsilon
.
Hence all eigenvalues of the inverse of this matrix lie between
\frac{1}{1+\varepsilon}=1+O(\varepsilon)
and
\frac{1}{1-\varepsilon}=1-O(\varepsilon)
.
Then

\|I_{d}-\operatorname{cov}(\bm{y}^{0})^{1/2}\widehat{\operatorname{cov}}(\bm{y}^{0})^{-1}\operatorname{cov}(\bm{y}^{0})^{1/2}\|\leqslant O(\varepsilon),

(1-O(\varepsilon))\cdot\operatorname{cov}(\bm{y}^{0})^{-1}\preceq\widehat{\operatorname{cov}}(\bm{y}^{0})^{-1}\preceq(1+O(\varepsilon))\cdot\operatorname{cov}(\bm{y}^{0})^{-1}.

Then

\displaystyle\|\hat{W}\|

\displaystyle=\|(\hat{U}^{\top}\widehat{\operatorname{cov}}(\bm{y}^{0})\hat{U})^{-1/2}\hat{U}^{\top}\|\leqslant\|(\hat{U}^{\top}\widehat{\operatorname{cov}}(\bm{y}^{0})\hat{U})^{-1/2}\|=\|\widehat{\operatorname{cov}}(\bm{y}^{0})^{-1/2}\|

\displaystyle=\|\widehat{\operatorname{cov}}(\bm{y}^{0})^{-1}\|^{1/2}\leqslant((1+O(\varepsilon))\cdot\|\operatorname{cov}(\bm{y}^{0})^{-1}\|)^{1/2}.

The given assumption also implies that

-\varepsilon\cdot\operatorname{cov}(\bm{y}^{0})\preceq\widehat{\operatorname{cov}}(\bm{y}^{0})-\operatorname{cov}(\bm{y}^{0})\preceq\varepsilon\cdot\operatorname{cov}(\bm{y}^{0}).

Hence

\displaystyle\|\widehat{\operatorname{cov}}(\bm{y}^{0})-\operatorname{cov}(\bm{y}^{0})\|\leqslant\varepsilon\cdot\|\operatorname{cov}(\bm{y}^{0})\|.

Using these bounds on
\|\hat{W}\|
and
\|\widehat{\operatorname{cov}}(\bm{y}^{0})-\operatorname{cov}(\bm{y}^{0})\|
, together with the fact that
\hat{W}\widehat{\operatorname{cov}}(\bm{y}^{0})\hat{W}^{\top}=I_{d}
, we get that

\displaystyle\|I_{d}-\hat{W}\operatorname{cov}(\bm{y}^{0})\hat{W}^{\top}\|

\displaystyle=\|\hat{W}(\widehat{\operatorname{cov}}(\bm{y}^{0})-\operatorname{cov}(\bm{y}^{0}))\hat{W}^{\top}\|

\displaystyle\leqslant\|\hat{W}\|^{2}\cdot\|\widehat{\operatorname{cov}}(\bm{y}^{0})-\operatorname{cov}(\bm{y}^{0})\|

\displaystyle\leqslant\varepsilon\cdot(1+O(\varepsilon))\cdot\|\operatorname{cov}(\bm{y}^{0})\|\cdot\|\operatorname{cov}(\bm{y}^{0})^{-1}\|

\displaystyle\leqslant O(\varepsilon)\cdot\|\operatorname{cov}(\bm{y}^{0})\|\cdot\|\operatorname{cov}(\bm{y}^{0})^{-1}\|.

Then all eigenvalues of
\hat{W}\operatorname{cov}(\bm{y}^{0})\hat{W}^{\top}
lie between
1-\delta
and
1+\delta
, for
\delta=O(\varepsilon)\cdot\|\operatorname{cov}(\bm{y}^{0})\|\cdot\|\operatorname{cov}(\bm{y}^{0})^{-1}\|
.
Hence all eigenvalues of the square root of this matrix lie between
\sqrt{1-\delta}=1-O(\delta)
and
\sqrt{1+\delta}=1+O(\delta)
.
Then

\|I_{d}-(\hat{W}\operatorname{cov}(\bm{y}^{0})\hat{W}^{\top})^{1/2}\|\leqslant O(\varepsilon)\cdot\|\operatorname{cov}(\bm{y}^{0})\|\cdot\|\operatorname{cov}(\bm{y}^{0})^{-1}\|.

∎

A.5
Miscellaneous lemmas

Lemma A.27
.

Let
W\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
and
\Sigma\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
with
\Sigma\succ 0
symmetric. Suppose that
W\Sigma W^{\top}=I_{d}
. Then
W=Q\Sigma^{-1/2}
for some orthogonal matrix
Q\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
.

Proof.

We have

\displaystyle W\Sigma W^{\top}=I_{d}\Longleftrightarrow(W\Sigma^{1/2})(W\Sigma^{1/2})^{\top}=I_{d}\Longleftrightarrow W\Sigma^{1/2}=Q\Longleftrightarrow W=Q\Sigma^{-1/2}

for some orthogonal matrix
Q
.
∎

Lemma A.28
.

For integers
0\leqslant s\leqslant t
,

\binom{2t}{2s}(2t-2s-1)!!\leqslant\binom{t}{s}(et)^{t-s},

\binom{2t}{2s}(2t-2s-1)!!\geqslant\binom{t}{s}(t/2)^{t-s}.

Proof.

We use the known fact that
(2t-2s-1)!!=\frac{(2t-2s)!}{2^{t-s}(t-s)!}
. Then

\frac{\binom{2t}{2s}(2t-2s-1)!!}{\binom{t}{s}t^{t-s}}=\frac{\frac{(2t)!}{(2s)!(2t-2s)!}\frac{(2t-2s)!}{2^{t-s}(t-s)!}}{\frac{t!}{s!(t-s)!}t^{t-s}}=\frac{(t+1)(t+2)\cdots(2t)}{(s+1)(s+2)\cdots(2s)(2t)^{t-s}}.

For the upper bound, we have

\displaystyle\frac{(t+1)(t+2)\cdots(2t)}{(s+1)(s+2)\cdots(2s)(2t)^{t-s}}

\displaystyle=\frac{(t+1)(t+2)\cdots(t+s)}{(s+1)(s+2)\cdots(2s)}\frac{(t+s+1)(t+s+2)\cdots(2t)}{(2t)^{t-s}}

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{(t+1)(t+2)\cdots(t+s)}{(s+1)(s+2)\cdots(2s)}

\displaystyle\leqslant\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{t}{s}}}\right)^{s}\leqslant e^{t-s},

where in the last inequality we used that
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{t}{s}}}\right)^{\frac{s}{t-s}}=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1+\frac{t-s}{s}}}\right)^{\frac{s}{t-s}}\leqslant e
.

For the lower bound, we have

\displaystyle\frac{(t+1)(t+2)\cdots(2t)}{(s+1)(s+2)\cdots(2s)(2t)^{t-s}}

\displaystyle=\frac{(t+1)(t+2)\cdots(t+s)}{(s+1)(s+2)\cdots(2s)}\frac{(t+s+1)(t+s+2)\cdots(2t)}{(2t)^{t-s}}

\displaystyle\geqslant\frac{(t+s+1)(t+s+2)\cdots(2t)}{(2t)^{t-s}}

\displaystyle\geqslant\frac{1}{2^{t-s}}.

∎

A.6
Proofs deferred from Section
4

Proof of
Lemma

4.10
.

For the first proof, with probability
1-\varepsilon/100
,

\displaystyle\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{8.25113pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-8.25113pt\raisebox{-6.47223pt}{\makebox[8.25113pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{2}$}}}\hskip-8.25113pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[8.25113pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{v}$}}}$}}}v^{\top}\operatorname{cov}(\bm{z})v

\displaystyle=v^{\top}\widehat{\operatorname{cov}}(\bm{z})^{1/2}\widehat{\operatorname{cov}}(\bm{z})^{-1/2}\operatorname{cov}(\bm{z})\widehat{\operatorname{cov}}(\bm{z})^{-1/2}\widehat{\operatorname{cov}}(\bm{z})^{1/2}v

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(*)}}{{\leqslant}}\|\widehat{\operatorname{cov}}(\bm{z})^{-1/2}\operatorname{cov}(\bm{z})\widehat{\operatorname{cov}}(\bm{z})^{-1/2}\|\cdot\|\widehat{\operatorname{cov}}(\bm{z})^{1/2}v\|^{2}

\displaystyle\leqslant(1+\eta)C,

where in (*) we used
Lemma

A.21
.

Similarly, for the second proof, with probability
1-\varepsilon/100

\displaystyle\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{8.25113pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-8.25113pt\raisebox{-6.47223pt}{\makebox[8.25113pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{2}$}}}\hskip-8.25113pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[8.25113pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{v}$}}}$}}}v^{\top}\widehat{\operatorname{cov}}(\bm{z})v

\displaystyle=v^{\top}\operatorname{cov}(\bm{z})^{1/2}\operatorname{cov}(\bm{z})^{-1/2}\widehat{\operatorname{cov}}(\bm{z})\operatorname{cov}(\bm{z})^{-1/2}\operatorname{cov}(\bm{z})^{1/2}v

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(*)}}{{\leqslant}}\|\operatorname{cov}(\bm{z})^{-1/2}\widehat{\operatorname{cov}}(\bm{z})\operatorname{cov}(\bm{z})^{-1/2}\|\cdot\|\operatorname{cov}(\bm{z})^{1/2}v\|^{2}

\displaystyle\leqslant(1+\eta)C.

where in (*) we used
Lemma

A.20
.
∎

Proof of
Lemma

4.11
.

We have

\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{11.00691pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-11.00691pt\raisebox{-6.47223pt}{\makebox[11.00691pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{2t}$}}}\hskip-11.00691pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[11.00691pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{v}$}}}$}}}\hat{\mathbb{E}}\langle\bm{z},v\rangle^{2t}=\mathbb{E}\langle\bm{z},v\rangle^{2t}+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\hat{\mathbb{E}}\langle\bm{z},v\rangle^{2t}-\mathbb{E}\langle\bm{z},v\rangle^{2t}}}\right).

For the second term we have that

\displaystyle\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{11.00691pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-11.00691pt\raisebox{-6.47223pt}{\makebox[11.00691pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{4t}$}}}\hskip-11.00691pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[11.00691pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{v}$}}}$}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\hat{\mathbb{E}}\langle\bm{z},v\rangle^{2t}-\mathbb{E}\langle\bm{z},v\rangle^{2t}}}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle\quad=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\hat{\mathbb{E}}\langle\operatorname{cov}(\bm{z})^{-1/2}\bm{z},\operatorname{cov}(\bm{z})^{1/2}v\rangle^{2t}-\mathbb{E}\langle\operatorname{cov}(\bm{z})^{-1/2}\bm{z},\operatorname{cov}(\bm{z})^{1/2}v\rangle^{2t}}}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle\quad=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\hat{\mathbb{E}}\langle(\operatorname{cov}(\bm{z})^{-1/2}\bm{z})^{\otimes 2t},(\operatorname{cov}(\bm{z})^{1/2}v)^{\otimes 2t}\rangle-\mathbb{E}\langle(\operatorname{cov}(\bm{z})^{-1/2}\bm{z})^{\otimes 2t},(\operatorname{cov}(\bm{z})^{1/2}v)^{\otimes 2t}\rangle}}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle\quad=\langle\hat{\mathbb{E}}(\operatorname{cov}(\bm{z})^{-1/2}\bm{z})^{\otimes 2t}-\mathbb{E}(\operatorname{cov}(\bm{z})^{-1/2}\bm{z})^{\otimes 2t},(\operatorname{cov}(\bm{z})^{1/2}v)^{\otimes 2t}\rangle^{2}

\displaystyle\quad\leqslant\|\hat{\mathbb{E}}(\operatorname{cov}(\bm{z})^{-1/2}\bm{z})^{\otimes 2t}-\mathbb{E}(\operatorname{cov}(\bm{z})^{-1/2}\bm{z})^{\otimes 2t}\|^{2}\cdot\|(\operatorname{cov}(\bm{z})^{1/2}v)^{\otimes 2t}\|

\displaystyle\quad\leqslant\|\hat{\mathbb{E}}(\operatorname{cov}(\bm{z})^{-1/2}\bm{z})^{\otimes 2t}-\mathbb{E}(\operatorname{cov}(\bm{z})^{-1/2}\bm{z})^{\otimes 2t}\|^{2}\cdot C^{t}

Note that
\mathbb{E}\bm{z}=0
. Then
\operatorname{cov}(\bm{z})^{-1/2}\bm{z}
is in isotropic position.
By
Lemma

A.23
, with probability
1-d^{2t}\delta
, we have that

\|\hat{\mathbb{E}}(\operatorname{cov}(\bm{z})^{-1/2}\bm{z})^{\otimes 2t}-\mathbb{E}(\operatorname{cov}(\bm{z})^{-1/2}\bm{z})^{\otimes 2t}\|^{2}\leqslant\frac{1}{n\delta}(p_{\mathrm{min}}^{-2}d)^{O(t)}.

Select
n=(Cp_{\mathrm{min}}^{-1}d)^{O(t)}\eta^{-1}\varepsilon^{-1}
large enough the right-hand side is upper boundeed by
\eta^{2}C^{-t}
with probability at least
1-\varepsilon
.
Then it follows that

\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{11.00691pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-11.00691pt\raisebox{-6.47223pt}{\makebox[11.00691pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{4t}$}}}\hskip-11.00691pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[11.00691pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{v}$}}}$}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\hat{\mathbb{E}}\langle\bm{z},v\rangle^{2t}-\mathbb{E}\langle\bm{z},v\rangle^{2t}}}\right)^{2}\leqslant\eta^{2}.

Then, by
Lemma

A.9
,
we get that

\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{19.56857pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-19.56857pt\raisebox{-7.21112pt}{\makebox[19.56857pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{O(t)}$}}}\hskip-19.56857pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[19.56857pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{v}$}}}$}}}\hat{\mathbb{E}}\langle\bm{z},v\rangle^{2t}-\mathbb{E}\langle\bm{z},v\rangle^{2t}\leqslant\eta,

\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{19.56857pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-19.56857pt\raisebox{-7.21112pt}{\makebox[19.56857pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{O(t)}$}}}\hskip-19.56857pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[19.56857pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{v}$}}}$}}}-\hat{\mathbb{E}}\langle\bm{z},v\rangle^{2t}+\mathbb{E}\langle\bm{z},v\rangle^{2t}\leqslant\eta.

Rearranging leads to the desired results.

∎

A.7
Proofs deferred from Section
5

Proof of
Lemma

5.14
.

We have

\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{11.00691pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-11.00691pt\raisebox{-6.47223pt}{\makebox[11.00691pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{2t}$}}}\hskip-11.00691pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[11.00691pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{v}$}}}$}}}\hat{\mathbb{E}}\langle\bm{y},v\rangle^{2t}=\mathbb{E}\langle\bm{y},v\rangle^{2t}+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\hat{\mathbb{E}}\langle\bm{y},v\rangle^{2t}-\mathbb{E}\langle\bm{y},v\rangle^{2t}}}\right).

For the second term we have that

\displaystyle\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{11.00691pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-11.00691pt\raisebox{-6.47223pt}{\makebox[11.00691pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{4t}$}}}\hskip-11.00691pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[11.00691pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{v}$}}}$}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\hat{\mathbb{E}}\langle\bm{y},v\rangle^{2t}-\mathbb{E}\langle\bm{y},v\rangle^{2t}}}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\hat{\mathbb{E}}\langle\bm{y}^{\otimes 2t},v^{\otimes 2t}\rangle-\mathbb{E}\langle\bm{y}^{\otimes 2t},v^{\otimes 2t}\rangle}}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle=\langle\hat{\mathbb{E}}\bm{y}^{\otimes 2t}-\mathbb{E}\bm{y}^{\otimes 2t},v^{\otimes 2t}\rangle^{2}

\displaystyle\leqslant\|\hat{\mathbb{E}}\bm{y}^{\otimes 2t}-\mathbb{E}\bm{y}^{\otimes 2t}\|^{2}.

By
Lemma

A.23
, with probability
1-d^{2t}\delta
, we have that

\|\hat{\mathbb{E}}\bm{y}^{\otimes 2t}-\mathbb{E}\bm{y}^{\otimes 2t}\|^{2}\leqslant\frac{1}{n\delta}(p_{\mathrm{min}}^{-1}d)^{O(t)}.

For
n\geqslant(p_{\mathrm{min}}^{-1}d)^{O(t)}\eta^{-2}\varepsilon^{-1}
the right-hand side is
\eta^{2}
with probability at least
1-\varepsilon
.
Then it follows that

\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{11.00691pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-11.00691pt\raisebox{-6.47223pt}{\makebox[11.00691pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{4t}$}}}\hskip-11.00691pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[11.00691pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{v}$}}}$}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\hat{\mathbb{E}}\langle\bm{y},v\rangle^{2t}-\mathbb{E}\langle\bm{y},v\rangle^{2t}}}\right)^{2}\leqslant\eta^{2}.

Then, by
Lemma

A.9
, we get that

\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{19.56857pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-19.56857pt\raisebox{-7.21112pt}{\makebox[19.56857pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{O(t)}$}}}\hskip-19.56857pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[19.56857pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{v}$}}}$}}}\hat{\mathbb{E}}\langle\bm{y},v\rangle^{2t}-\mathbb{E}\langle\bm{y},v\rangle^{2t}\leqslant\eta,

\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{19.56857pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-19.56857pt\raisebox{-7.21112pt}{\makebox[19.56857pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{O(t)}$}}}\hskip-19.56857pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[19.56857pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{v}$}}}$}}}-\hat{\mathbb{E}}\langle\bm{y},v\rangle^{2t}+\mathbb{E}\langle\bm{y},v\rangle^{2t}\leqslant\eta.

Rearranging leads to the desired results.
∎

A.8
Proofs deferred from Section
6

Proof of
Lemma

6.5
.

We have

\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{11.00691pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-11.00691pt\raisebox{-6.47223pt}{\makebox[11.00691pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{2t}$}}}\hskip-11.00691pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[11.00691pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{v}$}}}$}}}\hat{\mathbb{E}}\langle\hat{W}(\bm{y}^{0}-\hat{\mathbb{E}}\bm{y}^{0}),v\rangle^{2t}=\hat{\mathbb{E}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\langle\hat{W}(\bm{y}^{0}-\mathbb{E}\bm{y}^{0}),v\rangle+\langle\hat{W}(\mathbb{E}\bm{y}^{0}-\hat{\mathbb{E}}\bm{y}^{0}),v\rangle}}\right)^{2t}.

Let
\delta>0
to be specified later. For the upper bound:

\displaystyle\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{11.00691pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-11.00691pt\raisebox{-6.47223pt}{\makebox[11.00691pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{2t}$}}}\hskip-11.00691pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[11.00691pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{v}$}}}$}}}\hat{\mathbb{E}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\langle\hat{W}(\bm{y}^{0}-\mathbb{E}\bm{y}^{0}),v\rangle+\langle\hat{W}(\mathbb{E}\bm{y}^{0}-\hat{\mathbb{E}}\bm{y}^{0}),v\rangle}}\right)^{2t}

\displaystyle\quad\leqslant\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1+\delta}}\right)^{2t-1}\cdot\hat{\mathbb{E}}\langle\hat{W}(\bm{y}^{0}-\mathbb{E}\bm{y}^{0}),v\rangle^{2t}+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1+\frac{1}{\delta}}}\right)^{2t-1}\cdot\langle\hat{W}(\mathbb{E}\bm{y}^{0}-\hat{\mathbb{E}}\bm{y}^{0}),v\rangle^{2t},

where in the inequality we used
Lemma

A.6
.

For the lower bound:

\displaystyle\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{11.00691pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-11.00691pt\raisebox{-6.47223pt}{\makebox[11.00691pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{2t}$}}}\hskip-11.00691pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[11.00691pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{v}$}}}$}}}\hat{\mathbb{E}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\langle\hat{W}(\bm{y}^{0}-\mathbb{E}\bm{y}^{0}),v\rangle+\langle\hat{W}(\mathbb{E}\bm{y}^{0}-\hat{\mathbb{E}}\bm{y}^{0}),v\rangle}}\right)^{2t}

\displaystyle\quad\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(1)}}{{\geqslant}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1}{1+\delta}}}\right)^{2t-1}\cdot\hat{\mathbb{E}}\langle\hat{W}(\bm{y}^{0}-\mathbb{E}\bm{y}^{0}),v\rangle^{2t}-\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1+\frac{1}{\delta}}{1+\delta}}}\right)^{2t-1}\cdot\langle\hat{W}(\mathbb{E}\bm{y}^{0}-\hat{\mathbb{E}}\bm{y}^{0}),v\rangle^{2t}

\displaystyle\quad\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(2)}}{{\geqslant}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1}{1+\delta}}}\right)^{2t-1}\cdot\hat{\mathbb{E}}\langle\hat{W}(\bm{y}^{0}-\mathbb{E}\bm{y}^{0}),v\rangle^{2t}-O\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1+\frac{1}{\delta}}}\right)^{2t-1}\cdot\langle\hat{W}(\mathbb{E}\bm{y}^{0}-\hat{\mathbb{E}}\bm{y}^{0}),v\rangle^{2t},

where in (1) we used that, by
Lemma

A.6
,
\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{18.66075pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-18.66075pt\raisebox{-6.47223pt}{\makebox[18.66075pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{2t}$}}}\hskip-18.66075pt\raisebox{3.32222pt}{\makebox[18.66075pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{A,B}$}}}$}}}A^{2t}\leqslant(1+\delta)^{2t-1}(A+B)^{2t}+(1+\frac{1}{\delta})^{2t-1}B^{2t}
, so
\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{18.66075pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-18.66075pt\raisebox{-6.47223pt}{\makebox[18.66075pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{2t}$}}}\hskip-18.66075pt\raisebox{3.32222pt}{\makebox[18.66075pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{A,B}$}}}$}}}(A+B)^{2t}\geqslant(\frac{1}{1+\delta})^{2t-1}A^{2t}-(\frac{1+\frac{1}{\delta}}{1+\delta})^{2t-1}B^{2t}
. In (2) we assumed that
\delta=O(1)
, which will be the case for our choice.

Now take
\delta=\frac{\eta}{100t}
. Then
(1+\delta)^{2t-1}\leqslant 1+\eta
and
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1}{1+\delta}}}\right)^{2t-1}\geqslant 1-\eta
for
\eta
small.

For the second term in both bounds, we use that

\displaystyle\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{11.00691pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-11.00691pt\raisebox{-6.47223pt}{\makebox[11.00691pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{2t}$}}}\hskip-11.00691pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[11.00691pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{v}$}}}$}}}\langle\hat{W}(\mathbb{E}\bm{y}^{0}-\hat{\mathbb{E}}\bm{y}^{0}),v\rangle^{2t}

\displaystyle\leqslant\|\hat{W}(\mathbb{E}\bm{y}^{0}-\hat{\mathbb{E}}\bm{y}^{0})\|^{2t}

\displaystyle=\|\hat{W}W^{-1}W(\mathbb{E}\bm{y}^{0}-\hat{\mathbb{E}}\bm{y}^{0})\|^{2t}

\displaystyle\leqslant\|\hat{W}W^{-1}\|^{2t}\cdot\|W(\mathbb{E}\bm{y}^{0}-\hat{\mathbb{E}}\bm{y}^{0})\|^{2t}

\displaystyle=\|(\hat{W}\operatorname{cov}(\bm{y}^{0})^{-1}\hat{W}^{\top})^{1/2}\|^{2t}\cdot\|W(\mathbb{E}\bm{y}^{0}-\hat{\mathbb{E}}\bm{y}^{0})\|^{2t}.

By
Lemma

A.20
and
Lemma

A.26
, with probability
1-\varepsilon
,

\displaystyle\|W(\mathbb{E}\bm{y}^{0}-\hat{\mathbb{E}}\bm{y}^{0})\|

\displaystyle\leqslant\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{\eta}{t}}}\right)^{O(1)}

and

\|(\hat{W}\operatorname{cov}(\bm{y}^{0})^{-1}\hat{W}^{\top})^{1/2}\|\leqslant 1.

Then the second term in both bounds becomes

\displaystyle\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{11.00691pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-11.00691pt\raisebox{-6.47223pt}{\makebox[11.00691pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{2t}$}}}\hskip-11.00691pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[11.00691pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{v}$}}}$}}}O\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1+\frac{1}{\delta}}}\right)^{2t-1}\cdot\langle\hat{W}(\mathbb{E}\bm{y}^{0}-\hat{\mathbb{E}}\bm{y}^{0}),v\rangle^{2t}\leqslant O\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{100t}{\eta}}}\right)^{2t-1}\cdot\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{\eta}{t}}}\right)^{O(t)}\leqslant\eta.

∎

Proof of
Lemma

6.6
.

We have

\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{11.00691pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-11.00691pt\raisebox{-6.47223pt}{\makebox[11.00691pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{2t}$}}}\hskip-11.00691pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[11.00691pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{v}$}}}$}}}\hat{\mathbb{E}}\langle\hat{W}(\bm{y}^{0}-\mathbb{E}\bm{y}^{0}),v\rangle^{2t}=\hat{\mathbb{E}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle W(\bm{y}^{0}-\mathbb{E}\bm{y}^{0}),v}}\right\rangle+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\hat{W}-W}}\right)(\bm{y}^{0}-\mathbb{E}\bm{y}^{0}),v}}\right\rangle}}\right)^{2t}.

Let
\delta>0
to be specified later. For the upper bound:

\displaystyle\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{11.00691pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-11.00691pt\raisebox{-6.47223pt}{\makebox[11.00691pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{2t}$}}}\hskip-11.00691pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[11.00691pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{v}$}}}$}}}\hat{\mathbb{E}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle W(\bm{y}^{0}-\mathbb{E}\bm{y}^{0}),v}}\right\rangle+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\hat{W}-W}}\right)(\bm{y}^{0}-\mathbb{E}\bm{y}^{0}),v}}\right\rangle}}\right)^{2t}

\displaystyle\quad\leqslant\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1+\delta}}\right)^{2t-1}\cdot\hat{\mathbb{E}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle W(\bm{y}^{0}-\mathbb{E}\bm{y}^{0}),v}}\right\rangle^{2t}+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1+\frac{1}{\delta}}}\right)^{2t-1}\cdot\hat{\mathbb{E}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\hat{W}-W}}\right)(\bm{y}^{0}-\mathbb{E}\bm{y}^{0}),v}}\right\rangle^{2t},

where in the inequality we used
Lemma

A.6
.

For the lower bound:

\displaystyle\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{11.00691pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-11.00691pt\raisebox{-6.47223pt}{\makebox[11.00691pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{2t}$}}}\hskip-11.00691pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[11.00691pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{v}$}}}$}}}\hat{\mathbb{E}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle W(\bm{y}^{0}-\mathbb{E}\bm{y}^{0}),v}}\right\rangle+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\hat{W}-W}}\right)(\bm{y}^{0}-\mathbb{E}\bm{y}^{0}),v}}\right\rangle}}\right)^{2t}

\displaystyle\quad\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(1)}}{{\geqslant}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1}{1+\delta}}}\right)^{2t-1}\cdot\hat{\mathbb{E}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle W(\bm{y}^{0}-\mathbb{E}\bm{y}^{0}),v}}\right\rangle^{2t}-\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1+\frac{1}{\delta}}{1+\delta}}}\right)^{2t-1}\cdot\hat{\mathbb{E}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\hat{W}-W}}\right)(\bm{y}^{0}-\mathbb{E}\bm{y}^{0}),v}}\right\rangle^{2t}
